PREFACE 

OMMUNITIES have long faced various problems created by the commission of sex crimes against young girls. Both public and private agencies have been called upon by distressed parents for assistance-but usually only after an overt act has been committed. For well-recognized reasons, the press has refrained from publishing details of such cases; when they do, public revulsion rises to demand severe penalties for the offenders. Following the hue and cry, and once the perpetrators have been apprehended and punished, apathy seems to develop in respect to the fundamental causes involved. Should no more than sporadic thought be given to rape and carnal abuse of young girls? 

These crimes are well-nigh of daily occurrence in every large community, and of proportionate incidence in less crowded centers of population. The occasional instance that attracts attention and notice in the press, is but a boiling over of many social ills and evils that are constantly brewing, and that leave demoralization and sickness in their paths. 

Parents, teachers, community workers, judges of inferior courts, and other interested persons have maintained a definite reticence about these cases. 

They have exhibited a laudable desire to protect the names and reputations of the innocent victims. 

They have further shown a disinclination to discuss details of antisocial behavior which, looked upon solely as a crime, presumably did not warrant any further attention than that of the police and the 5

GIRLS ON CITY STREETS 

courts. However, it is herein that many have faltered. A public obligation has rested upon them to bring the light of day upon a situation which demands organized thinking and action, so that needless physical and moral sacrifices of many young girls might be averted. In this study of 1400 cases of rape and attempted rape, the one thought has been to focus attention on the need for saving young girls from the moral and physical harm that may be their lot until communities take vigorous measures to correct some glaring social shortcomings. All

The authenticity of the case material contained in this volume is vouched for by the authors. the cases refer to New York City and were culled from records covering the last few years. While it might have been desirable to include some data relating to incidence rates for New York City, a statistical basis could not be established, due to the nature of the material at hand. The Uniform 

Crime Reports for the United States indicate that the record for rape for the year 1933, per 100,000 population for the entire United States, was five and five-tenths, and the largest rate was for cities under 10,000.

Complete reports on medical and psychiatric examinations of the girls involved were not available and thus could not be made a part of this study. 

Likewise, detailed information of the parents and homes of the girls, other than that which is indicated, was usually lacking. The same applies to the males involved in these cases.

PREFACE 

All the names used are fictitious, and all possible means have been taken to hide the identities of the girls whose unfortunate affairs are portrayed. Likewise, clues as to the male perpetrators or participants in these sex affairs have similarly been eliminated. 

New York, September 1935.

J. A. G. R. W. G.

THE CHAPTER I  INTRODUCTION 

HE late Professor William James once remarked that an unlearned carpenter of his acquaintance, and in his hearing, had made a rather sage observation of human beings and their worth, when he stated that: "There is very little difference between one man and another; but what little difference there is, is very important". This appraisal is of particular interest when applied to parents. 

They are the first recipients of the love interest of their children, and tend for some years, at least, to serve as models for them. A child may come to resemble its parents in various ways, not only because of heredity, but similarly because of the conduct patterns which parents in long and close association provide for the growing child. Even slight variations in the conduct of parents may be of utmost importance to the future of a child. If they are intelligent and deem it desirable to make a lasting impression upon their children, it is naturally essential that such be of a wholesome and worthwhile nature. Thereby the children and society as a whole may become the ultimate gainers. On the other hand, a serious disservice may be done to impressionable youngsters through improper parental conduct by word or deed. Children imitate their elders. They are likewise hero worshippers, and are apt to demonstrate the results of their pow-

ers of observation, and, possibly, of imitation as well. Even seemingly slight deviations in the conduct of the adult, more especially of parents, older brothers and sisters, or others under whose guidance and supervision children are placed, may eventuate in undesirable impressions lasting throughout life.

Parents are often harassed as to the action to be taken with an apparently misbehaving and possibly delinquent child. What is perhaps of more importance to them, though not of the same urgency, is to determine the reasons for the misconduct or antisocial deportment. The answer is often beset with perplexities. For instance, how is one to account for a presumably normal and healthy girl deciding to absent herself from classes without a tangible explanation to parents or teachers; how can respectable parents account for the fact that their daughter is consorting with friends who are loud, boisterous, and whose general demeanor raises doubts as to their social and moral standards; why is it that a formerly happy and carefree adolescent has become morose and depressed; what is the significance of the sudden antipathy to a father or stepfather, brother or uncle; what explanations can be offered for transient or fairly fixed character and conduct transformations, when the mental health is not brought into question; how can parents determine primary causes of sex or other delinquencies in their young boys and girls? These are but a few of the problems that face parents, educators and community leaders in dealing with young people.

Perhaps a fundamental need in the control of adolescent delinquency is an intelligent attitude on the part of parents and community leaders, and a recognition of the fact that a delinquent girl may be an unadjusted girl, and one who has, for an unknown reason, been unable to find her proper place in the social group. Before any attempt can be made to deal effectively with such a girl, to correct her antisocial tendencies and to help her realize the possibilities for a normal life, the cause of the failure to adjust herself to her group and environment must first be discovered.

Girls who become involved in sex delinquencies, or are the victims of sex assaults by immediate members of their own families, friends, strangers, or others with whom they come in contact, are of major concern. The analysis of the histories of 

1400 such girls, from various types of homes, living in different neighborhoods of a large city, of diverse physical and mental ages, and subject to numerous influences within and without their homes, throws considerable light upon problems faced by growing girls in a metropolitan city. Likewise, many of the fundamentals recorded in these histories are applicable to girls of smaller communities.

The grouping of the sordid and sorrowful experiences of this fairly large number of young girls may tend to give a false sense of perspective, unless one keeps definitely in mind the thought that boys and young men, fathers and middle-aged men, were the fellow participants or perpetrators in the sex experiences through which these 1400 girls 10

passed. The term "delinquency" cannot be applied only, if at all, to the girls, but must similarly be attached to the host of antisocial, mentally and emotionally unstable males, who were responsible for the violation of these girls. A slighting of this phase of delinquency, involving rape, incest and seduction, would merely tend further to cloud an adequate appreciation and understanding of the causes. In the matter of prostitution, some communities now pay attention to the man in the case as well. In dealing with the problem of curtailing sex delinquency and violation, adequate thought must be given to the preventive aspects by emphasis on the boys and men involved. Until these matters are dealt with in a comprehensive way, mitigation of the evil will be slow, and many girls will needlessly continue to be sacrificed to the viciousness and uncurbed desires of the boys and men with whom they come in contact.

THE TASK FACING PARENTS 

The gamut of problems faced by the girls and their parents under discussion was so extensive, that the mere listing of them would perhaps tend to confuse rather than to clarify the issues. Taking a broad view of the matter, it might be said that there were two general groups into which the 1400 girls could be classified: namely, those who, through no apparent fault of their own, were enticed or forced into sex relations, generally with a man some years older than themselves; secondly, 11

girls who because of lack of understanding of sex matters, or who were under the influence of alcohol or drugs, were more or less willing partners in the sex activities in which they participated or which were foisted upon them.

Sex delinquency or experience of minors cannot be charged to any one cause. Primarily, there are at least two persons involved, even if no third person plays the part of procurer.

The mental and emotional status of the two participants in the sex act, their environment, education, etc., all contribute their share in the destruction of character. This complexity renders solution of the outstanding problems extremely difficult. The challenge to parents, as well as to official and non-official representatives of the community, cannot be avoided or minimized.

Whenever we consider the misconduct of children and young people, the natural tendency is, first of all, to find out something about their parents. 

This is, undoubtedly, the proper approach. Moreover, if society is to achieve any definite advance in the prevention of delinquency, young people should be served and properly contacted even before parenthood is upon them. Patently, such a status does not automatically bring with it knowledge and training in the proper methods of rearing children. It was long ago demonstrated, for instance, that parental ignorance of good methods of caring for and feeding babies was one of the causes of a high rate of infant mortality, while educational efforts expended to reduce such ignor- 12

ance resulted in the definite lowering of the infant That part of the battle has in large death rate. measure been won.

Many parents who pride themselves on following to the letter the prescribed rules for the physical health of their children, nevertheless overlook or are largely ignorant of the importance of mental health. Even among those who realize the fundamental need of the early control of behavior problems, there are many who are deeply concerned when their children lie or have temper tantrums, but attach little or no significance to such unhealthy signs as undue self-consciousness, seclusiveness, day-dreaming or jealousy. Still less do they realize that these manifestations may arise from their own attitudes toward the children, or from an imitation of conduct patterns set by the parents. 

Various instances of delinquency have been traced to the attempt of parents to make the child's life compensate for their own failure to reach certain goals of achievement; to the fact that the child has for years been buffeted between the rigid discipline of one parent and the laxity of the other; to the dominating attitude of one member of the family group which permits the child no opportunity for asserting himself as in individual; to the lack of companionship suitable to the age of the child; and to other conflicts between the needs of the child and the conditions prevailing in the family. Whatever the ultimate causes of delinquency may be, it must ever be kept in mind that parental attitudes 13

are of fundamental importance to the mental health of the growing child.

Children brought up in homes in which their parents can provide a fair standard of physical comforts and educational opportunities, are indeed fortunate. This is especially true if the parents likewise muster a knowledge and understanding of child life, without which the development of children is seriously hampered and harassed. If a careful appraisal were to be attempted of parents prepared and qualified to give their children the benefits not only of real sympathy, but adequate guidance and information as well, it would probably be found that but a small percentage in any community was so equipped. The relatively large number of parents with no earned incomes or with low incomes; with poor homes in slum and tenement areas; with insufficient space for the ordinary decencies of family life; with inadequate food for growing children; and often afflicted with acute and chronic illnesses-these and other concomitants of poverty were the lot of many of the girls whose experiences will be set forth. Their lives were harassed by existence just above the poverty line, if that term is sufficiently descriptive; many were on the brink of actual want. A major challenge faces society. Effective plans are urgently needed to meet the social, economic, medical, recreational and other problems of girls and boys who come from such homes, and whose parents were raised under similar conditions. 14

PARENTS AND ENVIRONMENT 

It is fair to presume that if all children were protected in their inalienable right to be well born into a good home with competent parents, adequate opportunity for intellectual and spiritual development, and intelligent direction in their studies and play, there would be no serious problem of juvenile delinquency. This ideal possibility for every child is, indeed, a goal to be aimed at. The number of children who are fortunate enough even to approximate these essentials is comparatively small, Physically, mentally and emotionally healthy parents are blessings of the first order. They are apt to follow deliberate and studied efforts of healthy young men and women to choose proper mates and to prepare themselves for adequate parenthood. How often such preparation precedes the thousands of marriages consummated annually in any large city, is, of course, open to question. But the ultimate results of poor marriages, those in which the parents do not score high in the attributes they should possess, are frequently recognized as being disastrous to the offspring. Leaving out of consideration for the moment the factor of heredity, it becomes readily evident that ill-equipped, mismated, ailing and economically insecure parents are a marked drag and handicap to their children. 

The destructive effects of poverty and borderline economic existence are too well known to need reiteration. When young children are exposed to the demoralizing influences of poor environment, 15

irreparable damage to character may be the result.

This is illustrated in the lives of many of the girls who were misled or compelled to participate in sex acts which left indelible marks upon their bodies and souls. To take some cases at random, there were the girls whose mothers ran off with men, deserting their own husbands and young children; the large number of husbands and wives who were divorced, separated or deserted, with detrimental effects upon their children; the mothers who, with the knowledge of their young daughters, lived with men to whom they were not married, and even had illegitimate children by them; the girls who were the offspring of common law arrangements between Negroes and white women; the mothers who consorted with many men and even brought them into their own homes; the fathers who were notoriously promiscuous; and a long list of other deplorable situations which certainly nullified the right of children to be decently brought up and sheltered. While these were among the grosser environmental situations to which girls were subjected in their own homes, there were many others, in combination with various destructive factors, which tended to break down the resistance and the finer sensibilities of growing girls. Their tragedies could often have been foretold, once the environmental circumstances were known.

Some might question the basis of this conclusion, presuming that innate tendencies play an important, if not a deciding part, in the conduct pattern of young people particularly. As will be adequately 16

indicated, there is no attempt to minimize the toll exacted by poor heredity-by physical, mental and emotional disabilities of parents. Nevertheless, such carefully culled information as is available at present, warrants the assumption that environmental factors stand out as important determinants of delinquency, and that they played a decisive part in the character formation of many of the girls under consideration. Further, the moral and social apathy of the boys and men who were the aggressors in numerous sex acts, could, in large measure, be assigned to the same factors. 

HEREDITY AS A BASIC FACTOR IN DELINQUENCY 

The influence of heredity upon conduct disorders has been studied for some time. However, marked differences of opinion as to the effect of heredity upon such delinquencies still persist. Dr. William 

A. White has pointed out that heredity and environment are frequently placed in opposition to each other and talked of as if they were mutually exclusive and independent, while this is not and cannot be so. The problem that is often involved is whether characteristics which individuals present and which are like those of the parents are or are not purely imitative phenomena. Are they more or less an identification of the individual with the parent possessing these qualities? Or, are they the outcome of other factors that may be unrecognized? The results of this identification are at times remarkably accurate copies of the parental 17

. pattern, making differentiation between simulation and true heredity an extremely difficult task. 

Application of the principle of heredity to sex and other delinquencies has been of particular moment in the matter of the feebleminded. Numerous studies have tended to show that the feebleminded, besides bulking large among those involved in misconduct of various types, have had a poor heredity. Several earlier reports along these lines have been quite thoroughly discredited because of looseness of methods in gathering the data, and in drawing conclusions which were not warranted from inaccurate and incomplete information. Of recent years, doubt has been thrown upon the assumption that most, if not all of the so-called feebleminded, are the progeny of feebleminded parents. 

Such an assumption takes no cognizance of those born mentally retarded because of intra-uterine disturbances of unknown etiology; of those who do not progress mentally as a result of glandular disorders present at birth or of subsequent development; and of those who failed to grow normally on the mental side due to birth injuries to the brain. 

Some qualified students have held that many of the so-called feebleminded did not have parents or grandparents who were themselves feebleminded, but were primarily retarded as a result of some of the causes cited. Whatever the ratio may be of mentally retarded children to normal parents, serious doubt has been raised as to the value of the earlier interpretations of the one cause of feeblemindedness. 18

stock.

No informed person would in any way attempt to belittle the undesirability of mating individuals of poor stock. The movement favoring and legalizing sterilization which has had a notable development in this country in a few years, and judicial decisions of the higher courts taking cognizance of the personal and social value of such a procedure, are indicative of the present trend to protect society. 

Whether or not the wisdom of sterilization in some form or another is accepted without reservation, little doubt now remains as to the value of such surgical interference in carefully studied and selected cases.

Feeblemindedness is only one factor in the usually accepted relationship of heredity and delinquency. Other problems arise out of emotional and mental instability. Dr. William Healy, who has been one of the outstanding observers in this field for some years, is of the opinion that there is no good evidence in his cases of conflict which arose upon any specific hereditary basis. If any predisposition is inherited, it is not, according to Healy, in general, such as may be identified and traced in any ordinary family history, or by any methods of discernment now available. Large numbers of delinquents presenting various conflicts have consistently shown normal ancestry as far as firsthand information and observation could determine, A comparable study of girls who likewise were involved in sex delinquencies, indicated that heredity played an important role in the conduct problems of the girls. The major factor of concern was 19

the alcoholism of the parents. If addiction to alcohol is, in some cases at least, a manifestation of mental and nervous instability, then heredity may well be said to have stood out prominently. In many of these cases the home atmosphere was far from healthy, with financial hardships, family quarrels and immorality of well-nigh regular occurrence. 

Supervision was lax, parents neglected their children, and some practically had to care for themselves, with virtually little parental control and advice. Incidentally, the school histories of these chilren were almost uniformly poor.

The effect of alcohol on the inheritance of certain character traits is of prime importance. In the majority of cases, alcoholism affects usually the father only, while in the reproduction of offspring, cells of two individuals of the opposite sex coalesce, with some descendants showing the specific defect and others escaping. Alcoholism will apparently transmit physic defects quite readily, and in families where both parents are alcoholic, the possibility of the inheritance of psychic defects, with consequent conduct disorders in early youth, is indeed marked.

Syphilis is another social poison of the first rank. 

The inheritance of syphilis, and its attendant physical, emotional and mental disturbances, has long been recognized. Little adequate data are available as to the incidence of congenital syphilis. One of the authors has recently completed a fairly extensive investigation into the incidence of prenatal 20

syphilis in New York City. It was found that in two large municipal hospitals 15 per cent and 19 per cent, respectively, of pregnant women who came for prenatal care, gave evidence of having syphilis. This is not the complete picture. Following this study came a careful analysis of records of the antisyphilitic treatment received by infected pregnant women. The frequent inadequacy of treatment accorded the prospective mother for the prevention of congenital syphilis in the as yet unborn child, was quite disturbing. These studies were made in a large city with a fairly wide distribution of hospitals and clinics. While not meeting complete needs, these institutions were available as stations for free or partially free prophylaxis. Conjecture as to probable medical conditions in communities scattered throughout the country leads one to assume that at least insofar as inherited syphilis and its consequences are concerned, much still remains to be done. An ap- praisal of the influence of congenital syphilis on sex and other delinquencies would yield interesting and valuable data. Some doubt may still be raised as to the line of demarcation between heredity and environment in certain types of conduct disorder. 

Evidence is available that such racial poisons as alcohol and syphilis do affect the unborn child. 

With the advent of early childhood or adolescence, children born of alcoholic or syphilitic parents may manifest a higher susceptibility to conduct disorders, coupled with mental, emotional and physical disturbances. 21

SOME ASPECTS OF SEX HYGIENE 

The mental health of the growing child obviously has an important bearing on his future status as a member of modern community life. It is generally recognized that the relationship existing between the fields of mental hygiene and social hygiene are not as yet completely clarified. While there might be a tendency to presume that the former was inclusive of the latter, yet a clear line of demarcation is apparently indicated. The presumption that many phases of social hygiene are closely related to various aspects of mental hygiene, is undoubtedly valid. Thinking primarily from the point of view of the young girls with whom this discussion is concerned, it might be well to state that the field of social hygiene has much to contribute aside from the mental phase. The large group of girls whose sex experiences were not the result of their own misconduct comes to mind at once. Seemingly voluntary sex involvements of many others cannot be divorced from the presumption that, had they been brought up in a wholesome environment and properly taught the fundamentals of sex life and conduct, many would have avoided the pitfalls which awaited them. As will be indicated in a considerable number of cases to be cited, emotional and mental factors undoubtedly played an important part in sex involvements of the girls under consideration.

Wherein lies the responsibility of parents in the matter of social hygiene, and more specifically, in 22

sex education? It would seem that the time many become aware that the bringing up of children is a task they assumed without adequate preparation, is when the first-born child begins to ask questions having a sex significance. Unfortunately, many parents look upon such childish, yet natural and well-intentioned questions, with a sense of horror and shame. They wonder how such thoughts entered into the minds of their pure and innocent children, and attempt to suppress them by scolding, threatening and expostulating that "a nice child never asks such questions." But to purge a mind of normal sex thoughts is beyond the ability of even solicitous, though poorly informed parents.

Their reactions are primarily due to prudery, misinformation, or inadequate knowledge of child psychology, and the mental needs of growing children.

A gentle reminder to those who are sensitive to a quizzing on sex matters in the guise of queries actually directed to parents, may suggest some of the things that troubled them as children. For instance, typical questions asked by young boys and girls include the following: Where do babies come from? Do storks really bring the babies? Where does the hospital get the babies? How are babies made? Did we come from an egg? Mother, do you have to sit on the egg? Why do we buy so many babies? 

Is it true that women get big before they have babies? Why did you have a tiny baby? 

Do babies really grow inside the mother? Do you suppose I have a baby in me? How do you know 23

when the baby is going to be born? 

How does the baby get out of the mother? Why do you have milk? Why don't I have milk like you? Do girls bleed when they get older? What does "pregnant mother" mean? Why can't fathers give milk to the babies?

These queries might perhaps be called preliminary in nature, marking the sex awakening of the children who asked them. Many develop an early awareness of sex factors. Possibly because of what they imbibe from other children or following initial guidance in sex matters by parents and teachers, they soon want to know more. For instance, preadolescent youngsters have asked: What do you do to get a baby? If the baby grows in the mother, why does it look like the father? Who plants the seeds for the baby? Just how does the father plant the seeds in the mother? How does the father part get into the mother? These are questions normally developing children have addressed to parents who had their confidence. What about the many children who cannot turn to father or mother for an answer to troubled thoughts?

Whose responsibility is it to provide correct information in intelligible terms, robed in expressions that satisfy the curiosity of the growing child? Answers to childish doubts and the questions of youth are made extremely difficult when educational systems shut out all sex hygiene instruction. matters of serious import. 

These are

The young girls whose unfortunate experiences are revealed in following chapters, were urgently in 24

need of elemental instruction which might have served to protect them. When approached with the suggestion that they participate in sex relations, or bribed with favors and offers of small sums, they frequently asked what was it all about, what was expected of them, what did the sex terms mean. 

Primary facts of biology and sex hygiene were a closed book to many of the girls. This was often due to the failure of their own parents to foresee the possibility of such experiences as befell their young daughters. They had been given little, if any, informative advice. It is quite true that mothers and fathers sometimes did the best they knew how, but this was not enough. A warning not to associate with boys or men, coupled with the threat of a beating in case of disobedience of this parental prohibition, is a poor substitute for proper instruction. These girls were, in the main, brought up in a city where school officials have not looked with favor upon attempts at integrated or other forms of sex teaching. A most desirable avenue of instruction was thereby denied to many adolescent girls. 

It has been aptly stated by Miriam Van Waters that the individual parent can no longer wholly protect his child from what is called "bad company." As long as these sources of contact exist in a community, they will enter into the lives of youngsters who somehow will partake of them, despite the inhibiting efforts of parents. Whether or not these unwholesome factors bring in their wake unsocial conduct, depends upon the degree 25

of immunity which has been set up in a given individual. This immunity comes not from physical innoculation, but from mental activity or education. When the child responds to vulgarity or obscenity, it may be because of an unsatisfied curiosity, or some feeling for adventure and knowledge. 

The uninitiated and uninformed youngster swallows with avidity the tall tales and vocabulary of his elder playmates on matters relating to sex. This warrants the assumption that the properly educated child is immune to a great deal of such street talk. 

Misinformation relating to sex can offer no allurement for the child whose desire for knowledge has been satisfied by well-informed parents and teachers. It can only hold out a tense moment with subsequently disturbed thoughts for those children whose curiosity has never been met, and who are liable to over-stimulation by any discussion or reference to sex topics. Often because of unsatisfactory adjustment in their own lives, parents find it difficult to assist their children to gain a normal concept of the subject of sex. To protect themselves from discomfort, they discourage the discussion of sex problems and meet the children's questions with evasions. Parents thereby fail to realize that if they do not provide the desired information, their children are bound to get it, even though it comes from a polluted source. This attitude of prudery, when passed on to the children, makes adjustment in later life much more difficult. 26

GIRLS AND THE COMMUNITY 

It is problematical whether by any available means, growing girls, and boys as well, can be provided with those essentials for normal life which are the birthright of every child. It would be possible to visualize a reorganized form of society in which every young adult would be given adequate educational preparation prior to marriage and parenthood; only those physically and mentally fit to be parents would be entrusted with that responsibility or participation in the functions of parenthood; all families would have the minimum income necessary to provide a decent home in a satisfactory neighborhood, together with all requisites for healthful living and recreation; and every child and adolescent would be given an education suited to his or her own capacities and predilections, with proper preparation for life as an outstanding function of the educational system. 

Major social and economic changes would be necessary to bring about such a millenium. However, community dislocations and social apathy are at the root of many of our social problems. They must be faced with courage and understanding if progress is to be made in the solution of questions involved in such matters as rape and sexual assault on young girls.

The purpose of these remarks is not to appear pessimistic, nor yet to alarm parents, teachers and others who have some part in the raising and education of children. Rather, it is to bring to the fore the thought that the elimination of sex delin- 27

quencies, as well as of sexual assault, requires not only that parents take seriously the necessity of their own preparation for worthwhile parenthood, and that teachers and community workers in contact with children likewise educate themselves for fuller service, but that the cooperation of various elements in the community and body politic is required. Granted that advance has already been made towards the provision of some of the community requisites previously enumerated, there yet remains the necessity of an aroused citizenship, public officials of energy and high motivation, and wise counsel as to how to deal with sex and delinquency problems affecting the health and morals of untold numbers of young people.

The stories of girls whose sex involvements and delinquencies were inquired into with as much care as available records made possible, are presented in abbreviated form. The cited cases are to be taken for what they are intended-typical instances of what girls of many races and varying types of homes suffered and experienced. Major causes included broken and overcrowded homes; cruel, abusive and drunken parents; poor heredity and environment; the viciousness of boys and men; and a number of other subjective and objective phenomena. The apparent simplicity of correcting certain of the conditions and abuses which gave rise and impetus to many catastrophies of individual girls, should give heart and conviction to parents and others as to the possibility of eliminating some of the outstanding personal and community failings. 28

YOUN CHAPTER II  INTRODUCTION 

OUNG girls and boys who succumb to their "wanderlust" urge create innumerable problems for their parents and other legal guardians, as well as for society in general. Sometimes they lay the basis of serious situations for themselves, and suffer irreparable damage to health and character. The beginning of delinquency is often associated with a first escapade, which may take on no more serious form than playing truant, or running away from home for one or more days.

Actually, this seeming impulse of girls to get away from some real or fancied unpleasant situation, may be in response to an urge for amusement, adventure, pretty clothes, favorable notice and a greater sense of freedom for which many adolescents crave. Such a caprice on the part of a girl is not to be taken as proof that home conditions possibly warranted the action, or that cruel and abusive parents made the step inevitable. The following case histories may throw some light on this aspect of the problem.

Some psychological explanations have been attempted for this "runaway" phenomenon among young people. Plausible reasoning might lay the cause, not so much in the home, nor yet at the feet of the parents or other guardians, but rather in the personality of the runaway. 

It may arise, perhaps, 29

in response to a sense of inferiority and inability to meet certain situations which are fairly simple for most young people. Among those of school age, the child who is a truant or runaway may find school work onerous, or he may be bright and suffer boredom while being kept down to the level of those who learn less readily. Furthermore, a child may be of a hypersensitive and nervous make-up, or he may be manifesting the early symptoms of mental disease. He seeks a way out, and though unprepared for the consequences, leaves his accustomed environment in home and in school. The probability is that some personality factor is involved among those who seek a quick and easy escape from a presumed unpleasant situation.

In an analysis of the reasons given by girls for running away, the factor of ill-treatment at home was emphasized by them. Usually, some inconsequential incident was the immediate cause. Unsatisfactory home conditions, if such there were, may have been endured for some time prior to departure from the family circle. With very few exceptions, girls who run away find themselves in poorer and less desirable situations than before their escapade, if such it may be called. The major objective of these ofttimes inadequate personalities in leaving home was to attain an atmosphere devoid of supervision, where their desires and instincts could have full sway without the interference of parental or other control.

The question of environmental and economic factors as influencing runaways cannot be slighted, 30

nor yet need these matters be overemphasized. In individual cases, a cruel and abusive father or stepfather, an immoral or mentally unbalanced mother, an unsympathetic stepmother or other substitute for a mother, family overcrowding, and other results of poor environment and low income, may impel some girls to leave home. But, by and large, there is more than a semblance of personality difficulty to account for an impulsive or even a considered plan of running away.

In the present series of 1400 girls who became involved in sex situations, 43.7 per cent were runaways, for longer or shorter periods of time. Exclusion of those under 12 years of age raises this figure to almost 56 per cent. 

The reasons they gave for leaving home were frequently naive, a common excuse being that they were visiting a girl friend or had gone to the movies and were afraid to go home because it was 10 P. M. or 11 P. M., or even later. This was obviously an excuse concocted to account for an act for which the girls themselves could offer no better explanation. The urge to run away, and to be free from supervision was in the mental foreground, and it would probably have been acted upon at some time, even if the visit had terminated sooner or the second show in the movies had come to a close an hour earlier.

SOME RUNAWAY GIRLS 

Were it possible to decide upon a common denominator as the factor involved in stimulating or encouraging girls to run away from home, it might 31

be comparatively simple to concentrate on a solution. In such a procedure remedies would be planned and applied with some surety of eventual suc- 

However, as in most other conduct problems, primary causes are not readily discernible. cess. There may be the thought that given situations are predisposing or contributory causes; or there may even appear to be valid reasons for assuming that in a certain case, both preceding behavior and current conduct have been at work. Caution born of experience in dealing with these adolescent problem cases warrants the belief that while the runaway girls, or for that matter boys as well, may readily assign some excuse for leaving home, much careful study is required to complete an inquiry into motivating causes of runaway propensities. 

Certain aspects of the problem will be readily apparent from a reading of the following stories of girls who ran away. Some speculation might be indulged in as to why girls of a certain age apparently have a pronounced urge to run away; the influence of inadequate parents; unrest caused by ill-suited school curricula; the distress caused by early manifestations of mental and emotional disturbance; the part that unregistered and unsupervised furnished rooming houses and cheap hotels play in this picture; the responsibility of taxicab drivers in aiding and abetting runaway girls, and in helping them enter a life of prostitution; etc. These matters present a certain grim reality in the sordid. tales, and conjure up many more possible factors in stimulating or inducing girls to run away, only to 32

become involved in devastating mental and physical experiences. 

TESSIE, aged 13, and in the first term of junior high school, lived with her mother, father and two sisters 17 and 11 years of age. Her father was a tailor, and her mother worked in a tea room. While visiting an aunt in New Jersey during the summer, she met two young men at an amusement park and accepted an invitation to go to their bungalow, where both assaulted her. Later Tessie left home for one night and remained out with a 19 year old taxi driver whom she met but once before through a street flirtation. On this occasion she sat in the taxicab with him for several hours, but nothing serious occurred. About 3 A. M. the friend borrowed another cab, and after driving a few blocks to a deserted neighborhood, he went into the rear of the car where Tessie permitted him to have relations with her. She did not return home until the following afternoon, when a severe scolding from her mother was her lot for running away. 

Thereafter she was not permitted to be out of the house in the evenings for a number of weeks. 

A month later she went to visit her father at his place of employment and was accompanied by her younger sister and a girl friend. On the way home

Tessie met two young men about 18 years of age, whereupon she sent her younger sister home with her girl friend, and accompanied the men for a walk. One of these she had met the previous summer when in New Jersey, and the other lived in her neighborhood. The three took a taxicab to 33

a dance hall on Long Island and did not return to the city until 3 A. M. They went to a small hotel in the midtown area, where the clerk showed them to a room into which one of the men took Tessie, while the other remained in the hallway. She permitted him to have relations with her, and after he went out his friend entered and was likewise allowed the same privilege. They left the hotel at 5 A. M. Later in the day Tessie went home.

ANGELINA was 15 years old, and lived at home with her father, mother, an 18 year old sister and a 17 year old brother. The father owned and operated a restaurant with the assistance of the two older children, and provided a comfortable home for his family. The parents were kind to 

Angelina, though they had to insist that she should keep regular hours. The first time Angelina left home she rented a furnished room on Long Island. 

The reasons she gave for running away were that she wanted to be free from the strictness of her father and to have a good time. Angelina thought that by living alone she could have more fun, and would find a man to support her and supply the excitement she craved. She spent two fruitless days standing on stoops and walking the streets, but no man paid any attention to her. When her limited funds became exhausted she returned to the city, where an accidental meeting with her father took place and she went home.

Angelina remained home about two months and then ran away again to Long Island. There a flirtation with a young fellow resulted in her being 34

taken to a boarding house and introduced as the wife of her escort. She lived there with her recently acquired friend for three weeks, during which time he had relations with her. They then went to New York where they met another couple, and the four rented one furnished room, the men telling the landlady that they were married. The couples occupied two beds in the same room, and on several occasions changed sex partners. 

In a few days the other girl deserted her boy friend and Angelina was asked to find another to take her place. She sought out her chum Anna with whom she had gone to school. Anna's mother permitted her daughter to spend the night with Angelina, as she believed that the latter was living with her parents. Anna readily fell in with the new mode of life, and filled the place which had been vacated by the other girl. The group of four again occupied two beds in one furnished room and both girls had relations with the men. This continued for several weeks, the rent being paid by the men, who came to visit them every night. One evening Angelina and Anna flirted with two men on the street and were taken for a long automobile ride. When they refused to permit intercourse, they were put out of the car and had to walk back to their temporary quarters in the furnished room. 

Arriving there about 3 A.M. they were picked up by the police as wayward minors. 

MARY, 15 years of age, was born in one of the mill towns in Massachusetts. She attended school till she was 14 years old and reached the seventh 35

grade. During this time she lived at home with her father who worked as a laborer, her mother, three sisters and two brothers. After leaving school, she held two jobs as a houseworker. 

A friend of Mary's had gone to New York and had written her about the big city. In one letter she invited Mary to join her and enclosed the necessary money for expenses. The 15 year old friend 

Helen met her and took Mary to a furnished room in a poor section of the city. Mary found it difficult to adjust herself to New York at first. After a while she began to frequent a cheap dance hall in the theatrical district and made the acquaintance of several girls of questionable character. Through one of them Mary was introduced to Harry, aged 18, who worked in a retail store in her neighborhood. She saw Harry on the street almost every day thereafter, and he seemed sufficiently interested to inquire about her welfare and whether she had her meals regularly, as he knew she was without funds. He gave her one or two dollars whenever they met, and soon began to propose that she should permit sex intercourse. While refusing at first, Mary finally consented, feeling she was under obligation to him for the money and attention he had been paying her. Harry took her to an apartment and, after the consummation of the act, he gave her $4.00. For some days thereafter he continued to give her spending money. When the rent money was due Mary had no funds, as Helen had departed, and she went to see Harry and told him about her needs. He suggested that he would get 36

some fellows to have intercourse with her for which she would be paid. Mary consented to this plan and in a little while Harry sent four men to her room. 

She had relations with two of them who paid her $2.00 each, and was prepared to entertain the others when a policeman broke in, sent the men away and arrested Mary.

It is interesting to note that so long as Helen was with Mary she refrained from compromising contacts with men. The sex experiences in New 

York were not the first in Mary's life. She went to many dances in her home town and a year previous while at a dance, met a young fellow for the first time and accepted his invitation to go riding in his automobile. When she asked to be taken home, he refused unless she had relations with him. 

Mary claimed that in this instance the man beat her and then raped her.

HANNAH, living in a comfortable home with her father, mother and 17 year old sister, had reached the fourth term in high school when she was 15 years old. Together with a girl friend of her own age she began to frequent Greenwich Village restaurants and dance resorts. It was during visits to these places that she became acquainted with several young men who made the usual sex proposals, but she seemingly managed by design or accident to remain free of any involvements. 

On one of her trips to a Village restaurant with her chum, the girls flirted with two young fellows and remained with them till 3 A. M. Hannah became alarmed as to what excuse she could give her mother 37

for staying out so late.

One of the men suggested that the girls spend the night with them in their furnished room and the girls acquiesced. Hannah slept in the bed with one man and her girl friend slept on the floor with the other. Hannah's companion wanted her to have intercourse but she refused, claiming she was not that kind of a girl. The next morning she telephoned to her mother and concocted the story that she had spent the night at a girl friend's house. The following day, after having gone home first, she and her chum again went out, picked up some boys, went to a dance, and did not get home till 3 A. M., when Hannah was severely scolded by her parents.

A few weeks later, while on the way home from high school, she met another girl friend who introduced her to two young men, and accepted an invitation to go riding in their automobile. The men soon started to take liberties with them, and the girls jumped out of the car, although they were told by the men that they were only fooling and meant no harm. Some days thereafter Hannah and her pal went out walking after 11 P. M. and meeting the same men, again accepted their invitation to go riding. Three other young men were also picked up and all drove to a deserted part of Long Island. 

During the ride the men drank much gin and became somewhat intoxicated. On reaching a deserted area the girls were told they would have to permit intercourse this time, and if they refused to cooperate the five men would force them. Both 

Hannah and her friend were thrown down in a 38

field, after which Hannah was assaulted and her friend was also raped. Fearing to go home, they remained all night in the car with the men, and in the early morning they were driven to New York, They walked the streets all day and spent the night in the subway. Hannah sought out a girl friend who permitted her to sleep at her home the following night. Her companion in sad adventure decided to face the wrath of her parents. Hannah spent the next two days at the home of the girl friend, after which the older sister found Hannah and took her home.

A few days later she again ran away and visited another friend who welcomed Hannah into her home. Flirtations with young men followed and she invited them to the room where she had spent the night. Her sister, who had been searching for 

Hannah, found her in this new place and summoned a policeman who took the erring girl in custody.

ETHEL lived at home with her parents, and at 15 years of age was in the 7-B grade. A friend of the family, John by name, aged 27 and a married man, was in the habit of paying the father social calls. On a particular evening John and the father had a number of drinks; then the parents retired for the night and left Ethel sitting in the kitchen with John. She prepared and served tea, after which he kissed and hugged her and then requested that they have intercourse. Ethel demurred at first, but finally consented. Several days later he came to the house while the mother was out and suggested to Ethel that they again have relations, 39

to which she readily agreed. When she was questioned about her conduct the excuse she gave was that John treated her so nicely that she couldn't help but like him.

About this time Ethel ran away from home and spent the night with a married sister, but the brother-in-law feared difficulties with his emotional father-in-law and insisted that she should leave.

A few days later she ran away again and came back to her sister's home, where she met John. Ethel told him she had left home, and he advised her to return to her parents, but she refused and walked the streets with John for several hours. 

She entreated him to permit her to spend the night at his home; he consented and let her sleep in his wife's room, as she was in the country visiting relatives. 

The following day John told Ethel he was afraid to have her in his apartment and it would be best if she went home. To this she agreed, but when she came near her home fear of her parents overcame her and she decided to return to John's flat late that night. After ringing the bell and receiving no answer, she spent the night in the hallway, where John found her in the morning. He induced the young girl to return to her sister's house, where she remained for a few days. The excuse she gave for her conduct was that her parents drank excessively and were very quarrelsome. 

BELLE, a 15 year old Negress, originally resided in Philadelphia, and later moved to Atlantic City, where she lived with her mother who often beat her. Thinking she would be better off away from 40

her parent, she accepted the invitation of David, aged 24, of whose antecedents she knew nothing, to accompany him to New York on the promise of marriage. When she reached New York, David took Belle to the home of his aunt, to whom he introduced the girl as his wife. They were given a room and that night David and Belle had relations, this being the first sex experience for the runaway. 

The following day they went to a friend's home where Belle saw Charles, who told her that she met with his approval. He also informed her that David was married and had a young son, though he had not lived with his wife for some time. 

Belle quickly transferred her affections to Charles, and lived with him for a month. He offered to marry her and take her to Boston where his family lived, but she refused because of uncertainty as to whether she loved him sufficiently. Their affair was abruptly ended when Belle's mother located the runaway.

LINA, a 13 year old Negress, and only in the 4th grade, was born in South Carolina and had lived in New York for three years. Her father was a laborer and her mother worked in a factory. As she had no brothers or sisters or other relatives at home, Lina would be left to her own resources after school hours and until her father or mother returned from work. Her first sex experience occurred when she was 12 years old, and the boy involved was three years her senior. A friend took her on a visit to the home of this boy and his sister, 41

and while there Lina went into a bedroom with the boy and had relations with him. 

The first time Lina ran away from home she was accompanied by her 14 year old friend Mary. They went to Philadelphia where they obtained jobs doing housework, until Mary's mistress learned that the girls were runaways and telegraphed to their mothers. While away from home they were not involved in any sex experiences. 

Six months later Lina and Mary together with another girl friend, ran away to Newark and rented a furnished room for which they paid with money they had taken from their mothers. Lina wanted to have a good time, and the first night went to a dance where she met Harry, aged 15, to whom she was attracted. After escorting her to the furnished room, she invited him to visit her. 

The next day, when he called on Lina, she readily agreed to have intercourse with him. The girls were located by their mothers at the end of two weeks and were taken home.

Several months later Lina was visiting Mary and was presumably to spend the night at her home. 

The girls decided to go to a local dance hall after Mary's parents and two brothers had retired for the night. They arrived there at 1.30 A. M. and danced with a number of boys till 3 A. M., when two of the boys asked Lina and Mary where they were to spend the balance of the night. Both said at first that they were going home, but fearing to do so at that hour, accepted an invitation to visit the new friends at their apartment. On arriving there, 42

they prepared to go to bed, Lina with one fellow and Mary with the other in separate rooms. Lina voluntarily permitted her companion to have relations with her, and in the morning learned that Mary had done likewise. They remained away from home the following day and that night went to another dance hall and did not leave till 4 A. M. 

Returning to Mary's house they told her mother that they had been to dances and nothing further was said by the mother, despite the absence of two days. The girls went to school, and when Lina's mother came home from the factory that day she sought out her daughter and gave her a whipping. 

ADA was 15 years old and in the third term of high school. She lived with her mother, who was very strict and did not permit her to go out alone at night. The first time Ada ran away she was gone for three days. She left home presumably to attend a party, but not having a real party dress, she walked about her neighborhood until 1 A. M., and then went to the roof of the house she lived in and spent the night there. The next morning Ada did not go to her apartment but went window shopping until three men in an automobile invited her to go riding. She consented and they drove off towards Connecticut. On reaching a deserted section, the men stopped the car and each in turn assaulted her, despite her efforts to prevent them. 

They brought her back to the city at 3 A. M. and she spent the balance of the night riding in the subway. In the morning she went to the home of an aunt and spent the night with her. The following 43

day she returned to high school, where she was located by her mother.

Two months later Ada again left home, presumably because her mother was too severe and oldfashioned, as she made her wear long dresses and did not allow her to use face powder, nor to wear silk stockings or high heeled shoes. The first week she spent the nights in the ladies' rooms of subway stations. She then met a young fellow to whom 

Ada confided that she had run away from home.

He suggested the renting of a furnished apartment for her, and as Ada agreed, he proceeded to do so. 

After being installed, the man went away and said he would see her the next night. When he came he compelled her to have relations with him, and the same thing happened the following night. On his second visit he brought a friend and asked her also to permit him to have relations with her but she refused because he looked too repulsive. men then left and she saw no more of them. Both

At the end of a week, having no money for rent, Ada left the rooms in which she had been living. 

She flirted with a young man, and after some coaxing, agreed to move to his furnished room. She spent three nights with him during which time they had sex relations. Ada also picked up other men on the street and permitted them to take her to various hallways and have intercourse with her, for which favors she accepted small sums of money. 

One man took her to his room and paid her $5.00. 

During this period of three days she had relations with about 25 men. One of her transient acquaint- 44

ances asked for no sex favors, but wanted Ada to carry a revolver for him which he intended to use in a hold-up.

A friend of the family saw Ada on the street and, knowing she had been reported as missing, had her taken in custody. 

JULIA, 14 years of age, and in the seventh grade, lived in a small New Jersey town with her father, mother, and a 16 year old brother. Her 15 year old sister, Elizabeth, had gone to New York to work.

The first time anything happened to Julia was at the age of 13 years. She met a young fellow at a picnic and when, while riding with him, he asked her to let him have relations with her, she agreed without knowing much of what it was all about. 

The following summer she ran off to New York to be with her sister and to try to obtain work. The girls rented a furnished room and lived together. 

When they had no money left, as the sister had lost her job, they were put out on the street. Elizabeth finally obtained a position and went off without any concern for her younger sister. Julia walked the streets and finally appealed to the police to send her back home. While in New York she had nothing to do with any men. 

GLADYS was 15 years of age, in the eighth grade of school, and lived with her mother and four sisters 24, 18, 12 and seven years of age. She stayed away from home the first time on the invitation of a taxi driver and went riding with him till a late hour. Fearing to go home as she could not face questioning by her mother regarding her where- 45

abouts for so many hours, Gladys explained her predicament to the taxi driver, and he took her to a furnished room. He gave her some money and returned the next day and drove her to a ferry, as Gladys wanted to go to New Jersey to visit her On arriving at the aunt's home, her mother and sisters were informed. This escapade preyed on her mind, and she came to believe that all her acquaintances were talking about her misconduct. aunt.

She finally decided to run away again and left home at 3 A. M., with the intention of finding her friend, the taxicab driver. Not being able to locate him, she agreed to the suggestion of another taxicab driver to go to his furnished room and sleep there. He explained that he worked at night and 

Gladys could have the use of the room during that time. Upon awakening the next morning, she found him in bed with her. He compelled her to have intercourse with him, and threatened her with death if she told anyone about it. Gladys slept alone in the room the following two nights. After that she returned home and promised her mother never to run away again. 

FANNIE, aged 15, lived with her widowed mother and two older brothers about 50 miles from 

New York City. She

During her early adolescence she had several sex affairs with boys and men in her town. was not strong physically. The school nurse thought she might have tuberculosis and took her to a clinic for examination. The girls in her class suspected that Fannie was sick and shunned her, 46.

with the result that she used this as an excuse for running away. She started to walk to New York, and while on the road she met a woman and her five year old son, who invited Fannie to her home in New Jersey. After accepting this invitation, she remained but a few days, as she struck the child and the mother compelled her to leave. Fannie then came to New York and, meeting a man on the street, went with him to a furnished room, stayed with him four nights, and let him have intercourse with her each night.

She soon grew to dislike this man and left him when she obtained a job as a waitress in a Chinese restaurant. There she met a young Negro with whom she lived for two nights, although she would not let him have relations with her. Quitting her job, she picked up an acquaintance with a laborer and lived with him for four nights. Each time he had relations with her she asked for $2.00, which sum she usually obtained from other men friends. She deserted this last lover, if such he could be called, and picked up another with whom she lived for a week. This man suspected Fannie had a venereal disease and would have nothing to do with her. A few days later she fainted on the street, was taken to a hospital, and after some treatment was discharged with the diagnosis of an "abscess in the womb." Upon leaving the hospital and being without shelter and with no funds, she went to the home of a fellow-patient whose acquaintance she had made in the hospital ward. 

Fannie remained only two nights because the hus- 47

She then band of her fellow-patient made improper advances to her and she was obliged to leave. flirted with a young fellow who took her to his furnished room where she remained four days. He wanted to have relations with her but she would not let him and offered the explanation that she was sick. On being found alone on the street late one night, she was taken into custody as a possible runaway. 

LILLIAN, aged 15, and in the seventh grade, lived in Massachusetts with her father, stepmother, her 20 year old sister Mary, and two older brothers. The first time she left home was in the company of her sister, but both girls were picked up by policemen in Westchester at 2 A. M. and their father was notified to take them home. This experience did not deter them from leaving again a few weeks later. This time they arrived in New 

York, and walking along Riverside Drive, accepted an invitation of two men to go riding. Lillian and her sister told their newly found friends they were runaways and in need of work. The men promised to get them jobs in a dance hall, and invited the sisters to spend the night with them. They all slept in one bed, and while nothing happened to 

Lillian, she did not know what her sister's experiences were. The next morning they went to the dance hall, but the manager refused to employ Lillian because of her apparent youth. The older girl was taken on, and after the first night of taxidancing, she returned at 3 A. M. to the room they occupied with the men. Meanwhile Mary had 48

become acquainted with two other men who invited both girls to care for their apartment, in exchange for which they would be given shelter and food. 

On the following night they met the men and were taken to a five room apartment, where they were joined by five other men a short while after their arrival. One of the men took Lillian to a bedroom and raped her. He was followed by three others, two of whom she succeeded in fighting off in their attempt to assault her. In a little while the men subjected Mary to similar treatment, after which she left without her younger sister's knowledge. When the men learned that Lillian was only 

15 years of age, they acted as though they were frightened over possible consequences. 

That night Lillian was very sick and the men could not arrange to have her leave the apartment. 

She slept in bed with one of the men, but he did not touch her and kept the others from assaulting her. 

The following day Lillian was taken away in a car, given some money and told to go home. This suggestion did not fit in with her plans, as she feared her stepmother and would not think of going back to Massachusetts. When she was put out of the car and was standing alone at a street corner, some young fellows came along and inveigled her to accompany them to a nearby apartment on the assumption that she would find her sister there. On finding that this was not so, the men told her the whole thing was a joke and asked her to have relations with them. After explaining that she was sick, they gave her food and medicine and permitted 49

her to remain in their apartment for a week. 

At the end of that time two other men came and forcibly had relations with her, after which the occupants of the apartment asked that she allow them the same privilege. The next day Lillian was provided with food and taken to the movies. 

One of the men introduced her to Jimmy, his supposed brother. That night she was brought to a room and told that she would be expected to permit intercourse with several men who arrived in a short while. However, after conferring, they left without doing anything to her. She spent the night alone in the room and in the morning Jimmy told her she could stay as long as she wanted, as his friends would not do anything to her because they had their own girls. She lived with him for six weeks as she had become somewhat fond of her protector.

During this time he did not have relations with her and prevented his friends from molesting her. 

He explained that his intentions were serious and he expected to marry her. Jimmy did not work and his plans went askew when the police arrested him for burglary and also took Lillian into custody. 

ANNA, 15 years old, lived in New Jersey with her parents and three younger brothers. She left school in the 7-B grade and worked in a factory where she earned $13.00 a week. Anna had two girl friends who lived in a nearby town, and when they asked her to run away with them, she readily agreed. The reason she gave later for leaving home was that her strict father would not permit her out alone at night. The three girls had an 50

uneventful trip to Trenton, even though they hitchhiked the entire way.

They rented a furnished room and in a few days Anna obtained a job as a waitress in a lunch room. 

On account of constant friction with the other girls, she took a room for herself. One night at a theatre she met Frank, aged 21, with whom she became very friendly in a short while. He introduced her to his family and it was understood that they were to be married within a few months. After a friendship of only three weeks, they rented a furnished room where Anna permitted the persistent Frank to have relations with her. He suggested that they should get married and go to New York where 

Frank's father lived. Upon arriving there the marriage did not take place, but Frank went to his father's home and Anna to a furnished room in a poor neighborhood.

Frank was not the only one to have relations with her. While he was away for a night, Anna met a mutual friend and went to the theatre with him. After the show they took a walk in Central 

Park and, when he suggested they have intercourse, she readily agreed and accepted $4.00 for granting this favor. Some days later detectives came to the furnished rooming house and took Anna and two other girls found there into custody as wayward minors. 

HELEN, aged 15, was born in Boston and lived with her mother, stepfather and an older sister. 

She became a social problem quite early in life and had been committed to an institution as a delin- 51

quent when only 12 years old. Two 17 year old girl friends suggested to Helen that they should run away from home and go to New York. They hitchhiked as far as Providence the first day, and the man who gave them a lift took the girls to his friend's house. He agreed to take them to New 

York on condition that Helen would have relations with him. As she acquiesced, he kept his bargain and the girls arrived in New York about midnight. 

They then asked a cab driver where they might find a place to sleep, and he advised them to go to a midtown skating rink where they might be able to pick up sailors with whom to spend the night. 

When they reached the rink and saw the large number of young fellows and girls coming out drunk, they were afraid to go in. The cab driver then took them to a police station, and Helen was sent to a hospital for examination and treatment; three weeks later she was sent home to her mother. 

She did not remain there more than two weeks but picked up another girl friend and again hitchhiked to New York. Nothing eventful happened to the girls on the way and one autoist gave them $7.00, asking no sex favors in return. They rented a furnished room from which they were soon ejected because they entertained men. For some weeks the girls specialized in flirting with sailors and having relations with them. In order to be nearer to the men in uniform, they moved to the Navy Yard section of Brooklyn and continued their operations there, with the assistance of two men who posed as their husbands. Policewomen finally 52

became suspicious of the girls and arrested them as wayward minors.

DOROTHY had reached the 8-A grade at 14 years and lived with her father, stepmother, two younger brothers and a baby sister. Her girl friend 

Helen, aged 16, seemingly had a bad influence upon her, and was the immediate cause of her being raped. Even before she ran away Dorothy had sex relations a number of times with a man who posed as Helen's uncle. He usually offered Dorothy money for her sex favors, but she refused to accept any, while Helen always took all he gave her when she permitted him to have intercourse. 

Dorothy and Helen left home together and were away for one week. The night the girls ran away they had a date for an auto ride with two young men who drove to Long Island and had intercourse with both of them. When asked why they did not want to go home, they said it was too late and that they had run away from home, whereupon the men gave them money to rent a furnished room. Since this scheme worked so well, Helen told the same story to other men with whom the girls had relations, and was likewise able to obtain money from them. The last night they were away they had a date to go riding with two men, but instead of being taken for a long ride into the country, they returned Dorothy and Helen to their own homes and told them that was where they belonged. 

Dorothy's father and stepmother were always good to her and it was not because of them that she ran away. It may have been, she said, because 53

she read so many "crazy" sex books and magazines that she wanted to get away from home and have a good time. 

WILMA, 15 years of age, lived with her father, stepmother and a younger brother. She only reached the 7-A grade in public school, and ran away from home because she did not care to attend school and wanted to work. Her father, however, insisted that she should continue at school. Upon leaving her home she stood around a subway station and started a flirtation with Harold B., who was 

29 years of age. He invited Wilma to his threeroom apartment which he shared with his friend, George. She accepted his invitation and remained there the week she was away from home, keeping house for the two taxicab drivers. She first arrived

An hour later Harold arrived and he too, had intercourse with her. JOSEPHINE lived in a Pennsylvania mining town with her parents, and had left school at the age of 15, while in the eighth grade. She came to New York where two older brothers and a married sister resided, and went to live with her brother Stephen. She soon obtained a job as a houseworker, but quit after eight months, following the suggestion of a girl friend that they both become waitresses. Josephine readily consented to have intercourse with him and returned to his room several times thereafter for the same purpose.

the furnished room. In a few days she went to an employment agency through which she had previously obtained employment, and was found there by a policewoman who took her in custody as a runaway. 

OLGA was only in the seventh grade at 14 years, and lived in upper New York State with her parents, three younger brothers, and a younger sister. Following a quarrel with her mother, she left home and came to New York. There she flirted with a sailor and spent the night with him in a cheap midtown hotel. Olga returned home a few days later. Within two months she ran away again and repeated her previous experience of picking up a sailor and going to the same hotel with him. The man left early the next morning and Olga returned to Riverside Drive to try to make dates with other sailors of the United States fleet then in port. 

On returning alone to the hotel, the manager introduced her to a street car conductor who took her to dinner and to the theatre. The following day she flirted with several other men and made dates with them. The manager told her that a detective was searching for her and later that evening Olga was taken into custody. 

MILDRED lived in Pennsylvania with her her parents, an older sister and three younger brothers, and at the age of 15 was in the second year of 56

high school. Frequent quarrels with her mother made her unhappy at home and she decided to run away. Taking $40.00 of her mother's money, she came to New York with a girl friend of her own age.

In a few days the girls obtained factory jobs and were able to meet their ordinary needs on their wages. 

One evening Mildred met a man walking his dog and engaged him in conversation. She took his telephone number with the idea of making a date with him. She telephoned two days later and accepted his invitation to go to the theatre. After the performance she went to his room presumably to be shown the man's dog. While there she was prevailed upon to drink some wine, and her next recollection was when she awoke the following morning with her clothes removed and with the realization that she had been raped. Two days later the man gave her a check for $20.00. 

Within two weeks Mildred went home for the 

Christmas holidays and returned to New York in a few days, apparently with the consent of her mother. In a month's time she again met the man involved in her misadventure and accepted his invitation to accompany him on a visit to a woman friend. Mildred agreed to remain for a few days as she had no money.
