Mildred had been promised $5.00 for every man she had intercourse with, but no accounting was ever given to her. She left the apartment only once during this time when the woman took her to a physician for an examination. 

One day Mildred was sent to the market for some fruit in the company of Sophia, who occasionally came to the apartment when an extra girl was needed. On the way they decided to take an apartment and divide the earnings, but this partnership lasted only one week, for Sophia was arrested for soliciting. During these days a number of men were entertained by Mildred, among them being the man who was originally responsible for her predicament. She also "filled in" at another apartment when called upon. About this time Mildred became very depressed and, in an attempt at suicide, drank some iodine. A physician was called and he sent her to a hospital, where she remained four days. After discharge from the hospital, she returned to her former mode of earning a living, and shortly thereafter was arrested as a prostitute. 

HARRIET, one of eight children, lived in New Jersey with her father and stepmother, and at the age of 15 was only in the seventh grade. The first time she ran away she remained for two weeks at 58

the home of a girl friend in a nearby town. When the stepmother learned of her whereabouts she took her home. Three weeks later she ran away again and came to New York by hitchhiking. She telephoned a friend and was invited by him to his room in a midtown hotel, where she remained for three days and had intercourse with him several times. 

As he had to leave the city, he gave her $1.00 and advised that she should return home. Being afraid to do so, she went to visit a friend in New Jersey from whom she borrowed $2.00 and a dress, and came back to New York. She flirted with a man and concocted a story about needing money to get home and thus obtained $4.00 from him; the next morning she telephoned this newly found friend and agreed to visit him in a midtown hotel. He gave her money to rent a furnished room, to which he came the same day and had relations with her. 

A day later Harriet flirted with a salesman in a retail store, and accompanied him to his friend's apartment, where they found two men and all four had several drinks of whiskey. One man had intercourse with Harriet, and when she told him she had no money, he invited her to live with him. 

She agreed and soon began to entertain men whom she charged $5.00 each time she granted them her favors. The money was turned over to the man she was staying with, and he provided her with food and clothes.

A woman who lived in the next apartment arranged that Harriet should come to her place from time to time when she had extra "customers" to 59

accommodate, at the same fee. After three weeks of this existence, two detectives posing as "customers" arrested Harriet, the salesman who first brought her to the apartment, and the man in whose apartment she was carrying on. She told the detectives about the woman next door and the neighbor was likewise arrested. Harriet's entrance into professional prostitution came when she decided that since so many of her boy friends were recipients of her favors for nothing, she might just as well realize upon her kindness and thus make a lot of money.

WHY DO GIRLS RUN AWAY? 

A reading of the preceding 20 cases of girls who left home and became involved in sex difficulties, immediately raises the fundamental question as to why did they run away in the first place. Each girl presents a particular pattern, and it is somewhat difficult to be dogmatic and point a finger at one or even two or more causes, and claim that these and these alone were responsible for the situations in which these youngsters came to find themselves. 

It has been previously indicated that if there is any one underlying causative factor, it may be said to rest in the personality of the individual girl. While it is realized that placing the blame upon this base is very much like begging the question, it would be well to give recognition to a fundamental aspect of the problem so as to concentrate upon obtaining a better understanding of cause and effect in this matter. Having reached this tentative conclusion, 60

it becomes essential to recognize that human personality, in the generic sense, has so many facets, that a deep search for ultimate causes would be necessary in each case.

The picture becomes somewhat complicated when thought is given to certain factors that stand out in the life of each girl. Among these, it will be noted, were such given and reputed reasons as the mother's absence from home, desire for a good time, lonesomeness for friendship, alcoholism on the part of parents, craving for affection, cruelty of mother or stepmother, rigid and unsympathetic discipline at home, lure of an automobile ride, evil influence of an older sister, and apathy on the part of parents as to the interests, pastimes and activities of their children. It is a sad commentary on the lack of a sense of responsibility by the elders of these girls, when one notes that just so long as their daughters kept fairly regular hours, few questions were asked. Their spare time activities in attending dances, movies, or other events, automobile rides with strangers, overnight absences presumably at the homes of girl friends-all were apparently of no particular concern to some parents. 

Ten of the girls had run away on previous occasions, and their sex experiences often indicated that they were almost devoid of shame or other finer sensibilities. The readiness with which they accepted and continued degrading sex affairs, traded sex partners, procured girl friends to share or substitute in the experiences, accepted money, invited and stimulated the attentions of men-all 61

point towards a callousness among these young girls which well-nigh beggars description. The part that personality played in the fixing of attitudes of these girls, is undoubtedly of considerable importance. And yet, due recognition must be given to the inter-play with environmental factors, parental conduct and attitudes, family imitative patterns, and other matters which are involved in human conduct and its abnormal manifestations.

CONCLUSION 

For

The major question which comes to mind is the possibility for prevention and regeneration. It is quite proper to ask whether the experiences of the cited cases of runaways and those who constitute one half of the 1400 girls studied, might have been prevented, had certain forces been at play. instance, would things have turned out otherwise, if these girls had had proper recreational outlets, sympathetic parental supervision, adequate family income, no family or household overcrowding, decent clothes, proper schooling, and well-developed sex hygiene instruction. Unless an absolutist attitude is assumed which would hold that these girls were innately bad, that their mentality and personality were such that no amount or degree of adjustment would have been of avail, then it is fair to assume that certain community and family readjustments could have accomplished something, perhaps much, for these girls. It must be reiterated that while personality and individual idiosyncracy, 62

if one will, play a dominating part, it nevertheless remains true that some antisocial tendencies can frequently be modified and changed. So long as young people remain what they are today, the urge to run away and daydream of distant vistas where success and joy await the happy wanderer, boys and girls will continue to seek a way out from unhappy home and school life, and devastating and individually disturbing situations which are beyond their powers to solve. Without sympathy and understanding on the part of parents, teachers and others whose responsibility it is to help guide the lives of young people, similar individual and family disasters will continue to occur. 63

CHAPTER III  INTRODUCTION 

HE family is greater than love itself, for it TH includes, ennobles, makes permanent, all that is best in love. The pain of life is hallowed by it, the drudgery sweetened, its pleasures consecrated. It is the great trysting place of the generations, where past and future flash into the reality of the present.

It is the great storehouse in which the hardly-earned pleasures of the past, the inheritance of spirit and character from our ancestors are guarded and preserved for our descendants. And it is the great discipline through which each generation learns anew the lesson of citizenship that no man can live for himself alone." This beautiful description of the ideal family is from the pen Helen Bosanquet, and lends new hope to those who fear for the disruption of the family as a fundamental institution in social life. It is certainly a delightful and heartening picture in the face of the experiences of girls in broken homes, whose tales of woe and suffering are detailed. of Perhaps it might be well to define just what is meant by a broken home and to indicate what were the family situations faced by the girls whose histories are under consideration. By a broken home is meant one in which there is no father or mother, either because of sickness, death, institutional care or incarceration in prison; desertion or otherwise 64

missing; separated or divorced; illegitimacy of a child and separation of its parents; father or mother working or living out of town or in another country; both parents alive and child boarded out or placed in an institution; and both parents missing or dead. 

This is quite an inclusive list of possibilities leading to the break-up of a home, and yet, each type was represented in the cases dealt with. There are, of course, certain other situations which might give rise to the breaking up of a home. 

SOME BROKEN HOMES 

The importance of the broken home as a possible factor in sex delinquency and unfortunate sex experiences, may perhaps be brought to the fore by noting that 30 per cent of the girls in this series. had lost their fathers by death, 14.7 per cent of the mothers had died, and two per cent had lost both parents, making a total of 46.7 per cent of the girls whose homes were disrupted by death of one or both parents. If to this figure are added the many factors included in the several other situations which may be the cause of broken homes, then the picture is indeed a sad one. The environmental antecedents of many of the girls under consideration were such as to presage their involvement in serious conduct problems. This applies with particular emphasis to those girls whose family circles had been disrupted for one or more reasons.

Additional figures are available insofar as broken homes and sex delinquency among girls are concerned. In another study of determinants of sex 65

delinquency among adolescent girls, it was found that 71.6 per cent of 500 girls in a New York institution came from broken homes. Still other surveys have likewise indicated that for obvious reasons the broken home looms large in the problem of juvenile delinquency. When normally constituted, the family performs a vital service in the social order, training the children and implanting within them adherence to and respect for the group standards and institutions. When the family is disrupted, either by death, serious illness, legal or other separation, it cannot properly perform the functions or meet the needs for which the family group exists. 

This is most apparent in the homes of poor children, in which competent and well-trained substitutes for a missing father or a deceased mother often cannot be provided.

In the following cases which are cited in summarized form, the circumstance that a girl came from a broken home is not necessarily the only factor which contributed to her unfortunate experiences. It would be difficult to assume that the broken home or any other single phenomenon was the only cause in any given case. However, the broken home is accepted as being at least a heavily weighted item in the misconduct or misadventure. 

Were the question asked as to whether the girls whose experiences are recited could have avoided. the pitfalls, the equivocal answer would possibly be that the probability is that if their families had been normally constituted, the chances of missteps and debilitating occurrences would undoubtedly 66

have been lessened. A reading of the following histories may serve to clarify this point of view. 

EDNA was 15 years old and had only reached the 5-A grade in public school. When she was 12 years old her mother obtained a divorce, and then went out working to support herself and her daughter, thus leaving the young girl without proper supervision.

Edna's first sex experience occurred when she was 13 years old and lived in Pennsylvania with her mother, younger brother and an uncle. She was employed to take care of two young children and do light housework before and after school hours, sleeping in on several nights of each week.

The man of the house came to her room late one night when she was dozing off and raped her. He came to her room a second time several weeks later and forced her to have relations with him. 

Edna informed her mother who in turn notified the police.

When her mother remarried, Edna lived with her and the stepfather for two years, and subsequently spent some time with her father and younger brother, of whom she was very fond. Three older sisters were living apart, as the divorce had affected them as well. Mary, the oldest girl of the family, ran away from home when quite young because her parents quarreled so much. Edna came to stay with Mary in New York. Although she found her living with a man to whom she was not married, Edna remained with her as she had no other place to go. 67

Edna finally decided to leave her sister's home and joined another runaway girl of her own age.

They went to the neighborhood of the Brooklyn Navy Yard where they flirted with two sailors. 

However, a policeman had observed their conduct and, separating them from their newly found sailor friends, took them to a local Catholic priest who placed them in a proper home. 

LUELLA, 14 years of age, was in the 6-B grade and lived with her mother, stepfather and a sister three years old. She would occasionally talk to 28 year old John B., a neighbor whom she frequently met on the stoop of the house where she lived. He told her he sold dresses and several times invited her to his room to see some samples. 

She was always afraid to go, but one day he prevailed upon her to accompany him. Once there she quickly realized he had deceived her as he had no dresses. A sense of uneasiness came over her and she tried to get away, but he forced her to remain and then assaulted her. He warned her not to tell her mother, who had often advised Luella that if she ever became involved in a sex affair she would be sent to an institution.

About a month later Luella met John and confided to him that she did not feel well and had missed her period. He acted as though he was much disturbed and suggested she should come to his room where he would advise her what to do. 

She accompanied him and quickly learned he was unconcerned about her possible condition for he again forced her to submit to his usual demands. 68

Two months later Luella was certain she was pregnant and John promised he would take her to a woman to have her "fixed up." She again went to his room and remained there for two days, during which period he had relations with her several times. A neighbor who saw the missing girl on the street informed her mother and Luella was taken home. John B. was the only man who had ever "touched" her.

RUTH was 15 years old and had graduated from public school at the age of 13 years. evening high school for a year and a half and during the day stayed at home to help with the housework. She lived with her grandmother, a maiden aunt, a sister of 14 and a brother 10 years old. Her mother had been dead for six years, while the father was employed out of town and visited his children once or twice a week. Ruth ran away from home because her grandmother did not permit her out alone after 8 P. M., nor to have any boy friends. She had contemplated making this move for a long time because of a presumed oppressive home environment since the death of her mother. The day after leaving she obtained work in a store, but learning that her father had reported her to the police as missing, she gave up the job and remained at the home of a girl friend who had recently been married. 

Ruth had a 20 year old boy friend named Henry, whom she had known for six months. She met him whenever the supervision of her grandmother was relaxed, and had become sufficiently fond of 69

him to accept a ring with the understanding that they were engaged. The grandmother knew nothing of this affair. Henry came to visit her once when the young people with whom Ruth was staying were out for the evening. While there was no premeditation, they both "lost their heads" and had intercourse. The same thing happened a week later when the occupants of the apartment were not home. Ruth's affection for Henry soon began to wane, particularly because he had falsely accused her of not having been a virgin. They finally had a serious quarrel and she returned the ring as she wished to end their affair and to see no more of him. 

A few days later Ruth learned that the police were seeking her erstwhile boy friend because of a crime he had committed, and this information reinforced her dislike for him.

At the end of a month Ruth met an aunt at the home of a girl whom both were visiting and was persuaded to return to her grandmother, even though she stood in fear of her. 

MARTHA, 13 years old, had reached the 7-B grade and refused to go to school any longer, as she preferred to obtain a job to help meet the family needs. She lived with her mother, who kept house for Martha, her 10 year old brother, and an elderly male boarder. The mother supplemented the family income by sewing at home. Her husband had deserted his dependents three years previously, and was sending them a few dollars a week through the Domestic Relations Court. Through two older girl friends, Martha met several boys 70

who assured them that they could earn much money by soliciting subscriptions to magazines. They joined a group of 10 girls and 20 boys and were advised to tell prospective subscribers that they were trying to work their way through college. 

They reported every night to a crew manager, who in turn was responsible to a "Dr." G., whom the girls, however, never met. 

On arriving in Philadelphia, Martha and her two girl friends rented a furnished room, the three of them sleeping in one bed. They had unintentionally selected a rooming house in which prostitution was permitted, and Martha was made aware of this by the number of men coming to the house every night, as well as by those who attempted to assault her in the hallway.

Soliciting magazine subscriptions was difficult work and Martha earned from $2.00 to $3.00 a day. 

Many men suggested they would give her a subscription if she would make a date to go out with them at night. One evening the three girls went into a garage to solicit orders and accepted the offer of a man to go riding with him and his friend that night with the promise of subscriptions. Only one of the girls accompanied Martha, who sat in the back of the car with one of the men. When they reached a deserted section, Martha's companion proceeded to rape her despite her struggles and screams, while her friend who sat with the driver of the car and was apparently much more experienced in such matters, laughed at her. Later the men changed places and the girl friend went 71

into the back of the car and had relations with the other man.

The group then traveled to other Pennsylvania communities. In one town Martha and her two friends visited a factory to solicit subscriptions and made a date to meet three men that night. They went to a "speakeasy" and Martha joined the others in drinking. All became intoxicated and two of the men forced Martha to have relations with them. 

The next day the girls learned that the so-called "Dr." G. had been arrested, as those who had paid for their subscriptions did not receive the magazines. Martha and her two friends became involved in further sex experiences on succeeding days, until her friends decided to leave her, as they claimed she was not a "good sport." Their final advice to her was to make the men pay when they had intercourse with her. However, Martha was too naive to follow their suggestion, even though she knew that they had made a lot of money through their misconduct.

Martha returned home when the police became active in putting a stop to the solicitors. Her plan was to marry a boy friend in New York as soon as he was cured of a disease contracted by "going with girls." He had never attempted to do anything to Martha, but she knew he had tried to have relations with her friend. 

JENNIE was 15 years old. She was born in

New York City and lived with her widowed mother who was a practical nurse, and three brothers 22, 18 and 12 years of age. Jennie's first sex experi- 72

ence occurred when she was 14 years old, at which time she accused a man of having raped her. After this incident her mother sent Jennie to live with an aunt in Connecticut, where she remained for four months and worked as a clerk in a retail store. While waiting for her, Jennie met Henry on the street and accepted his invitation to go to his sister's home, where she remained for two nights. On the second evening, when Henry and

The mother summoned a policeman and had both Jennie and Henry taken into custody, the latter being held on a charge of impairing the morals of a minor. After the trial, Jennie was taken back to New York by her mother. 

HANNAH, aged 14, was in the seventh grade of public school, and had been demoted because of her deportment. She lived with a childless aunt and uncle; her parents had been divorced some years previously, and she had not seen her father since the court proceeding. Her mother had remarried and had six children by the second hus- 73

band. Hannah had been living with her aunt for nine years and prior to that time with her grandmother, who died when Hannah was five years old. 

The grandfather then made his home with the aunt, and when he remarried Hannah remained with her also.

For two years Hannah went to a local food store to make purchases for her aunt. The storekeeper was married, had no children, and occupied an apartment above the store. At first he would inquire of Hannah about the well-being of her family; after a time he would kiss her and give her $.25 every time she came to the store. His intimacies with her increased from month to month, and she would come to his store not for the purpose of making purchases, but to receive money for the indecencies he perpetrated when his wife was not around.

When the storekeeper began to indulge in sex activities with Hannah, she received from $.50 to $1.00 each time she came to his apartment, which was about once a week. Her school teacher was told that Hannah visited this man rather frequently, and when she inquired whether he molested her in any way, Hannah told the truth. The headworker of a settlement house that Hannah attended also learned of her doings and likewise followed up the matter. Shortly thereafter the situation was brought to the attention of the school principal, who called in the mother and aunt, to whom Hannah repeated the story of her experiences. This storekeeper was the only one who had relations with her. 74

GRACE, at 15, was only in the 6-B grade in public school. She lived with her widowed grandmother who went out to do housework by the day. 

Her mother had died two years previously and 

Grace had never seen her father. She had been told that he had also died. 

When Grace was 14 years old a boy she went to school with took her to Central Park and she permitted him to indulge in sex play with her. A year later Harold, who also went to school with her, asked Grace to let him "do it" to her and, after a little coaxing, she allowed him to have sex privileges. They went to the roof landing of a neighborhood tenement and, when he repeated his request a month later, they again went to the same place and had relations. Some weeks thereafter 

Harold told Grace his mother wanted to see her. 

She went to his home and found only Harold there.

This time he had to force her into submission as she tried to fight him off. In a few months the grandmother became suspicious of her appearance and took Grace to a clinic for examination. A diagnosis of pregnancy was readily made and she then told of her sex experience. 

ANNA, aged 13, was in the vocational class in public school. Her mother died in Virginia when 

Anna was nine months old, and the father brought the infant girl to New York to be raised by an aunt. 75

When Anna was only 11 years old, Edwin, 17, "touched" her on four occasions while they were in the home of a neighbor. A year later, Anna acquired a boy friend, Edward, whom she had met at a school dance. He had relations with her once in her own home when no one else was present. 

Shortly thereafter, Edward left the city to return to Virginia. Anna wrote him a note in school which fell into the hands of her teacher. Fearing that something might happen to her because of the contents of the note, she decided to run away. She accidentally met her married friend Mary, who was separated from her husband. Mary in turn introduced Anna to James, who took them to a cellar apartment where they slept in one bed, and during the night James had relations with both girls. 

Mary went to work in the morning, and Anna spent the day with friends and attended a motion picture theatre. In the evening she visited a dance hall where she met William, who gave her $1.00 to pay for a room at a Harlem hotel. The following day Anna went to the home of a young couple of her acquaintance, and sent a note to William, telling him of her whereabouts. He came to see her a few days later, and they indulged in intercourse on the three occasions that he visited Anna at her new stopping place.

At the end of two weeks the young woman with whom Anna was staying telephoned her aunt. The latter suggested that Anna had nothing to fear and should return home, which advice she immediately acted upon. 76

EMILY, aged 15, and in the eighth grade of public school, lived with her widowed father and 12 year old brother. She became somewhat depressed following the death of her mother. The neighborhood boys bothered her a great deal with improper sex suggestions, as a result of which she decided to find work and leave home. With the first week's wages of $12.00 as her total capital, she rented a furnished room and looked for another job. After two days she met her friend John and explained to him that she had run away from home and had no place to stay. He advised her to return to her father but when she refused, he rented another furnished room for her. She lived in this room for six weeks and John visited her every evening. He paid for the room and gave her spending money in addition. However, he repeatedly asked her to have intercourse with him with a promise of marriage in case difficulties arose. His demands had started even before she ran away from home, but she persistently refused to accede to his requests.

One night John visited her in the furnished room and remained but a short while. After Emily had gone to bed he returned and forced her to have relations with him. They quarreled as a consequence of his behavior, after which Emily decided to support herself and obtained a factory job. At the end of three days her ready money was gone, leaving her even without sufficient carfare to return to the furnished room. That night she spent on a park bench until a policeman took her to a station 77

house and sent for her father. 

John was the only man who ever had relations with her. 

THERESA, aged 13, was in the 6-B grade, and lived with a woman she presumed to be her mother; a male roomer shared the small apartment with them. The "mother" went out to work and left the young girl entirely to her own resources throughout the day. One afternoon, when alone,

Theresa flirted with two boys she saw on the street from her apartment window. They came up to the apartment, and soon forced her to have relations with them. They then departed, but returned in an hour together with their friend Marie, 13, who was likewise Theresa's friend, and had relations with this girl. Theresa refused to permit them to do anything to her. 

Theresa's "mother" came home unexpectedly, and finding the group in the apartment, became suspicious of their actions and questioned Theresa.

She refused to make any admission of misconduct. 

The next day a representative of a Big Sister organization was called in and the truth finally was revealed. As one outcome of this experience, Theresa learned for the first time that the woman she had known as her "mother" was in reality her mother's sister, and that her mother had disappeared; she never knew anything about her father.

It further transpired, during questioning, that the previous year when Theresa was only 12, her "mother" had an occasional male visitor. Once he stayed so late that he was invited to sleep in a room adjoining the bedroom shared by Theresa 78

and her supposed mother. In the morning the latter went to her work and Theresa left for school. 

When she returned home to fix her own lunch, the man was still in the apartment and after a struggle, he raped the young girl. She never told anyone of this experience.

ANNA, 15 years old and in the 7-B grade, was the daughter of a baker who had been deserted by his wife when his daughter was quite young. She had an older brother with whom the mother had been pregnant before her parents were married, though another man was responsible for the pregnancy. For some years after the desertion, the father, who worked at night, maintained a housekeeper to look after Anna. As she grew older, the realization came upon her that her father was living with this woman illicitly. This common law union was finally dissolved. 

Anna's troubles began when she started a flirtation on a subway train with a young man named Stephen and took him to her home while her father was away. She had intercourse with him the first time he visited her and on a number of occasions over a period of months. Each time he came he gave her $5.00, though she knew several of her friends who did similar things with boys and young men but did not accept any money for their favors. 

Anna's father finally became suspicious of her appearance, and when he took her to a physician a diagnosis of well-advanced pregnancy was readily made. Stephen was the only man with whom she had relations. 79

MARGARET was 13 years old and in the seventh grade of public school. Her mother was dead and she lived with her father and his common law wife. After a quarrel, Margaret decided to leave home, largely because of resentment over her father's illicit relationship. Mistreatment naturally caused her to be disrespectful towards the woman who set the young girl a bad example. 

When she finally decided to run away, Margaret sought out her friend Janet. Both girls went to a motion picture theatre where they flirted with two men. After the show Janet went off with one of them and Margaret was asked by John to go to his room. She accompanied him and on her promise to live with him he in turn offered to provide her with food and clothing. After a week of this arrangement during which time intercourse was attempted on several occasions, John suggested that Margaret should accompany a woman friend of his to Connecticut and help her to look after her baby. 

She returned to New York with the woman and baby in two weeks.

Needing money with which to pay for food and lodging, Margaret applied for a job as a dancing hostess in one of the cheap Broadway dance halls, and was hired despite her apparent youthfulness. 

She soon became friendly with another hostess and went to live in her furnished room. Margaret quickly became aware of the sex demands of customers in these dance halls, and within a week had accompanied five men to furnished rooms and had intercourse with them. 80

One of the men with whom she had relations asked her to live with him regularly and she agreed. 

They rented a furnished room, posing as man and wife, and lived together for five weeks. At the end of that time Margaret accidentally met her father, who brought her to a police station as an incorrigible minor. 

HANNAH was 14 years of age, and lived with her widowed father, three sisters 19, 17, and 10 years old, and a younger brother of eight. She attended public school and was in the eighth grade. 

A year previously she became acquainted with several undesirable young fellows and kept company with them against the wishes of her father and older sisters. One night she made an appointment with Philip, aged 18, and went to a local park with him where he took her into the bushes and raped her. Two weeks later she made another date with him, and again accompanied him to the park where she permitted him to have intercourse with her a second time.

About this time her father came to believe he could not control her at home and, through a social agency, placed her in a so-called opportunity home where she assisted Mrs. K. with the housework and continued to attend school. The home selected was inadequate in various ways and the supervision over Hannah was apparently lax. Another place was found where she remained for six months. During this period, however, she continued her undesirable outside contacts and, while she apparently 81

did not get into any serious difficulties, it became necessary to take her back to her own home. 

HELEN, at 15, had only reached the 8-A grade. 

She left school in order to do housework for a family, and later to keep house for her widowed father, a brother 10 years old, and a sister eight years of age.

Her difficulties began when she met 

Samuel, aged 28, at a dance hall in Greenwich Village. She saw him frequently after the first meeting since they both lived in the same tenement house, where he maintained a three-room cellar flat. 

Sam was a Jack-of-all-trades and in addition, bottled and sold bootleg liquor.

Helen's father was a garage helper, his working hours keeping him away from home during the night, when his young children were left alone. One night after he left for his place of employment, Sam came to visit Helen and started fooling with her. She became frightened and tried to get him out of the apartment but failed; he suddenly grabbed her and forcibly had intercourse with her. In order to try to stop her tears, he told her not to be afraid as he was going to marry her, but that she must tell no one of their relations. His visits to her home continued for several weeks, during which time he had relations with her many times. 

With a promise of marriage and of expensive clothes, he prevailed upon Helen to accompany him to a furnished rooming house where she was introduced as his wife. A few days later they had a serious quarrel and Helen joined a girl friend in renting a room which they occupied for a week. 82

Upon returning to her home to get some clothes, she was told by her young brother that their father wanted her back. She waited till he returned from work. When he questioned her as whether Sam had done anything to her, she told him the whole story. 

HENRIETTA, aged 15, was in the seventh grade of public school, and lived with her mother, stepfather, and three sisters 16, 12 and 10 years of age. She had become acquainted with two young men who were garage workers. One of them, Frank, invited Henrietta and her 14 year old friend

Mary to a party and took them to a furnished room. 

They found no one there and, becoming suspicious, asked to be taken home. Frank suggested that they wait a few minutes while he went out to telephone to find out why the other guests had not arrived. 

When he did not return in a little while, the girls prepared to leave, but found that they had been locked in. About 2:30 A. M. a man about 25 years old opened the door and proceeded to make himself at home. He suddenly seized Mary and forced her to have relations with him, in spite of Henrietta's efforts to protect her. The girls remained with the stranger till 6 A. M., when they went home.

Henrietta again met Frank two weeks later and, when he invited her for an automobile ride, she agreed and they drove only as far as the furnished rooming house in which Mary had been assaulted two weeks previously. He asked Henrietta to go upstairs with him, and though she refused at first, finally consented after some coaxing. Once in the 83

He would not permit her to leave till the next morning, when she started directly for home. Henrietta met her mother on the way and found she had been searching for her with a policeman. 

ALICE, aged 14, lived with her mother, two brothers 18 and 16 years of age, and a man to whom her mother was not married. Six years previously her mother had deserted her husband to live with this man who was still a part of the household. The father cared for the four children, but finding after two years that he could not support them, he placed the youngest boy in an orphan asylum, and sent

Alice and her two brothers to their mother. She, in turn, somehow had the three children accepted in an institution, where they remained till Alice was 13 years old.

Alice kept house when the mother was forced to go to a hospital for a serious operation. After a week of the mother's absence, the "friend" who had asked the three children to call him uncle, came to the kitchen where Alice was working, carried her to his room, and raped her. After that he forced her to have relations with him a number of times and perpetrated other indignities upon her. 

When the mother returned from the hospital, still very ill, Alice and her brothers were ordered out of the house by their so-called uncle. They went to live with a friend of their mother's who had taken an interest in them. In a little while Alice presented various signs of being pregnant, and when 84

questioned, told the whole story of what had happened to her.

MAUD, who was 13, had only attained the 5-B grade and lived with her mother who went out to work, and an aunt who kept house and was a seamstress. Her father and mother had been separated for some years.

One day on the way home from school with her 13 year old friend Ruth, they met Harry, a porter in their apartment house. He made various suggestive remarks to them, but they ignored him and continued on their way. Ruth's mother also worked out, and the young girl had to prepare her own noon meals.

The girls went to Ruth's apartment, and in a little while some one knocked on the door. 

When it was opened, Harry walked in and bluntly told Ruth he wanted to have intercourse with her. 

She told him she did not know what he meant and he went on to explain the sex act. When she refused and threatened to tell her mother, Harry promised Ruth some money if she would let him "do it" to her, whereupon she agreed. After the act he approached Maud with the same request and, when she attempted to run out of the apartment, he seized her and likewise raped her. The girls cried and he gave them each five cents with a warning not to tell anyone of the incident.

A few days later he asked Ruth to meet him in a vacant apartment and to bring Maud with her. 

Harry again had relations with both girls and gave them some money. During the next few weeks 

Harry accosted them on several occasions and asked 85

them to have relations with him. 

Ruth always agreed and permitted him to do so, but Maud finally threatened to report him to the police if he did not stop annoying them. He then became frightened and begged the girls to deny that he had done anything to them. Maud soon picked up sufficient courage to report the matter to the local police station, with the result that the offender was quickly taken into custody. 

ANNA, who was 16 years old, and had been graduated from public school, lived with her grandmother and an uncle, while her mother and stepfather remained in another city. At 13 she had her first sex experience at the home of a girl friend.

The latter asked her to go into her brother's bedroom and, once there, the girl friend held her arms while the brother tore off her clothes and raped her. After that she avoided seeing them again.

When Anna was 15, she had relations with an 18 year old fellow, whose married sister she visited from time to time. On one such visit the brother was home alone. He asked her to have relations with him and Anna consented. When requested to repeat the act on a number of subsequent occasions, she refused as he had hurt her very much the first time.

A year later, Anna had a sex experience with a 17 year old boy. On some pretense he led her to the roof of the house in which she lived. There he threatened to beat her unless she permitted him to have intercourse, and she yielded under this duress. 

MARY was 14 years old, in the seventh grade 86

of public school, and lived with her widowed mother who went out to work, two sisters 19 and 11 years old, a 20 year old brother and his wife, and two male boarders 21 and 32 years of age. Peter, aged 

32, had been with the family for about a year. He had invited Mary to go out with him on several occasions but she usually refused, though she accompanied him when her mother and brother went along. One day, when Mary came home from school together with her 11 year old sister, she found Peter in the apartment. 

The 

The younger girl asked him for money to buy some candy and he Her gave her ten cents and sent her out. Then, taking

She did not altogether object to having relations with him, as she liked him and he had often promised to marry her at a future date. After that he continued to have intercourse with Mary about twice a week for a number of weeks, always in his room and when no one was home. mother, of course, was not aware of the relationship, although she had previously warned Mary to tell her if any man ever made any improper suggestions or tried to do anything to her. When the question of marriage had been raised previously, the mother had objected because of the youthfulness of her daughter and the marked difference in ages. 

Finally, Peter told the mother and brother that Mary belonged to him. This was the first inkling they had of the improper relations that had existed, and the arrest of Peter quickly followed. 87

ANGELICA was 13 years old, and only in the 5-B grade at school. She lived with her mother, a sister of nine and a brother of three. In addition, there were two male boarders in the crowded family quarters. The mother had been separated from her husband since the birth of the youngest child. 

Angelica's first sex experience occurred when she was but six years old, three young boys indulging in some sex play with her at that time.

A more serious situation arose when she was thirteen. An acquaintanceship with Henry, 24, resulted in frequent meetings on the street or in hallways, without the knowledge or consent of her mother. On one occasion she was sent to do some family marketing and, meeting Henry, went to the movies with him instead. They did not leave the theatre till 10 P. M. and he asked her to accompany him to his furnished room. While she objected at first, he persuaded her to go with him, and once there, he kissed and petted her though she tried to stop him.

Suddenly, he put his hand over her mouth to keep her from making an outcry and forced her to have intercourse with him. Fear overcame Angelica and she remained in the room for the night, though sleeping in the bed alone. Early the following morning, not daring to face her mother and not fully recovered from the effects of the previous night's experience, she went to New Jersey to visit an uncle. Upon finding that he was not home, she returned to the city and went to school, where 88

she encountered her mother and confessed everything to her.

THE BROKEN HOME AND DELINQUENCY 

Someone has well said that out of interest and joy in caring for children in their weakness and watching that weakness grow to strength, family life came into being and has persisted through the ages. The emphasis on the weakness of the children is of particular import to this discussion of the broken home as a factor in sex misadventure and misconduct. A careful reading of the preceding cases, and the retention of a mental picture of the broken home situations depicted, doubtless leaves one with the feeling that there must be something in this matter of family disruption and its relation to the sex involvement of the daughters of such homes. As figures already presented indicate, the broken home was no unusual occurrence, but appeared rather frequently in the histories of the many girls in question. This fact must be emphasized so that the impression may not be left that the worst cases have been selected to prove a particular point of view. Rather, there was such a plethora of material that elimination became a rather difficult task. 

If young girls require parental leadership, control and supervision, they are certainly greatly in need of such help and service in their pre-adolescent and adolescent years. Of the twenty (20) girls discussed in this chapter, six were 13 years old, four were 14, nine were 15, and one was 16 years 89

old. They were of the ages when motherly help, advice and sympathy were most in demand, while paternal guidance and protection should have been available. Such lack of help and supervision can undoubtedly be counted upon to produce the dire results previously depicted. A further analysis of the histories of these girls indicates that one father deserted his family; two mothers did likewise; parents were divorced in two instances and separated in two others; five fathers and six mothers had died. Five of the mothers remarried, thus complicating the family picture for their daughters by bringing a strange man into the family circle. 

Some of the other outstanding situations included mothers who kept male boarders; fathers who lived with women to whom they were not married; both fathers and mothers who deserted their children or left them with other relatives; two instances where the girls were probably illegitimate; a mother who remarried and had six children by her second husband, with no room in the new family for the child by the first marriage; a mother who ran off with her lover, taking her young and impressionable daughter with her; and other situations which did not tend to develop moral strength and fortitude in girls who needed proper guidance and understanding.

It has often been remarked that "when emotion becomes involved in a situation, reason flies out of the window." Parents who find that their own family life is not what they should like it to be, or whose youthful dreams have not borne fruit, 90

and precipitate a situation which may result in disruption of the family group, should give serious thought to the deplorable status of so many children who are bereft of father or mother, or both. More serious and objective thinking, with greater concern for the future of children in apparently unhappy homes, would lead many who seek a way out to defer such steps unless under extreme provocation. Young people who plan marriage might well bethink themselves of what they are facing insofar as the happiness of as yet unborn children is concerned. A reckless and haphazard attitude in the most serious step in the life of an individual may well mete out its own penalty, but why visit such undue misfortune upon others too young and inexperienced to forfend themselves against forces which are not fully understood? Why should children be made the victims of hasty and ill-considered actions of their parents? 

Separation, divorce and desertion, which is often the so-called poor man's divorce, are controllable situations which warrant careful attention for the sake of growing children. Many marriages end in divorce, and there are so many children of separated and divorced parents, that our social life is, as it were, corroded by the fate of such children. A fundamental question is whether parents who, for the sake of their children, remain unhappily bound together in marriage, are doing the right thing, or whether they should seek a way out of their own real or fancied difficulties. 

Should they separate and bring emotional and other 91

shocks upon their offspring, or remain together and expose the youngsters to tense and quarrelsome home situations? These are difficult questions upon which to generalize, as each family situation demands its own solution. Whatever step is contemplated or actually taken, it would be well to keep ever in mind the chasm which lies in the path of children of broken homes.

CONCLUSION 

There is no substitute for normal home life and intelligent parenthood in the rearing of children. 

It is in the home that the child's needs for affection, security and opportunities for growth and development, which play so important a part in shaping the personality, are met or thwarted. Even affectionate and intelligent parents may not always fully understand the child's needs for security and growth. Security is founded upon the emotional maturity of parents, upon justice, truthfulness and order in the home. Opportunities for development can be given the child only by parents who want to see the full flowering of their offspring, and are willing and prepared to make more than the ordinary sacrifices and endure more than the usual self-denials. With parents ready to do so, and given length of life so as to raise their children at least to the age of young adulthood, the tales of misery and mistreatment would be fewer, and unbroken family life would take on an even higher significance. 92

TH 

CHAPTER IV  INTRODUCTION 

HOSE who are proponents of large families with several children, have pictured, among other things, the beauties of life as a member of such a group, as well as the training and development of worthwhile attributes individual members are apt to receive. The obverse of this picture is occasionally left unmentioned by some who may favor the large family organization under modern conditions of urban life. Competent observers have in part summarized this situation by stating that if the family is large; if the home is thereby crowded; if the mother is distracted by the presence of many children and can give little individual attention to any of them; if the burden of custody and discipline during her absence fall upon the eldest child at home, then numerous difficult personal situations are apt to arise. Still further, if there are relatives or lodgers lending complexity to an already confused family circle; if there are an unsympathetic stepparent and a mixed family of children-then there is little opportunity for children brought up under such unfavorable conditions to have a proper chance to mature into fully normal, self-controlled and self-respecting men and women. 

The size of a family usually determines the amount of attention and devotion that can be bestowed upon each child. The first-born are apt 93

to receive more care, because during their infancy and early youth overcrowding is not a problem. On the other hand, in families with several children the youngest are likely to be the victims of circumstance, and to make normal demands upon overworked mothers which are often difficult to meet. 

The picture, however, becomes more complicated where there is a doubling-up or combining of families through the remarriage of a man with several children and a woman with children of her own; an even more serious situation arises when such a man and woman proceed to have additional offspring, thus creating three sets of children in one family circle.

Aside from the lack of parental control and guidance, children of large families are often the victims of sacrifices and denials which all too frequently undermine their bodies and adversely affect their character. The avidity with which a young girl will submit to indignities for the sake of a few cents; the quick acceptance of the promise of pretty clothes and good food for companionship with questionable characters; the easy manner of picking up strange men and falling into their suggested plans for visits to furnished rooms or cheap hotels, or an automobile ride these and other phases of starvation of the body and spirit are too evident in the cases cited to need elaboration. Many have decried the fact that the so-called best families, or at least those possessed of a fair measure of economic security, are apt to have the fewest children, whereas those least able to support themselves and their 94

progeny, are the very ones creating havoc for their own families and adding to the problems which the community has to face. This has become a matter of serious concern during the past few years of widespread industrial depression and extensive public relief programs.

DELINQUENCY AND LARGE FAMILIES 

The utmost care must always be exercised in the interpretation of social phenomena so as not to be misled by presumable facts which may tend to prove a given point. The discussion presented in this chapter relates primarily to the sex experiences of girls who, incidentally perhaps, happened to be members of large families. It is not presumed that their difficulties arose only because they had a number of brothers and sisters or, as might possibly be reasoned, in spite of the fact that they were fortunate in having both older and younger brothers and sisters. This matter at once brings to the fore the question as to the importance of poverty in relation to prostitution, and the effect of economic depressions upon the recruiting of girls and women for a life of sexual promiscuity. Many have given thought to this subject, often with varying conclusions. The emotional and mental factors have been stressed instead of the economic phases in the determinations of some, while others have noted the greater ease with which some girls, at least, become sexually delinquent when faced with the denial of even the simplest physical comforts and needs. 95

Whether or not unemployment is responsible for a large measure of prostitution, is still an open question. Be that as it may, the following cases are cited exactly for what they represent—a group of young girls from comparatively overcrowded homes who became involved in sex experiences which might well have been avoided in most instances. 

Ignorance of certain fundamental facts relating to the sex function was often a major cause of their difficulties.

One may perhaps interpolate or read between the lines, the mental or emotional make-up of these girls; their so-called sex hunger, if such there were; their yearnings for new emotional outlets; their desire to satisfy a curiosity of sex activity witnessed in their own overcrowded homes; the urge for male companionship as a means, among other things, of getting away from drab and unpleasant home surroundings. All these factors played a part in the experiences of the girls whose episodes and sex experiences are delineated in the following pages.

MINERVA was 12 years old and lived in a small apartment with her parents, four brothers, five sisters, and a 26 year old male boarder. On a visit to the home of a school friend, she met Henry, who owned a tailor shop in the neighborhood. A month later she saw him on the street and, in answer to his questions, told him she was taking a note to her mother from the school principal relative to her own conduct. Henry took the note from Minerva and suggested he would go to the 96

school. He came to see the teacher in the afternoon of the same day and introduced himself as the girl's uncle. She denied this relationship and informed the teacher that he was merely trying to get "fresh" and that she should pay no attention to him. 

A few weeks after this incident, when Minerva was again visiting the home of her friend Beatrice, she was asked to accompany her and two other girls to Henry's tailoring shop, to which Beatrice had been sent by her mother. After the four girls reached the store, he told them to go to the rear of the establishment. He then seized Minerva, put his hand over her mouth, threw her on a long tailoring table, and raped her. The other girls were present and saw what happened. Henry then did the same thing to Beatrice and compelled the other girls to permit him to engage in certain sex play. Warning them not to tell anyone of what he had done, he sent them out of his shop. Minerva told her mother the whole story as soon as she came home. Acting upon the suggestion of Minerva's school teacher, whose advice the mother sought immediately, she went to the proper authorities with her complaint and action was taken to apprehend the culprit. 

SARA was 15 years old, had been graduated from public school, and attended high school for a few months. She lived with her parents, one older and three younger sisters, and two young brothers. After leaving high school Sara held several factory jobs in quick succession 97

due, in large measure, to her restlessness. She was very fond of dancing and often went alone to Coney Island with the expectation of picking up a male companion as a dance partner. On one such trip, she made the acquaintance of a 17 year old girl of questionable habits, and through her met several men whose moral standards made Sara rather apprehensive. Nothing, however, happened because she was on her guard. As she refused to smoke or drink, her newly found acquaintances of one night would generally drop her. Whenever she went to Coney Island she usually left her companions in time to reach home by 11 P. M. On several occasions her parents found it necessary to upbraid Sara for the late hours she was keeping, as previous to her dancing infatuation she always was in bed by 10 P. M. Among her pick-up friends was a sailor with whom she went to several dance halls, but as she was not particularly attracted to him and refused to drink and smoke, his proposals regarding sex matters resulted in no serious consequences. 

Together with a girl friend she first met at Coney

Island, Sara picked up two men and, after dancing with them for several hours, went riding in their automobile. The intentions of the man Sara sat with were patently dishonorable, and quickly recognizing that fact, she succeeded in rebuffing his advances till she was brought home at 4 A. M. This time her mother gave her a severe scolding and threatened to take some action if Sara continued such behavior. Fearing that she might actually 98

do so, Sara ran away and spent the next night in the women's lavatory of a subway station. 

Her ability to avoid sex entanglements was apparently the outgrowth of an experience she had had a few months previously with Harry, to whom she had been introduced by a girl friend. He frequently came to Sara's home and met with the approval of her parents. The young people went rowing and bus riding and participated in other perfectly proper recreational activities. One evening, after having known each other for several weeks, they went to Central Park and indulged in kissing and petting. This love-making ended in intercourse, without any understanding on Sara's part of the full import of her conduct, as she knew little of sex matters. Harry prevailed upon her to have relations with him on four other occasions, and repeatedly asked her to marry him. Despite the fact that she finally came to realize what she had been cajoled into doing, she refused to marry him as her affection for him had waned. Sara believed that she was not really a bad girl, and even though she had made a mistake once, she did not want to spoil her future by becoming bound to a man she did not love. 

ROSE, 14 years old and in the 8-A grade, was one of eight children, as she had two sisters and five brothers. The entire family of ten lived in three rooms the mother, Rose and two younger children shared one bed, three children slept in a folding bed, and the father and two children used still another folding bed. Rose was somewhat dis- 99

tressed over the family situation, particularly as her father frequently became drunk, cursed and beat the children and failed to provide the money he earned for necessities. She accompanied her mother several times to the Domestic Relations Court to compel the father to behave and to support his family. 

Because of this neglect and mistreatment, Rose left home and was away for three days, which time she spent with her godmother. It was her ambition to obtain a job and to earn enough to buy much needed clothes for herself. However, her mother induced her to return and promised that the father would behave in the future.

The family lived in a notoriously poor section of Manhattan, with many disreputable characters in the district. A week after she rejoined her family, Rose was raped by a group of neighborhood loafers. As she was mounting the steps of the house she lived in, four men intercepted her and asked whether she wanted to have relations with them and belong to their gang. Rose knew of other girls who did such things. She refused and ran away to the nearby Pennsylvania Railroad Station where she spent the balance of the day. 

When she reached the house again and saw no one in the dark hallway, she started up to her flat. 

A man suddenly seized her and he was joined by three others. They turned out to be the same hoodlums who accosted Rose earlier that afternoon.

Forcing her into a hall lavatory, the leader of the group pointed a revolver with one hand and 

100

covered her mouth with the other. He then raped her, and was followed by his three companions. 

When footsteps were heard in the hall, they opened the door and put her out.

Rose told her mother of this experience immediately. She was afraid of the gang of men and when she refused to take any action, her daughter insisted that something must be done as, otherwise, life would be unbearable for her in the neighborhood. The mother then summoned up sufficient courage and reported the matter to the authorities. 

MALVINA was 16 and had reached the 8-B grade in school. She lived with her parents, three brothers and three sisters between seven and 20 years of age. Her first sex experience occurred when she was only 13 and followed a visit to the home of 18 year old Charles, in the company of a friend. As soon as the girls arrived he sent the friend away, took Malvina into his bedroom and locked the door. He then forced her onto a bed and raped her. The common warning not to tell anyone followed. She never saw him again.

A year later Malvina made the acquaintance of another man and agreed to go to his room, where she permitted him to have intercourse with her. 

Within a week she flirted with Edward at a local motion picture theatre. He saw her home and they met frequently during the following two years. At the end of that time they had apparently become quite fond of one another, and when he invited her to his room, she allowed him to indulge in sex intimacies. Malvina thought there was nothing 101

wrong in permitting him these privileges, especially since she had previous sex experiences without any serious consequences. When the first signs of pregnancy appeared within a few weeks, she told Edward about her condition. He doubted she was pregnant but offered to marry her if her fears proved to be true. Malvina confided in her mother some weeks later and was advised to marry her 18 year old boy friend.

KATE was 11 years old and in the 4-B grade. 

Her father had been dead for several years and she lived with her mother and five brothers and sisters. 

Tony worked in a factory across the street from the house in which Kate lived, and she had noticed him on the street several times. One day he offered her five cents if she would let him do something to her. She tried to get away but he pulled her into a hallway and attempted to rape her. When she screamed he beat her over the face, and warned her not to tell anyone. Whenever Tony met Kate on the street after that incident he sought to entice her by an offer of money to go with him to the rear of the hallway in the house where he lived. She invariably refused and in spite of protests, he dragged the child into the hall and forced her to permit him to try to have relations with her on four subsequent occasions.

She finally told her mother that Tony molested and chased her, but because she feared him, did not relate all that had happened. Her mother took no action aside from a general warning to keep away from him. The last time he forced her 102

into the hallway he had offered her $.15 which she refused to accept. On that occasion when 

Tony returned to the factory Kate ran to the corner and told her story to a policeman. She accompanied him to the factory and Tony was taken into custody. He was the only one who had attempted sex relations with Kate. 

FLORENCE was 13 years old, in the 6-B grade, and an only child. Her mother had died when

Florence was an infant, and the father had remarried. The household in which she lived consisted of the father who had become blind and remained at home; the stepmother who worked out as a laundress; three lodgers, including a husband and wife, and an unmarried man. While there were no other children in this home, living conditions were crowded and otherwise undesirable for a growing girl.

Fearing that she would be scolded by her stepmother for a minor misdeed, Florence ran away from home. She went to visit a supposedly married girl friend, and remained with her three days. During this period a number of young men and women came to the flat and became intoxicated on liquor purchased from the friend.
