OVERCROWDING AND DELINQUENCY 

Family overcrowding has several aspects when looked upon from the point of view of delinquency. 

There is, of course, the primary factor of health, and its possible effects upon the future and conduct of individual children. Standards of decency are naturally difficult to maintain in congested tenement homes. Frequently the addition of a new baby in such poor and already crowded environment may not be a matter of joy and cause for celebration, but may rather give rise to a sense of futility for the preceding children in the family circle.

It is difficult to attempt the recommendation of any given panacea for the problem of overcrowding due to the number of children in a family. There are various economic, educational and biological factors, as well as religious beliefs, which stand in the way of applying any single remedy. Whether one type of solution or another is advisable, be it family subsidies, or legalization and dissemination of birth control information, or even of abortion under state auspices, as some have suggested, the social consequences of large families among low economic groups are many and complex. Children in such groups and especially in urban communities, often become the unwilling victims of economic 119

and environmental factors which spell despair and ruin to their lives at an early age. When to these difficulties are added such others as serious illness or death of a parent, immoral or degraded parents or other relatives in the family circle, residence in slum neighborhoods-then the lot of the young boys and girls who are members of such families is indeed a sad one.

It is not presumed, of course, that there are no considerable exceptions to this general proposition.

There are many men and women who have reached the heights of professional and commercial careers, and have developed into fine members of society, whose origins go back to homes with low incomes and many children. In the cases of many such successful persons, the added struggles and denials may even have contributed much to the development of fine character traits. Nevertheless, it is patent that families whose limited living quarters are crowded with children, and the income of the chief wage earner is inadequate to give all the offspring their birthright of a good home in its broadest sense, are unwittingly the cause of many human tragedies and no inconsiderable amount of delinquency.

SUMMARY OF HISTORIES 

An analysis of the histories of the preceding cases gives the following picture of overcrowding: in one family there were six individuals, but they included five adults and the girl in question, with a blind father, a stepmother and three lodgers—a 120

rather unpleasant and unsatisfactory family circle for a young girl. There was one family of six children and a widowed mother; three of eight persons; six of nine; four of ten; two of eleven; two of twelve; and one had thirteen individuals in the family group.

This overcrowding usually existed in a three or a four room flat, with all kinds of intrafamily problems constantly to the fore. Fortunately for the girls, sixteen of them still had both parents living. One had a stepfather, another a stepmother, and two lived with their widowed mothers. One father was a convicted thief who ran away after a warrant had been issued for his arrest, as he had raped his own daughter; and another virtually forced his daughter to run away, due to his abusive conduct.

CONCLUSION 

There would be no difficulty in presenting a fairly complete description of the sordidness and suffering to which many families are exposed due to overcrowding with children. The theme is an old one and has been dealt with by many competent observers. There are available several portrayals in which the hardships inflicted upon the proud or despairing parents of numerous children, and upon the innocent offspring as well, are fully depicted. 

While the individual suffering and degradation of the girls involved in sex experiences are sufficiently distressing, there arise other hazards which are ever present. Over these families there hangs the threat of possible death of the father, or the untimely de- 121

mise of the mother, with a brood of young children exposed to subsequent distress and serious adjustments. Propaganda in one form or another, whether desirable or otherwise, is reaching parents of large families. The community as a whole, functioning through teachers, social workers and other public minded persons must ever be on the alert to advise, protect and guide children of large families, for their problems are apt to be manifold. 

Concentration of service upon children of large families and low incomes, offers a possibility of much social good. Even if one be skeptical of the value of rendering assistance and advice to such families, there remains the menace to society of untended and footloose children. 122

CHAPTER V  INTRODUCTION 

UMAN conduct, in its various manifestations, HU 

effect of environment upon behavior. Sets of facts have been collated to prove various theories and interpretations of environmental influence. Whichever point of view or theory is accepted, an accurate and reasonable attitude warrants the assumption that surroundings and circumstances are always part of the situation involved in human conduct. 

This is the conviction of Dr. William Healy and others who have concerned themselves with the problem of delinquency. Upon conceding this point, however, one is immediately led to a further observation, which is that a similar environment may mean different things for different persons. 

This may be due in part to innate variations between individuals, as well as to previous experiences that have formed mental associations and have helped to fix points of view. 

Insofar as delinquency is concerned, environment may play a large part in some cases and a very small part in others. Competent observers, including Dr. Healy, have pointed out the fact that one child may be much dissatisfied in the very same school life which to another is tolerable and even pleasurable. This varied reaction to the similar school environment may be accounted for by special 123

biological characteristics, unused abilities, special mental interests and ambitions or even previously formed mental associations. The usual inference is that poor environment, such as is presented by particular family situations and the slum areas of our large cities, are the direct causes of juvenile delinquency in its several phases. Like most broad assumptions relative to cause and effect, wide latitude must be allowed for in this interpretation of social phenomena. Those long associated with children of degraded and neglected families and neighborhoods, are well aware of the fact that these children can be divided, on a conduct basis, into several categories. The same applies to their parents. 

There are at least two outstanding groups-those seemingly adjusted, and more or less satisfied to continue to live in the poorer sections and to bring up their children under physical and moral handicaps; and those families ever struggling to lift themselves out of the squalor and dirt, and to rise to higher economic and environmental standards of living. The probability is that the aspirations and struggles of the families in the second group lead the individual members to a finer outlook on life, and concomitantly, act as a buffer against some of the grosser and more debilitating phases of their environment. 

This is not an attempt to belittle environment as a factor in delinquency: Far too much is known of the destructive power latent in poor surroundings, sordid and overcrowded homes, early familiarity with the cruder aspects of sex, unavoidable 124

contact with vicious characters, lack of proper and supervised recreational facilities, and many other items in this vale of sorrows. An acceptance of these facts still leaves much to be said for the strength that lies in kind and judicious parental control and guidance, the influence of religion, the importance of properly adjusted school curricula, and the service of community leadership in lighting the way for young people who try to live the better life.

DESTRUCTIVE ENVIRONMENT AND SEX DELINQUENCY 

One phase of environment that seemingly has an important influence on sex delinquency is that of housing. The relationship of tenement house life to sex delinquency and activity among young children is of considerable importance. 

Poverty-stricken homes where children are crowded into bedrooms and even sleep in the same beds with their parents or other adults; parents of low standards and low mentality who make no effort to restrain their instinctive desires from the view of their offspring; boarders or male relatives who engage in sex play with young girls, and sometimes with boys in the families with whom they livethese and similar phenomena help to develop too great a familiarity with sex in the minds of many young children. The result is that an indifference or callousness to the true import of the sex instinct is engendered. Projection of the girl of the present who is exposed to such an environment to 125

the time when she herself may be a mother, is apt to lead her to become a pattern of immorality for her own daughter. Lack of proper orientation to sex life in its more idealistic aspects may result in untold harm to young people, while poor environment undoubtedly contributes to unsatisfactory adjustments in their sex life. This is one phase of slum conditions which might well be given serious consideration by municipal authorities. 

The following histories are typical of some results of inadequate environment, complicated undoubtedly by poor mental and moral stock in at least several of the cases. Quite naturally, questions will arise as to whether a given girl might have been spared the physical violence and shame if no male boarder had lived in her home; if the mother did not go out to work, leaving the young girl to her own resources in an undesirable neighborhood; if the mother herself had not been immoral and set her daughter a bad example-and a number of other matters of wide interest and importance in the prevention of unfortunate occurrences. It will be noted that the presumable causes of sex involvements ofttimes coincide with similar details as noted in other chapters. As already indicated, there are few clear-cut situations in many of the cases under consideration; most of them are complicated by various known and unknown factors. The stories cited below have been selected from a large number in which it appeared that environment was an outstanding, if 126

not the deciding factor, in the difficulties in which the girls came to find themselves. 

HELEN, at 14 years of age, was in the 6-B grade of public school, and lived with her parents in a three room flat on the lower East Side. She came to know 21 year old Albert by meeting him. on the street frequently, and believed him when he said that he had fallen in love with her. After they had known each other for a few weeks, he became a boarder in her already crowded home; he slept in the living room, the father in the kitchen, while Helen shared the bedroom with her mother. 

At the end of a week, and when Helen's parents were out, Albert told her of his sex activities with other girls, and suggested that Helen should have relations with him. She asked whether he wanted to marry her, and as he insisted that he did, she let him have intercourse with her. This was 

Helen's first sex experience, and when she went to school the next day she told one of her girl friends all about it.

Albert took advantage of her six times in a period of five months-three times while her parents were out, and three times when she went to his bed during the night. Her mother became suspicious of her conduct on one occasion and advised 

Helen to avoid any sex involvements. A quick denial of any serious interest in Albert followed. 

Within six months she began to tire of him, and once when he tried to have relations with her she cut him with a fruit knife. Helen's abdominal outline began to take on undue proportions, and 127

as her parents became aware of what had happened, they had Albert arrested. Helen offered to forgive him and pleaded that he should be discharged as he admitted everything and offered to marry her.

LAURA was 15 and only in the 6-B grade. Her father died when she was but two years old, leaving the widowed mother with six children-five girls and one boy. The burden soon became too great for the mother and she found it necessary to place the children in orphan asylums and to go out to work.

Ten years later, when Laura was 12, she and an older brother were returned to the home her mother had reestablished. As this arrangement lasted only a few weeks, Laura was placed in a boarding home and did not return to live with her mother until two years later. The mother had a two room flat and kept a boarder named 

Henry. He slept in one room with Laura's brother, while she and her mother occupied the other. A small restaurant was operated by the mother in the same building in which she lived. 

On one occasion, when Laura went to the sleeping quarters, Henry followed her and started to fool with her. He then forced her onto a bed and tried to rape her, but did not succeed as she fought him off and was able to get away. IMPORTANCE OF ENVIRONMENT 

In reaching an opinion as to the relative influence of environment upon character and conduct, the social philosopher must often turn to the biologist for a scientific opinion. The latter is enabled to carry out his experiments by using plants, insects and 151

animals of comparatively short life cycles, which make it feasible to study the due effects of both heredity and environment. Biology teaches that life is possible only within rather narrow limits of physical and chemical conditions and in the main, these limits are fixed by the constitution of nature. 

But apart from these antecedent conditions of life in general, there are many minor conditions of environment which exercise a profound influence upon organisms, especially in the course of their development. Very slight changes in food, temperature, moisture and atmospheric conditions, may produce great changes in the developing organism, and these conditions are for the most part entirely beyond the control of the individual affected. 

Of all living things, man enjoys the most extensive and the most varied environment, with correspondingly great effects upon his personality. Man, above all, has the longest period of immaturity, and it is during this period that the play of environmental stimuli on the organism is effective in modifying development. In addition to the material environment, he lives in the midst of intellectual, social and moral stimuli which are potent factors in his evolvement. The modifying influences of all these environmental conditions on personality are very great. According to Professor Conklin, the eminent biologist, so important is the power of environment, he lives in the midst of intellectual, that it may outweigh inheritance; a relatively poor inheritance with excellent environmental conditions often produces better results than a good inheritance with poor conditions. No sort of en- 152

vironment can do more than bring out the hereditary possibilities, but, on the other hand, those possibilities must remain latent and undeveloped unless they are stimulated into activity by the environment.

The influence of environment upon the minds and morals of men is especially great. To a large extent our habits, words, thoughts; our aspirations, ideals, satisfactions; our responsibility, morality and religion are the results of the environment and education of our early years. The surroundings and conditions of early life have a great influence in shaping personality, and are almost as much beyond the control of the individual as is heredity. 

HOME CONDITIONS AND CONDUCT DISORDERS 

Turning for a moment to the social philosopher, it is well to note that he, too, makes his contribution in the determination of the effects of environment upon the human being. While he cannot proceed in the same manner as the biologist does in carrying on his experiments he is able to read and interpret history, and to note the results of certain impacts upon the course of entire nations, and on given individuals as well. Currently, he can make repeated observations, gather morbidity and mortality statistics, court records, and the like, and divide them on the basis of city areas, family status, economic conditions, etc. Such results as can be obtained from carefully collated data of this kind, may, at least, indicate a definite trend upon which reconstruction of society can be attempted with some surety of attendant benefits. 153

An analysis of the case histories which have been presented in this chapter, lends added weight to the belief, both of the biologist and of the sociologist, that environment is of the utmost importance in the life of the growing individual. The stories of these 20 young girls, possibly at the most impressionable period of their lives, point out what happened to them in the environment in which they found themselves and which they could not readily alter of their own accord. In this series of sample histories, the fathers of 10 of the girls had died, while four mothers subsequently remarried; two mothers had died, and one father remarried; the parents of two girls were separated, while both parents of two girls were dead. With such histories of broken homes, abject poverty and the like, one would hardly come to expect uniformly normal conduct patterns by young girls bereft of needed guidance and protection. The story, however, is not complete. In the families where both parents were living, two families had male boarders in already crowded quarters; in another both father and mother were heavy drinkers and very abusive to their children; two other fathers were habitual drunkards; the mother of one girl was unfaithful to her husband; in another instance the parents lived in a distant state and left their young daughter in a home with a grandmother, seven cousins and two male boarders.

Of the widowed mothers, one was a prostitute and a heavy drinker, paying little attention to the welfare of her daughter, while two others were immoral and lived with men to whom they were not married. One mother who was 154

estranged from her husband lived with a lover, while her daughter was shunted around from one home to another. A young daughter of divorced parents lived with different relatives till she wound up with a cousin who was a prostitute.

CONCLUSION 

Other devastating environmental factors were met with, but the preceding are perhaps sufficient warrant for the conclusion that environment plays a very important part in the formation of personality and in the development of conduct disorders. 

Perhaps the grimness of the situation is relieved somewhat by consideration of the fact that, were it not for immoral stepfathers, two of the girls probably would not have been raped; that better economic conditions would have made it unnecessary for six of the families to share their already limited quarters with one or more male boarders. 

Likewise, better financial circumstances would have made it possible for widowed mothers to remain at home and guide and protect their daughters, instead of having to go out to work, with their children left on the streets. While the misfortune of being motherless or fatherless may make children in any social stratum more liable to become involved in difficulties, the loss of either parent is much more serious for the children of the poor. 

Their susceptibility and exposure are much greater. 

It would be the part of social wisdom to utilize the necessary measures to protect and serve such children long before they become community problems. 155

CHAPTER VI  

TH 

INTRODUCTION 

HE violation of young girls is one of the crimes upon which society has looked with particular repugnance. When proved, little sympathy is wasted upon the culprit, especially when he is an adult and in full possession of his senses to realize the significance of his act. It is important to remember that in every community, whether large or small, there are many males who are sex starved, if that term is appropriate. They are frequently men who are unmarried, with no permanent home ties, of inferior mentality or mentally ailing, and often with few, if any, moral scruples. These are the persons who usually make up a goodly portion of the so-called degenerates. There is no one remedy which can be applied to protect young children, either girls or boys, from the pernicious activities of such individuals. Careful parental oversight, properly supervised community play spaces, sex education, removal of slum areas and closer police surveillance of known haunts of such characters, are some of the measures which are patently of service.

METHODS OF CONTROL 

Such procedures and others of similar scope may be of some value insofar as anti-social characters 

But the problem is not as simple are concerned. 156

as all that, presuming that simplicity is indicated in dealing with these so-called moral imbeciles. 

The adolescent boy, for instance, who has not received any sex instruction and adequate moral and religious training and who perhaps, has his own sex feelings prematurely and abnormally aroused, may seek a way out of his emotional state by attempting to rape some young girl. Poor home environment, membership in unsupervised groups or gangs of boys, display of posters, magazines and motion pictures which arouse sex interest, and other easily recognized factors, raise questions which press for an answer if the community is to provide adequately for the protection of young and defenseless girls. 

The adult male relative who is accepted as a boarder in a family with young girls; the stepfather who casts longing eyes upon the adolescent girl who has become his stepdaughter and over whom he has acquired parental responsibility and control; the male boarder whose antecedents and personal traits are unknown; janitorial assistants of low calibre in apartment houses and in schools-these and others add to the category of individuals who are potential hazards to the health and morals of young children.

Recognition of these facts and the preparation of a proper mental attitude towards a mitigation of the problems presented, would go a long way toward reducing the incidence of cases such as are cited in the following pages. Unless parents and teachers are aroused to a full understanding of these matters; unless they and various groups of com- 157

munity leaders, clergymen, social workers, and others, openly and frankly face the disagreeable facts and instances and plan accordingly, little abatement can be looked for. Children are frequently too young to understand and defend themselves against the malign individual who offers them a stick of candy, or ice cream, or admission to a cheap motion picture theatre, in order that he may throw them off their guard by the presumed personal interest and supposed kindness. Their protection can come primarily from united efforts by enlightened parents, informed and active school teachers and community workers. If one were to take stock, as it were, of the many individuals who compose the anti-social groups in a community, the total would indeed be large. In addition to those previously convicted of various crimes against persons, it should be necessary to include many who have not fallen into the hands of the law, the mentally retarded and mentally ailing though sexually active males, and others whose mental and emotional states are such that they constitute potential hazards to young girls. The comparatively large number of sexually mature and unmarried males to be found in virtually every community, likewise presents a problem which requires coordinated planning and even fundamental social and economic changes. In the meantime, a percentage of such men will always be open to suspicion as a possible peril to the physical and moral well-being of young girls.

The cases that follow are indicative of some of 158

the situations that arose, with often serious consequences to girls of tender years. Remedial community measures are in some instances quite obvious. Their initiation will require, above all, an aroused public interest focussed on the protection of the health and safety of children. 

GERTRUDE was 11 years old and in the 5-B grade in public school. She lived with her parents and a 16 year old sister. 

Her father was a laborer and her mother worked from 7 A. M. to 5 P. M. picking and assorting rags, while the sister was a factory worker. Gertrude was left home alone all day, to clean the flat, prepare her own lunch, and spend her spare time as best she could. Usually this meant attending a cheap motion picture show on the Bowery whenever she had the necessary few cents to purchase a ticket.

Through her friend Helen she came to know 

Tony, a 50 year old carpenter who lived alone in two rooms. Her friend suggested that if Gertrude would go to his room and let him do something to her she would be given $2.00. She refused as she suspected that it was wrong to let a man do certain things to her. Some weeks later she met Tony in a Bowery motion picture theatre and he invited. 

Gertrude and a young girl friend to his rooms to have some refreshments. On the way, Tony met his friend Jim and suggested that he should come along. When they reached the flat, Jim attempted intercourse with Gertrude and Tony with the other young girl. Both children were given a dollar 159

each, which they spent for candy and admission to a motion picture show.

A month later, Gertrude and Helen again met Tony in the same motion picture theatre. He bought candy for them and suggested that they come to his rooms after the show. Once there, he asked that they let him "do it" and proceeded to attempt relations first with Helen and then with 

Gertrude, while each girl observed the other. Tony gave them 50 cents which they later spent for candy and ice cream. These were the only times that 

Gertrude went to his flat, but she learned that her friend visited there often and that other young girls would let Tony do things to them. 

ELIZABETH, at 12 years, was only in the 4-B grade. Her father died when she was an infant and she had no brothers or sisters. The mother went out to do housework, leaving home at 7 A. M. and returning between 6 P. M. and 9 P. M., thereby supporting herself and her daughter. Elizabeth was left alone all day, except for the time spent in school. On holidays she had all day to herself, with little, if any, supervision exercised over her behavior.

The first time Elizabeth had any sex experience was at the home of a neighbor; three boys 11, 13 and 15 years old, and two girl friends of her own age were likewise involved. The boys attempted intercourse and indulged in sex play with the three girls, who were sent home without the money which had been promised to them. Her second experience took place one afternoon when a middle-aged 160

man living in the same building asked Elizabeth to come to his flat and to let him "do it" to her. He gave her 25 cents, and the following day she went again, and received a similar sum after the man attempted intercourse. 

Two weeks later another man tried to have relations with her. She had gone to his flat for a drink of water and when he asked to be allowed to play with her, she consented and he attempted relations, after which he gave her 25 cents. The next day she returned of her own accord in order to collect another quarter.

Mr. H., who resided in the same building with 

Elizabeth, asked her to go to the cellar with him to get some wood for her own use. There he attempted to assault the young girl, but was caught in the act when another neighbor came on the scene. Her mother was advised of this incident and she administered a severe beating to her daughter. 

No other corrective measures were applied by the hardworking and poorly informed woman. 

HELEN was 12 years old, in the 5-A grade and lived with her married cousin, Mrs. H., and her husband; in addition, a married couple lived with the family. Helen had been shifted about among various relatives as her parents were dead. When she was 10 years old her home was with an aunt who, on one occasion, sent her to deliver a newspaper to a man in the same building. When Helen came into the room Mr. S. put his arms around her, disarranged her clothes and tried to have intercourse with her. Her aunt, who was waiting for 161

Helen, finally became anxious that something might be wrong and went to determine why the young girl was being detained. She found the door locked, and, after causing some commotion, was finally admitted. The aunt immediately suspected what had occurred and threatened to have Mr. S. arrested if he had done Helen any harm.

A year later Mr. G., 22 years old, who was a friend of Helen's aunt, invited the young girl to his furnished room and asked her to permit him to do something to her. He tried to have intercourse but was unsuccessful. Within a month he again invited her to his quarters and this time he consummated the act. Helen's next sex experience was with a stranger who engaged her in conversation while she stood on the stoop of the house in which she lived. He asked her to go to his flat as he wanted to give her something, but Helen refused because she had to care for her little cousin. The following day he again approached her with the same proposal, to which she agreed. He locked the door as soon as they entered and served Helen with some nuts. At the same time he asked her to permit sex liberties in return for the present of a few nuts. She consented and he attempted intercourse, after which he gave her 25 cents. 

A few months thereafter Helen began to miss her periods. She asked her cousin what might be the cause and, after considerable questioning, related the story of her several sex experiences. 

GRACE was 12 but only in the 4-A grade in public school.

She lived with her mother, a 10 162

year old sister and a brother one year old. The father deserted the family after the birth of the baby and failed to support them. This made it necessary for the mother and the three children to live in one furnished room. Their sole income was provided by Grace's mother, who went out to work as a charwoman. She left at 5 A.M. and did not return till 7 P.M., with even longer hours on several evenings a week. Grace accompanied her mother on two mornings a week to assist her in cleaning a dance hall, after which she would hurry to get to school in time for the nine o'clock class. The landlady of the boarding house cared for the baby part of the day, and the two girls looked after him when school was over. Grace and her sister were daily exposed to undesirable influences and individuals, and the use they made of their free time was uncontrolled. 

Grace had her first sex experience with a 17 year old boy who lived across the street. He asked her to go to his basement home but she refused, as her mother had warned her against companionship with boys and men. A few days later, he prevailed upon Grace to accompany him, on some pretext, to an empty apartment, and when there he raped her.

Her next experience was a similar one in which a man had forcible relations with her. He was 32 years old and assisted the janitor in the building in which Grace lived. He entered Grace's room when she was home alone, pretending that he was looking for a bag of tools and, before she could 163

defend herself, he threw her on the bed and had relations with her. The next day Grace told the janitor that his assistant was annoying her and he in turn discharged him. She did not see this man again, nor did she tell her mother of her sex contact with him.

SOPHIE was 11 years old and in the 5-B grade. 

She lived with her widowed mother, a married sister aged 29 and her son of 11, and another sister 23 years of age. The married sister was estranged from her husband Harold, though he came to visit her occasionally and remained on friendly terms with the other members of the family. Sophie was out playing on the street one afternoon when Harold came along and said he had a gift for her; also, that he wanted to take her to the theatre. Not suspecting any evil intent, she accompanied him to the furnished room in which he lived. He then stated that he had worked all night and was tired and sleepy, proceeding at the same time to remove his outer clothing. When 

Sophie asked him about the present he had promised, he told her to undress completely, as the gift was a shirt and a pair of bloomers. As she refused, he pulled all her clothes off, and throwing her on the bed, attempted intercourse despite her struggles and cries. On the pretext that she wanted to go to the hall bathroom, he let her dress, after which she ran home. Sophie told her mother she had visited Harold and started to cry; then the whole story was soon elicited. 

This was not the only time that Harold had done 164

something to her. When she was but nine years old and her sister lived with him in their own apartment, he attempted relations with Sophie when no one else was home. A year later she spent a weekend with her sister and Harold. He came into the young girl's bed while his wife was out shopping and engaged in sex play with Sophie as an unwilling victim.

MARGARET was nine years old and lived with her parents, two younger brothers and a baby sister. Margaret ran away from home with two other young girls because they remained out till midnight and were afraid to return as each expected severe punishment for disobeying the orders of their parents. They spent the night in a hallway. The following morning the girls stood outside of a cheap motion picture theatre until a man asked them if they wanted to see the show. As they answered in the affirmative, he paid their way in and bought them refreshments. 

During the performance he attempted sex play with Margaret and on leaving the theatre he suggested that the girls should go along with him. 166

He took Margaret and one of the other girls into a tenement house toilet, locked the door, and attempted intercourse with both girls, after which he gave them 50 cents each. The three girls spent the balance of the day and evening on the street, and still fearing to return home, they slept that night in a hallway.

The second day the girls begged for food, played in a local park and again slept in the rear hall of a tenement. On the third day, as they were still afraid to go home, and being hungry and without any money, Margaret and one of the other girls went to see a shoemaker in their neighborhood who had done "bad things" to Margaret and her friend on a number of occasions. They thought that if they permitted him to do "bad things" to them again, he would give them money. The girls guessed correctly, for after attempting intercourse with the two girls, the shoemaker gave them 15 cents each, which they quickly spent for candy. 

The third night they again tried to sleep in a hallway, but they were discovered at 3 A.M. and were finally taken to their homes. 

BERTHA was only eight and lived with her mother, whose cousin George, aged 27, occasionally took Bertha to the movies and bought candy for her. 

He lived with a married aunt at whose home Bertha was a frequent visitor as the aunt had a daughter of about her niece's age. When Bertha came to spend a week-end with her cousin Helen,

HENRIETTA was eight years old and in the 3-B grade. She lived with her widowed mother and a younger sister; Mr. John H., a widower, occupied two rooms in their apartment. The mother went out to work and the young girls were left virtually to their own resources. When Henrietta was only six years old, Mr. H. asked her to 168

come to his home presumably to help him carry a few packages. His wife had died and he was arranging to share the apartment with Henrietta's mother and her children. On this occasion he attempted to have the young child participate in a sex act, but she refused. He threatened to beat her if she told her mother about the incident.

After Mr. H. had been a boarder in Henrietta's home for a little while, he became more persistent in his demands that she should permit him to take certain liberties with her. He finally forced her to do so, after which the child's resistance was broken and the act was repeated many times, usually at about 6 P. M. when he came home from work, while Henrietta's mother did not return till 

7 P. M. In addition, he compelled Henrietta's younger sister to witness the performance at first, and later to become an active participant. Not content with embroiling two children, he had the girls invite a neighbor's child to the apartment and likewise initiated her into sex practices with him. 

The young sisters were very much afraid of Mr. H. and did not tell their mother of his misconduct. 

Henrietta, young as she was, finally rebelled against the outrageous treatment to which she and her sister had been subjected by the boarder for a long time.

She told a neighbor about his behavior, and through the help of this woman, the mother was made aware of the facts and took immediate action.

ANGELINA, at 11 years, was in the 5-A grade. 

She lived with her mother and stepfather, a brother 169

years old, a stepbrother of like age and her grandmother. The mother had married her present husband when Angelina was eight years old, after which both children were taken from an orphan asylum where they had been the preceding four years. During the first months in the new home Angelina slept in the same bed with her mother and stepfather.

The stepfather attempted relations with Angelina shortly after she rejoined her parent. Early one evening while the mother was out to attend to some shopping, the stepfather commanded Angelina to undress and go to bed. As soon as she had done so he removed his clothes and, despite her protestations, attempted intercourse with the young girl. 

He then ordered her not to tell anyone, threatening severe physical punishment if she did, and held forth the promise of a bus ride on the following day.

When the mother returned she noticed the disarranged bedclothes but said nothing, as she stood in considerable fear of her newly acquired spouse. 

Angelina, then only eight years old, did not tell her mother anything, partly because of the shock to which she had been subjected and largely because of the threats of the stepfather. Thereafter, he attempted relations with Angelina on an average of once a week for several months, sometimes when the mother was out or even when she was in another room in the same apartment. 

The stepfather was arrested for a felony and was sent to jail for a year. Upon his discharge, he resumed his former practice of periodically assault- 170

ing Angelina. She came to believe her mother knew all about the indignities to which she was being subjected, but feared her husband too much to protest or take any action against him. When the child was 11 years old, and had been mistreated for three years, she was seen crying by her grandmother only a few minutes after having been compelled to have relations with the stepfather. 

On being questioned she told her the entire horrible story, with the result that immediate action was taken by the grandmother to save Angelina from any further exposure to such treatment. Incidentally, the grandmother learned for the first time that her grandson, the brother of Angelina, had been made the victim of unnatural practices by the stepfather over a considerable period of time.

HELEN was 11 years old, in the 6-A grade, and lived with her father and mother in a fairly good neighborhood. The man who attempted to rape her was married and had three children of his own.

A week later Helen was again sent to do some marketing in the fruit store and, dropping a coin which rolled toward the rear of the store, she started to hunt for it. MILDRED, at 11 years, was in the 5-A grade, and lived with her widowed mother, a sister of 20, and two brothers 18 and seven years of age. man who attempted relations with Mildred was known to her mother for a number of years as a barber in a local shop. The young girl met him on the street one day walking with two other men.

When he saw she was alone he left his companions and, taking Mildred by the hand, led her into a cellar a few doors from her home. He ordered her to take off her clothes and, while she struggled to get away, tore her dress in trying to disrobe her.

After that he removed her underclothes and attempted intercourse. On coming home Mildred immediately told her mother what a supposed friend of the family had done. For some time this man had been saying "bad words" to her and would tell 

Mildred to go home and repeat them to her mother.

She once went to the principal of the school which Mildred attended for advice as to what she could do to keep this man from speaking to her young daughter as he did. The attempt at rape occurred shortly thereafter.

MARY, eight years old, was in the 3-B grade and lived with her mother, a sister of nine and a brother seven years old. VIOLATION OF YOUNG GIRLS 

Young children who are uninformed on sex matters, and come in contact with vicious characters, either in their own homes or elsewhere, are 180

especially prone to become victims of sex assaults. 

Classifying those under 13 years of age as belonging to this group, it was found that 20 per cent of the cases studied were between the ages of four and 12 years inclusive. In considering the histories and experiences of these children, several factors come to the fore, such as broken homes, poor environment, mental dullness, lack of proper sex education, economic insecurity of the families, and several others matters, some of which have been stressed previously.

The story of the home life of the 20 girls whose experiences have just been related, is filled with sorrow and distress. The fathers of eight of the girls had died, while one mother had remarried; one girl had lost both her parents by death; and in two families the parents were separated. To these must be added the findings that five of the mothers went out working, leaving their young children without adequate supervision; one mother was chronically ill; and the time and efforts of two mothers, one a widow and the other estranged from her husband, were expended in keeping house for boarders, in addition to looking after their own children. The family situations unearthed do not vary greatly from those in the lives of other girls whose stories have been considered in previous chapters. An analysis of the individuals guilty of assaulting these children may throw some light upon the measures that should be adopted to protect other defenseless and naive young girls, whose mental and physical 181

health may be ruined by such outrageous treatment as is here depicted.

THE MEN INVOLVED 

In only two instances were young boys involved in the cases under consideration while, even in these instances, men 32 and 50 years of age subsequently assaulted the two girls. The ages of the men implicated in these cases ranged between 22 and 50 years, though most of them were over 40 years of age. An analysis of their relationship to the girls indicates that one was the stepfather; two were other immediate relatives, including a brother-inlaw and a cousin; two were janitors; three were boarders in the girls' families; three were supposed friends of the families; four were neighbors; and five were strangers who made advances to the children. Three of the girls were assaulted or mistreated by strangers in neighborhood motion picture theatres. This division is generally representative of the whole group of young children who were assaulted, and suggestions for the eradication of such a situation as depicted would, on the whole, be effective for the amelioration of the lot of youngsters who are assaulted in many communities in the country.

CONCLUSION 

It is somewhat difficult to attempt to devise a plan for the protection of young girls from sex assaults because of the many factors involved. 

There is the matter of exposure of such children in 182

their own homes to stepfathers, boarders, family friends and the like. Mothers or parents of young girls often do not pay enough attention to the conduct and habits of their children. In certain situations, of course, such as in instances already cited, solicitousness on the part of mothers would often not have availed. One thing should be kept in mind. 

Mothers and fathers of young girls must be on the alert for any unusual attention paid their young daughters by male relatives, boarders, friends, neighbors and strangers. Many parents are largely unconcerned as to the spare time activities of their children; what they do when away from home; with whom they talk and walk and play, either in the streets, in motion picture theatres, in public parks, or even in their own homes. A more questioning and critical attitude would do much to ward off any contemplated mistreatment or assault, even though such an attitude did not partake, as it should not, of constant and harassing suspicion without due cause.

Children should not be permitted to attend motion picture shows with strangers, or be allowed by attendants and ticket sellers to stand in front of such theatres and solicit men to purchase tickets for them. This is a nefarious practice which is all too common in poorer neighborhoods, and unless the police and licensing authorities constantly press for the elimination of such a procedure, little will be availed. It is, of course, within the province of parents to warn their children, for school principals and teachers to point out the evil effects 183

and dangers, and for community workers to wage a consistent campaign against allowing this practice to continue. The cooperation of all is desirable and necessary. The question as to the moral and other effects of many motion pictures upon the conduct and personality of youngsters who view them is left out of the present consideration. 

Little progress can be achieved until parents and teachers become more fully cognizant of the moral and physical hazards to which young children are exposed, especially in broken and degraded homes. 

Vicious, unstable, mentally retarded and diseased males will continue to prey upon little girls unless public opinion is aroused to the point of conducting a continuous campaign for the control of wellrecognized community situations. Education of parents, enlightenment of teachers and community leaders, and the development of an effective social hygiene program are measures which should help to meet the problems depicted. 184

CHAPTER VII  INTRODUCTION 

CHOLARS who have carried on extensive in- SCH Tovestigations into the origin of social institutions, have discovered differing attitudes among early and primitive peoples relating to the matter of incest. Turning to that great source book of human experience, the Bible, one finds in the 18th chapter of Leviticus excoriation of the man "who shall approach unto any that is near of kin to him." 

This social code has prevailed in the western world, at least, for many centuries. Among some ancient nations, however, there was no prohibition of such matings. In historic Egypt, marriage of brother and sister, and mating of father and daughter, were apparently not deemed incestuous. day, several states in the United States forbid first cousins to marry, and participants in such a wedlock are liable to both fine and punishment. Agitation has been carried on in certain quarters to make such marriage prohibition uniform throughout the United States, on the assumption, of course, that unions between those of close blood relationship are racially undesirable and hazardous for the well-being of future offspring. This has been an open question for some time, with the weight of scientific evidence currently on the side of those who hold that these marriages are not necessarily 185

detrimental to the race, provided the stock on both sides is normal.

It would take one too far afield into various phases of anthropology, biology, psychology and other branches of science, to attempt an adequate discussion of the question of incest, with a few aspects of which this chapter is concerned. Further, the matters dealt with in the following pages offer concrete evidence of forms of incest which have been outlawed by all civilized peoples for many centuries. Whether or not the cases to be discussed are representative of pathological mentalities in fathers and brothers-to select only those instances which are generally looked upon as definite cases of incest, and leaving out of the discussion uncle-niece, cousin-cousin and similar relationships-the fact remains that many young girls have been and continue to be subjected to violation by those upon whom has rested the highest degree of responsibility for their protection and care. Perhaps the present cursory delineation may throw enough light upon the problem, to lead at least to some diminution in the number of young girls who are made the subjects of illicit passions.

COMMUNITY ASPECTS OF INCEST 

There have been various theories to account for the sex attraction of a father for his daughter, or of a brother for his sister, and vice versa. Psychoanalysis has of recent years attempted an explanation of this phenomenon through an elaboration of the theory of the Oedipus complex. While the 186

father who has what he believes to be an overpowering urge to have incestuous relations with his daughter may possibly have this motivation fully explained by the skillful psychoanalyst, and further, may be brought to realize the import of this psychological phenomenon and led to desist from it, nevertheless the problem is ever with us. 

It may be all well and good for the person of understanding and means to seek the therapy which psychoanalysis can offer him, but the community need probably be little concerned with fathers and brothers in this category. Rather, the problem centers about the man who, under the influence and stimulation of alcohol, seizes upon his young daughter as a readily accessible female to give vent to his overstimulated senses; the passionate father whose wife is ailing, or is in a hospital, or perhaps is dead, seeking through the person of his daughter satisfaction for a normal urge which is deflected into an abnormal channel; the adolescent brother who foists his early sex desires upon a younger sister; and like instances as they appear in the following pages.

The question whether or not solutions are available for some of these problems may not be beside the point, but the development and elaboration of such methods of control may involve a wide reordering of many social procedures. Mention has previously been made of the need for a broader understanding and dissemination of facts relating to the sex life of the human being. Until various inhibitions and extreme degrees of prudishness are 187

removed, they will surely remain as stumbling blocks in the effort to give girls, and boys as well, a wholesome conception of sex matters, taught under proper auspices by those with adequate preparation and understanding. All too frequently does one find girls who do not comprehend the import of the sex advances of father or brother.

Properly taught, and if one will, warned as well, many girls would learn how to avoid and, if necessary, defend themselves against sex suggestions and assaults.

The effect of low mentality and its concomitants in the guise of superstition, cruelty, lack of social sense, etc., have been recognized as important factors in incest. Due to the nature and limitations of the data available, it would be difficult, if not impossible, to delineate the character and mentality of the males involved in the following instances of incest. Perhaps sufficient evidence is presented to warrant the drawing of certain conclusions as to the mental and emotional make-up of the fathers and brothers involved in these antisocial situations. 

HAZEL was 13 years old and in the 7-B grade. 

Her school career had been somewhat disrupted, as she first attended classes in a small Pennsylvania town where she made a good record. Upon transfer to a New York school she did not do so well, being left back, tentatively promoted, and returned to a lower grade in the middle of the school term. 

She lived with her father and stepmother, and had two brothers, 22 and 16 years of age, who resided with relatives. Hazel was six years old when her 188

mother died; her father remarried and his second wife died when Hazel was eleven. A few months after her demise, Hazel's father took a third wife.

This spouse left for a visit to Europe about the time that Hazel's difficulties came to a head.

Due to the irregular home life, and her dissatisfaction with school because of inability to keep up with her grade, Hazel ran away. Prior to this she had absented herself from school for a number of days without telling her father. She left home late at night by climbing down the fire-escape so as not to arouse him, and went to a furnished rooming house half a mile away. As Hazel had only $2.00, she promised the landlord she would pay him the full week's rental on the following day. In the morning she went to the chain grocery where she usually did the family marketing. She had occasionally borrowed small sums of money from the manager of this store. At this time she was $3.00 in debt to him, and he refused to give Hazel more money. However, he made her the proposition that if she would have intercourse with him he would cancel the debt and give her $10.00 in addition, but she rejected the dishonorable offer.

Hazel then went in search of a job and obtained work by misrepresenting her age. Her evenings were spent on Riverside Drive flirting with sailors and making the acquaintance of girls of questionable character. After some days she picked up a sailor, dined with him till a late hour, and permitted him to come to her furnished room. The father had been seeking Hazel and had reported her 189

disappearance to the police; somehow he obtained her address, for he appeared just in time to send the sailor away and to take his daughter home.

Edward, the brother who was three years her senior, was the first to have relations with her. 

They did not get along well, for he started sex activities with her when she was quite young, and continued this practice for several years. Hazel did not realize that she was doing the wrong thing by letting Edward "play" with her, until she was given some sex instruction by a married woman. 

She was afraid of her brother as he beat her whenever she attempted to resist his advances; however, she never told the father or either of her stepmothers, whose confidences she did not invite. 

When Hazel was 12 years old, the wife of the minister of her church arranged for her to live with a respectable family in a small country town. 

There she attended school and was well treated and clothed, in return for which she did occasional errands for the disabled foster mother. While there she met Newton, a 16 year old high school boy, and became friendly with him. During a school lunch period within a month after her arrival, he asked

Hazel to have relations with him. She consented, as she liked him and did not yet understand the full implications of his request and subsequent conduct.

They repeated the act on several occasions, until she learned that Newton was doing the same thing to other girls as well. Thereafter she refused to yield to his suggestions any more. At the end of 190

five months Hazel returned home to her father and stepmother.

A number of boys and some of the sailors Hazel had met when she ran away suggested that she should have relations with them, but their offers of money in return for sex favors were rejected. 

HELEN was 16 and had been graduated from public school. She lived with her father; the mother had eloped ten years previously with a neighbor. Helen's father was the one who initiated her into sex experiences, as she was raped by him when 15 years old. He had made many advances to her previously and, whenever he came home drunk, he would beat her because she resisted his attempts to indulge in sex contact. The first assault was followed by three other instances, usually when he was under the influence of alcohol.

Helen more or less regularly without objection and seemingly with the tacit approval of her father.

He also maltreated her when she failed to bring some of her young friends to the house. From the time she was 10 years old, her father indulged in sex activities with her friends, usually giving them $1.00 every time they permitted him to "do it."

A serious situation developed for Helen when she brought 13 year old Frances to her home for dinner.

The matter became rather involved for the father as, during drunken sprees, he had boasted to friends and fellow workers that he was indulging in relations with his own daughter. 

DOROTHY was eight years old and in the 3-B grade. Her parents were living and she had a sister 17 and a brother 16 years of age. MARY was 15, had attended high school for one term, and lived with her parents, a grandfather and two younger brothers. The father was a clerk and the mother helped to augment the family income by working in the evening as a saleswoman.

Some weeks later, the mother became worried about her daughter's abdominal appearance, and took her to another physician. He readily made a diagnosis of pregnancy. The young girl was then questioned further, and refused to involve her father until she was threatened with arrest.

This made. it necessary for Sarah to remain alone at home the greater part of the night.

ROSALIE was 12 and lived with her parents, an 18 year old sister and an 11 year old brother.

VIOLET was 14 and lived with her parents, six brothers 21, 13, eight, five, two years old, an infant brother of three months, and a 20 year old sister. Her father was very cruel to his children, frequently horsewhipping them on the least provocation and abusing them in other ways as well.

She had no definite plans as to where she would seek shelter, and spent a week riding in the subway at night or sleeping in hallways, and subsisting on an occasional sandwich and cup of coffee. During her wanderings about the city she was approached by several young men with various presumably proper and also improper proposals. Violet turned all of them down, except that of William, whom she met twice and who had invited her to attend a party at his home. He lived with a brother and their paralyzed father. After the party it was suggested to Violet that she might remain overnight if she desired. The invitation was accepted. In the early hours of the morning William came to her bed and forced her to have intercourse with him. 

Despite his conduct, she passed the next day with him as she feared to go home and did not know where to turn for help. Some of the boys who had been at the party returned the next evening, and when she told them her story, they advised her to go home as otherwise she might wind up on the streets. They were apparently well-intentioned young fellows, for they finally prevailed upon her to rejoin her parents and hired a taxicab to take her to them. 

Violet's brother and William were the only ones to have forced their attentions upon her. 202

CATHERINE was 15, in an opportunity class in public school, and lived with her parents and a 10 year old brother. The father always mistreated her. When Catherine was but four years old he kicked her in the abdomen, as a result of which she suffered severe pains for some years. He beat her frequently with a strap and once struck her on the nose with a hammer, causing a fracture and subsequent disfigurement. The mother also struck her frequently and showed no restraint in dealing with the sickly brother. Neighbors often asked Catherine why she made so much noise and she told them of the beatings to which her drunken parents subjected her. 

When Catherine was 12 years old she awoke one night to find her father lying on top of her, and on seeing that she was aroused, he threatened her with a severe beating unless she kept quiet. He raped her on that occasion, and before he left his daughter's bed warned her not to tell anyone, as otherwise he would kill her. He often repeated the incestuous relations and advised her each time to keep the matter a secret. On learning that her menstrual periods had begun and fearing that she might become pregnant, he did not attempt intercourse again. After the last assault Catherine told her mother, but while the latter was indignant and threatened to have her spouse arrested, she did nothing about it except to upbraid him. The following day Catherine's father held an axe over her head and vouched he would kill her unless she denied that he had assaulted her. Whenever the 203 !

mother was intoxicated, she would accuse her husband of raping his own daughter. 

Matters at home showed no improvement after the father stopped his sexual assaults, for both parents continued to beat Catherine. She finally asked the advice of a girl friend, who suggested that Catherine should live in a girls' home and offered to take her to such a place in the neighborhood. Catherine told her story and exhibited a badly bruised body to the woman in charge and the necessary legal action was taken. 

JOSEPHINE was only seven when her father began to perpetrate incestuous attacks upon her. 

At that time her mother had found it necessary to go to a hospital for an operation, and it was while she was away that the husband, with only his five year old son and seven year old daughter at home, initiated a series of assaults upon his own daughter. He was a lazy and worthless type of person, and became drunk as often as he could obtain enough money with which to purchase whiskey. As a result, his ailing wife was obliged to work as a charwoman in two office buildingscleaning one during the early hours of the morning, and the other after office hours until late in the evening.

On a morning two years after the initial assault, and when his wife was away working, he came into Josephine's bed and indulged in sex play. Later, he attempted intercourse despite the severe pain he caused his nine year old daughter. Even this did not suffice to satisfy his depraved desires, for 1 204

he then proceeded to compel her to submit to unnatural practices. This conduct was repeated on many subsequent mornings while his wife was scrubbing and cleaning in order to earn enough to barely maintain the family. The serious situation finally came to a close when Josephine went to her minister and told him what her father had been doing to her. As a result, the mother was informed, and when she upbraided him and told him that the nefarious conduct had been uncovered through confession of his daughter, he deserted his family. Josephine's mother thereafter moved out of the neighborhood with the hope that her husband would not be able to locate her and the children. 

HELEN was 15 years old and in the second term of high school. Her father had deserted his wife and daughter and had failed to provide for them for a number of years. When Helen was 

14 her mother sent her to the father in New York so that he might be responsible for the support and education of his daughter. All went well for about six months. He maintained an apartment 205

One night while she was asleep he came into her bed and despite her cries and struggles, he raped her; at the same time he warned her to keep silent for fear of arousing the neighbors. 

A threat of a severe beating if anyone ever learned about his conduct followed. Thereafter he forced

This incestuous man posed as a physician though he displayed no sign or other identification of his right to practice medicine. Actually, he was an illegal practitioner and an abortionist, and boasted to his daughter that some days he earned as much as $500.00. Despite this vigilance she became acquainted with Mrs. F., and complained to her of the mistreatment and incestuous relations perpetrated by her father. Through this woman Helen was placed in a proper boarding home, and legal steps were taken against her father. 

ELIZABETH, who was nine and an only child, lost her mother when she was but one year old, after which she spent much time with her aunt and uncle. On special occasions she lived in her 206

father's household, which included, besides himself, Elizabeth's parental grandparents, two uncles, an aunt and her husband. ADELINE was 14, in the 7-B grade, and lived with her parents, an 11 year old sister, and three brothers 16, four and two years of age. 

Her father often abused and beat his wife and children. His daughters had a particularly severe time, for he raped both of them and continued the incestuous relations for several years. AGNES, 15 years old, was in the 7-B grade, and lived with her mother, an illegitimate half-brother of 10, and a similarly illegitimate half-sister six years old. Her parents were estranged when Agnes was three, the father taking two older boys and an older daughter to live with him. the brother had been doing to his own sister. She thereupon took legal action against him. 

NELLIE, at 14 years, was in the second year of high school, and lived with her parents and three brothers 28, 13 and seven years of age. Her parents beat her frequently for no apparent reason, and also permitted her oldest brother George to strike her. 

When Nellie was only six her brother George, then 20, came into her bed with incestuous intent.

During succeeding years he tried intercourse whenever he found his sister home alone. Once, when

He paid slight attention to her complaints and took no action to protect her against carnal abuse by his son. A week later she told her mother the same story, and while she threatened to put George out of the house, she, too, did nothing about the serious situation. 

As Nellie could not stop the incestuous attacks by her brother and as she received no consideration 210

or protection from her own parents, she finally ran away. The wife of a local physician befriended the young girl and offered her a good home.

DOROTHY was 15 and had reached the 8-B grade. She lived with her father and stepmother, and had an older brother who was a sailor and was often away from home. When she was 12 years old, her brother returned for a visit, and finding Dorothy home alone, told her to accompany him to a bedroom. Pulling off her clothes despite protests and struggles, he raped her. Before leaving, he warned her not to tell anyone and gave her some money as an inducement to keep quiet. That was the only time he did anything to her.
