Being without extra clothes and with very little money, she telephoned for assistance to a man who was an owner of the building in which her family lived, and whom she often accompanied on automobile rides. No intimacies took place between them till three months before Dorothy ran away from home. Following his suggestion, she permitted him to have intercourse each time they went riding, and would usually receive $5.00 for her favors. 

Dorothy rented a furnished room with the $10.00 she obtained from her friend and after a week met Mr. and Mrs. R. to whom she confided that she was a runaway. They offered her a home and some pay for her assistance with the housework. 211

Within a month the man of the house forced Dorothy to have relations with him while his wife was out shopping; a male roomer in the apartment accidentally witnessed the assault. Tiring of the housework and quarreling with Mr. R., she finally left. The same day she communicated with the male roomer, who likewise had moved out, and when she told him of her need of a home, he offered to rent a room for both of them as husband and wife. Dorothy agreed to this plan and lived with him for a month, during which interval they frequently had intercourse. At the end of that period she was found by relatives and was taken to their home.

AGNES was 15, in the 7-B grade, and lived with her parents and a sister seven years of age. 

She ran away from home, without extra clothing or money, because her father repeatedly sought to have intercourse with her. He tried to rape his daughter when she was only twelve. However, she succeeded in escaping from him and remained on the street till her mother returned from work. 

Agnes did not tell her about the incestuous attempt of the father. She was ashamed to speak of such matters and was afraid of being beaten by him if she complained. Following the first attempt, he often came to the bed of his daughter and engaged in sex play, despite her efforts to make him desist. 

Finally, when Agnes was 14 and could no longer tolerate the actions of the dissolute parent, she told her mother of his conduct. A family argu- 212

ment resulted and the father administered a severe beating to his wife.

Within a few months the mother was obliged to go to a hospital where she remained for several weeks. During this time the father insisted on sleeping in the same bed with his young daughter, despite her protests. He attempted intercourse and as she resisted him, he beat her severely; when she screamed he put his hand over her mouth.

Upon her mother's return from the hospital, she reported the actions of the father, but received scant sympathy, possibly because of her own illness and inability to cope with a cruel and abusive husband. 

Agnes ran away after another incestuous attempt by her father. She found refuge in the home of a girl friend, to whose mother she confided the story of her sad experiences and existence. This woman offered to look after Agnes and to provide her with a home until other suitable arrangements could be made.

FATHERS AND BROTHERS GUILTY OF INCEST 

Determination of what constitutes incest is clothed in some doubts, though it is generally accepted that sexual congress between a father and a daughter, a brother and a sister, raises no questions as to the incestuous relationship. A reading of the 18th chapter of Leviticus, to which reference has previously been made, presents the various relationships which are deemed incestuous in the 

Bible. The verses: "Profane not thy daughter, to make her a harlot", and "None of you shall ap- 213

proach the nakedness of the daughter of thy mother", are of particular interest to the present discussion. The 20 girls who were involved in the incestuous affairs detailed above, provided information which indicated that 13 fathers were guilty of sex relations with their daughters, and seven brothers of such relations with their sisters. 

Five mothers of these girls had died. In these cases, when the fathers of three remarried, older brothers were guilty of attacking their young sisters, while two fathers who did not remarry forced their daughters into sex relationships. One little girl of nine, not only had to suffer the indignities inflicted by her brutal father, but his 20 year old brother likewise forced his sex attentions upon the young child. In these instances there can be little doubt that the presence of a mother would have served as an effective curb upon the libidinous activities of fathers, and of an uncle as well. The fathers of three girls had died, while two mothers remarried. 

Alcohol again took its toll, for several of the incestuous fathers were heavy drinkers, and abusive and cruel to their wives and children. Some girls similarly fell into difficulties because their mothers went out to work, thus leaving their young daughters without adequate protection against evil-minded fathers.

SOME RECENT STUDIES OF INCEST 

Incest has long been accepted as being taboo among primitive peoples, and as a crime in more civilized countries. Numerous efforts have been 214

nomenon. made to find sociological, anthropological and psychological explanations of this sex aberration, and many reasons have been given to explain the phe- A careful study of these factors and their interpretations would hardly tend to clarify such situations as have been discussed in this chapter. Rather, it might be worthwhile to turn to a relatively recent investigation of the social aspects of the problem of incest, as reported upon in Great Britain a few years ago. 

In this report, which was issued in 1925 and entitled "Report of the Departmental Committee on Sexual Offenses 

Against Young Persons," various phases of the subject are discussed, both from the legal and social points of view. One matter insisted upon was that the consent of a female person should be no defense and should be immaterial. This is generally accepted as sound procedure in this country, and serves to counteract the contentions of some, usually the guilty persons, that there are sexually precocious girls who lead on men and youths and that the females should share in the responsibilities involved. 

A year later, namely in 1926, the Secretary for

Scotland likewise appointed a committee to report upon the subject. This group found that proceedings for incest were rare, but many cases went undetected. Information was seldom given to the police when the victim was intimidated, or was presumably a consenting party. The Committee brought out the fact that overcrowding was one of the main contributory causes of incest. In many 215

cases youths and girls of the age of puberty occupied the same room, and even the same bed. Such proximity between brothers and sisters, and even between some fathers and their daughters, undoubtedly is disastrous in its consequences for the girls. 

These living conditions are frequently duplicated in this country in the homes of the poor, more particularly where there are large families of children. 

In such instances, the preventive remedies are apparent, but the application of the necessary measures becomes clothed in many immediate difficulties.

CONCLUSION 

In the matter of incest, the facts that again come to the fore are broken homes, poverty, alcoholism and absence of mothers from the home. How much weight is to be assigned to the mental and emotional disorders of fathers and brothers cannot be determined, without a careful analysis and examination of the males involved. Perhaps the cruelty of some of the fathers and brothers is sufficient, though not excusable explanation, for their mental and nervous failings, or it may be merely indicative of their personalities. These in turn may be so complex and confused, that a careful and competent search for deep emotional factors may be desirable. Be that as it may, it is apparent that young girls need protection, and that concerted and serious thought must be given by representative men and women to the problems involved. An attitude 216

of silence in the face of these unpleasant facts will only serve to engender repetitions of the same deplorable experiences for young girls. Certain obvious steps can readily be taken; others require more serious thought and planning if lasting results are to be achieved. 217

CHAPTER VIII  THE INTRODUCTION 

'HE adequacy of the term "mental problems" is necessarily open to question. However, when one limits the interpretation of the expression to include only those of a fixed age group with, perhaps, a maximum of 15 or 16 years, the task of understanding becomes somewhat simplified. Generally speaking, a group of individuals presenting mental problems and below 16 years of age, may include those of degrees of mentality lower than the accepted standard for a particular age or, as usually expressed, with a low I. Q. (intelligence quotient). Also to be included under the terms of the definition are certain types of dementia praecox; early cases of manic depressive insanity; constitutional psychopathic inferiority; epilepsy complicated by mental deterioration or feeblemindedness; glandular disturbances resulting in mental manifestations; and variations and combinations of one or more of the preceding types. 

Delinquency of young people has, in many cases, been found to have its roots in maladjustment in school. Often, a wide discrepancy exists between a child's ability and the grade program with which he is struggling. This situation may be associated with a number of factors. There is the group of obviously retarded and defective children, who belong academically not higher than the fifth grade; 218

GIRLS PRESenting MenTAL PROBLEMS 

then comes the class of so-called dull-normals, or children who, by dint of prodding and pushing, manage to achieve the sixth, seventh or even eighth grades in public school.

With girls of defective mentality, among whom involvement in sex affairs is an outstanding phenomenon, the question of premature puberty with general physical over-development presents a serious problem. When this is combined with hypersexualism, there should be sufficient grounds to cause parents of such girls serious concern. 

It will be apparent, in a reading of the cases cited in this chapter, that most of the girls were mentally unable to advance beyond the sixth year of public school. In a number of instances it was only through the efforts of the teachers and school authorities, coupled with various adjustments in the curriculum, that enabled these girls technically to advance even to the sixth grade. Some, as will be noted, did not even get that far, though their ages warranted more rapid advancement. 

A question that naturally comes to the fore is whether our public schools are prepared to deal on an adequate basis with girls who present mental problems. The organization of ungraded classes has been of some assistance, with specially trained teachers, smaller class registers, emphasis on manual rather than on mental work, and closer supervision. On the other hand, there are apt to be many borderline or frank cases of mental deficiency, early mental disease, and mental defect or disease masked by nervous ailment, which are not 219

recognized by teachers and supervisors untrained in such matters. Even when discovered, there is generally apt to be a marked shortage of proper facilities to care for such children in the public school system, or through other communal agencies.

Much still remains to be done in this field. 

FACTORS IN SEX INVOLVEMENT 

Whenever the matter of mentally defective and delinquent girls is brought up for discussion, there naturally arises the question as to their possible sexual promiscuity and the part such girls may eventually play as prostitutes and disseminators of disease. Consideration must also be given to two other groups of potentially irresponsible persons who are apt to contribute more than their share to the army of girls who, at some time, may become cummunity liabilities. These categories include the cases of dementia praecox, generally a hopeless mental condition afflicting young people particularly; and of constitutional psychopathic inferiority, often resulting in irresponsible, nonadjustable and uncontrollable misfits in society. 

Together, these groups cause innumerable problems to themselves and to their families, with large expense and many difficulties to society as a whole. 

The histories of some of these girls are partly depicted in the following pages. It should not, of course, be assumed that the mental status of the girls is the only or necessarily the prime cause of their difficulties. Equally defective and vicious males with whom they come in contact, or who 220

make it a practice to prey upon such ailing girls, are in a substantial measure responsible for their sex involvements. To such men must often be charged the fact that girls presenting mental problems become unmarried mothers one or more times; that they become infected with syphilis and gonorrhea, and thereafter transmit these diseases to others who in turn may likewise take advantage of them; that they consort with those of criminal tendencies. Further, were it not for the poor economic status and drab slum areas, coupled ofttimes with parents who were themselves of low mental and moral calibre, these girls might have been spared the suffering, shame and abuse to which they were subjected. As elsewhere noted, the matter of dealing with so-called problem individuals is not a simple one, and involves a number of factors within and without the individual. 

How these cross-currents of environment, human conduct and personal inadequacy overwhelmed a number of girls who might properly be classified as presenting mental problems, is noted in the series of cases which follows.

BELLA had only attained the 6-B grade at the age of 15, and lived with her parents, four brothers and one sister. She was raped by a stranger when 

14 years old and, as a result, contracted gonorrhea, for the treatment of which she required nearly six months of hospital care. A year later Bella became acquainted with 17 year old Francesca who lived in her neighborhood. This girl had run away from home a number of times, and when Bella 221

came to know her she was living in a two-room flat with a man to whom she was not married. At her suggestion Bella left home and joined the unmarried couple and Frank, who was Francesca's 

20 year old cousin. On the first night the young girl slept with Frank and he forced her to have intercourse with him. Francesca then told her male partner to do the same thing to the runaway while the cousins went to bed together. Bella also tried to resist the second man, but to no avail. 

The following morning Francesca informed

Bella that she knew of an easy way to make money by entertaining men and having relations with them. Early in the evening the older girl brought two men to the small apartment. Despite the pleas of Bella, they compelled her to submit to their demands. One gave her $2.50 and the other $5.00, half of which amount Francesca took for herself. That night Bella again slept in the same bed with Frank but succeeded in warding off his attempts at sex contact. A day later the supposed husband of the girl friend came with the news that Bella's relatives were looking for her, and she hurriedly returned home.

Two weeks after rejoining her family, Bella complained of feeling unwell. She was taken to a clinic for examination and the diagnosis was made that she had contracted syphilis. 

HELEN, at 15 years, was in the 6-A grade in public school, and lived with her parents and two older brothers. She met 21 year old Beatrice at a local motion picture theatre and was invited by 222

her to go to the circus and to other motion picture showings. After an assumed friendship of about two weeks, Beatrice told Helen that she had a surprise for her and advised that she bring some extra clothes and meet her the following day. The conspiring young woman also promised a good time and that she would be well taken care of. 

Helen agreed, and was brought to a furnished room, where both girls lived uneventfully for four days. 

On the fifth day Beatrice introduced Helen to 

Mr. W. as her younger sister, and asked her to entertain him while she went out to do some shopping. As soon as Beatrice left, Mr. W. began to talk to Helen in an intimate way, and then made suggestive remarks and advances to her. Finally, he threw her on the bed and, before she could get away from him, he had raped her. After a while he coughed, as though by a prearranged signal, and 

Beatrice then came into the room. She asked him if he was through, and as he left he handed her several five dollar bills. As Helen was crying, Beatrice came to her and offered kind words of consolation with the statement that she would see to it that the experience never happened again. 

Beatrice then washed the stained bed linen while Helen threatened to cause trouble for the part her supposed friend played in the affair. It was apparent even to this mentally dull girl that Beatrice had arranged for Mr. W. to rape her.

The runaway lived in the furnished room three days longer, but no other men were brought there, 223

though she was introduced to several when she attended motion picture shows with Beatrice. The latter disappeared at the end of that time, and being alone, Helen decided to go home. She informed her parents about the shows she attended and the places she went to with Beatrice, but made no mention of the sex experience and the man in the case.

A week later, after Helen had resumed her work at school, she accidentally met Beatrice. Playing upon her fears, Beatrice scared her with the statement that she was going to have a baby as a result of her experience. She persuaded Helen to run away again and to live with her in another furnished room. The pretext she gave for desiring her company was that her husband was away from home and she wanted a companion. Likewise, she promised to look after Helen in her trouble. Somehow the mother located the young girl and on seeing her parent in tears, she immediately decided to desert the questionable character and returned to her own home.

MARY, who was 15 years old and but in the 

6-B grade, lost her mother when only three years of age. The household was considerably disrupted for some years thereafter. The father and Mary lived at home, a 19 year old unmarried sister was in a hospital because of a presumable pregnancy, a younger brother was in a truant school, and an 18 year old brother was committed to a state prison. 

Mary had been in an orphan asylum for 11 years, and was discharged to her father when 14 years old. 224

The only one with whom she had relations was a 13 year old boy. Both lived in the same building and she permitted him to attempt sex contact on three different occasions. Prior to these experiences, Mary had been initiated into perverted sex practices and indulged in them with some of her girl friends who were the mentors. Aside from staying away from school for several days at a time, Mary fell into no further difficulties up to her sixteenth year.

The school work was apparently beyond her capacity and the father had been advised to have his mentally retarded daughter transferred to a continuation school. 

ANTOINETTA was 15 years old, and left school at 14, after having reached only the sixth grade. She lived with her parents, two younger brothers, one sister older and one younger than herself. A man of 22 raped her when she was but 13 years old. She assisted his aunt with the housework and lived in her home. Antoinetta and Theresa were plied with liquor until they became intoxicated. They slept with the men and during the night sex contact took place. The men left early in the morning, and later in the day the girls went to the furnished room of Howard, whom Theresa had visited on former occasions. He was a night worker and the girls slept in his room while he was away. The girls were finally taken in hand at a railroad sta- 226

tion as possible runaways, and Antoinetta was returned to her parents.

EDITH was 14 years old and had only reached the 5-B grade; she was 11 when her mother died, and a year later her father passed away. She then went to live with Mrs. H. who had been her mother's friend; at the end of the year a married sister undertook to care for the young girl.

Edith's sex difficulties started when she ran away from the home of her sister for a period of four days. At the suggestion of 18 year old Amelia, she stole some money and joined the older girl and 15 year old Bernice at a dance hall. They enjoyed themselves till 1 A. M., when they left to go automobile riding with three young men whom they picked up at the place. The ride lasted the balance of the night and wound up at 6 A.M. in a small Connecticut town, to which Amelia had asked that she and her friends be taken. Nothing serious happened to the girls on this trip and they made a date to meet their male escorts a few days later.

The three runaways rented a furnished room where they rested and groomed themselves in order to be presentable when looking for a job. THE FEEBLEMINDED AS SEX DELINQUENTS 

The preceding stories of 20 girls involved in sex experiences give definite indication of the hazards faced by those of low mentality, and the problems they are apt to create for themselves, their families and the community. In the group as a whole, 15 years seems to be the particularly dangerous age.

The difficulties these youngsters faced in their school work is apparent from the findings that two were in ungraded classes, and the balance in the 6-B grade or lower. Broken homes again -stood out as contributory factors in sex involvements, for only 12 girls had both their fathers and mothers living, while two had lost both their parents; five lived with widowed mothers, two of whom had remarried; and the mother of one girl 245

had died, though the father did not remarry. Two of the three widows who did not remarry went out to work. Little, if any, supervision was exercised over their mentally retarded daughters during their absence from home.

An aspect of this question of feeblemindedness and delinquency that needs to be emphasized, is that the low mental status of the girls was not the direct cause of their apparent delinquency. The mere fact that these girls were feebleminded is not warrant for the assumption that they therefore naturally violated the social code. Rather, it should be emphasized that girls and boys of low mentality are apt to become involved in sex and other delinquencies because of many factors. Often, the discrepancy between what they understand to be the right, and the standards set by society in the current interpretations of the difference between right and wrong, is too abstruse for them. Up to recent years, for instance, it was assumed among some to be a meritorious act to help marry off the feebleminded, whereas present social understanding and needs tend to place difficulties and prohibitions about such undesirable unions. The fact should be remembered that feeblemindedness plus opportunity for evil associations and contacts, and minus proper training, may bring about the sex involvement of the type of girls whose stories have been depicted.

It has been pointed out by Dr. Healy that there are great variations in the tendencies to immorality among the feebleminded. Which girls are more 246

GIRLS PRESEnting Mental PROBLEMS 

prone to become entangled in sex difficulties is often impossible to determine, as there are at least two major factors involved-the tendency and sexual maturity of the individual girl, and the viciousness and antisocial make-up of the males with whom she comes in contact. Given the situation where it is feasible to segregate a feebleminded girl or to provide complete family supervision, the probability is that such a girl will go through life without any untoward experiences insofar as her sex life is concerned. On the other hand, if such girls come from homes and conditions as have been described, there is always the possibility that they may become involved.

MENTALLY AND EMOTIONALLY UNSTABLE GIRLS 

The only measure available to determine the mental status of the girls under consideration, was their reported school grades. As a result, all the cases presented in this chapter indicate slow progress in public school, though no satisfactory information was available as to the psychic and emotional status of the 1400 girls whose histories were studied and analyzed. It has been found that often a sex offense on the part of a girl may be merely a symptom of mental aberration. A previously moral girl who has become a victim of chorea, for instance, or who may have her first attack of manic depressive insanity, may begin to show erotic tendencies, run away in the full excitement of the disease, and seek sex experiences. This is an important fact to be kept in mind by parents 247

and others who may come in contact with such girls, for an early recognition of the disorders should lead to the prevention of sex involvement in many instances.

Mention has previously been made of the problems faced by early cases of dementia praecox, of psychopathic personality or constitutional psychopathic inferiority, of epilepsy, and combinations of these and similar disorders. All such unfortunate young people are apt to face serious situations of one kind or another, not the least of which relate to sex experiences. Girls manifesting early signs of psychic or emotional disorder are frequently misunderstood by parents and teachers, and made the butt of severe criticism when, instead, there should be sympathy and understanding, and an attempt to help by personal and medical care. 

The complete histories of many of the girls included in this study gave undoubted evidence of mental and nervous disorders, and yet, in but few instances, was there an apparent appreciation of their problems by those nearest to them.

CONCLUSION 

It is well to differentiate between the types of mental problems and to realize that psychopathic children differ from the feebleminded in that the former are afflicted with a mental disease or episode from which they may recover, while the latter have not developed mentally. Outside of a few general educational and guidance measures, little can be done for the feebleminded. On the other 248

hand, it may be possible, in given instances, to be of considerable service to psychopathic individuals. 

They may be of even markedly superior intelligence, but, for reasons not wholly understood by medical science, they have developed certain hallucinations, delusions, manias and other manifestations of mental disease and emotional disturbance. 

Much initial good has been done in recent years through the organization of child guidance services and clinics, and through the growth of interest in mental hygiene. With the further research work which is going on in this field, the extension of education of parents, teachers and others in the matter of the mental health of children, and the wider acceptance of certain measures of community control, there is at least the hope that sex experiences of such girls as are being considered will, after a time, be reduced appreciably. 249

CHAPTER IX GIRLS WITH NORMAL AND SUPERIOR THE INTELLIGENCE INTRODUCTION 

'HE depth of the reasons why girls of normal and even superior intelligence become involved in sex escapades, and permit themselves to be raped, or allow themselves to be placed in such situations as expose them to the possibility of violation, is indeed difficult to plumb. If a good or above the average school record is to be taken as a mark of normal intelligence, then it becomes apparent that girls possessing such an attribute are not immune from involvement in perplexing sex problems. In the stories that are presented in this chapter, all girls reported upon were public school graduates and had attained the second or a higher term in high school. They gave evidence of sufficient mental ability to be classified as normal, and a few were endowed with a high intelligence quotient, if their rapid advancement in public and high schools can be taken as a criterion. 

It has been previously indicated that many girls are made the unwilling victims of ruthless and conscienceless boys and men, who may themselves be mentally or emotionally inferior individuals.

This does not offer a full explanation for sex difficulties of presumably normal girls. Quite true it is that sometimes intimate contact with degraded persons has not been deliberately chosen, but has 250

Her been thrust upon a girl in her own home. degree of intelligence may often offer no protection against evil-minded men, whether they are relatives, friends or strangers. Exposure to neighbors of low standards, to friendship with young people of few, if any, ideals and questionable morals, may have a definite bearing upon the attitudes of even the girl of superior intellect. These are some factors influencing the conduct of young girls which may be said to be extraneous to their own personalities. One item might be emphasized and that is the indulgence in alcohol. The effect of a little wine or stronger alcoholic drink upon an adolescent girl in breaking down her social and moral inhibitions is notorious. Some of the following cases bear out this observation. The effect is often produced by premeditation on the part of the companions who desire to lower the intended victim's level of normal behavior. With the use of liquor to which she is unaccustomed, a moral and intelligent girl is apt to lose all sense of reserve, with usual barriers against misconduct removed.

BIOLOGICAL AND EMOTIONAL FACTORS 

There are other factors of at least equal importance. First among these stands the matter of biologic development. The girls under discussion were going through the period of pubertal growth, when they were being prepared, biologically speaking, for marriage and motherhood. If one were to say that the girl who indulges in sex relations 251

at the time of puberty was obeying a natural impulse, and was not to be regarded as depraved or immoral, there might be some who would seriously question such a statement, largely perhaps, on a moral basis. It does not, of course, mean that in our present western society we are prepared to condone or permit such conduct. Rather, the implication is that, in considering the experiences of adolescent girls who became involved in sex relationships, more expecially of those who gave some degree of assent, it is important that a deeper basis and significance be sought for. Whether their actions were due to a physical or psychic urge, or to a sense of curiosity, or to an innate emotional instability-the fact remains that these and other factors in the physical, mental and emotional make-up of the individual are important in themselves and must be given the recognition due them.

A word should be said about the emotions. 

Many sex delinquents are possessed of a normal or even superior mental status, with emotional defectiveness. If the mentality had been the deciding factor in their sex difficulties, they probably would never have become involved. But it was not their mental status, not their intelligence, but the way they reacted to certain stimuli, or their emotional status, which moved them to go beyond the line of what is ordinarily denominated as normal conduct.

Among such girls might be classified those who cannot resist the temptations of fine clothes; those who have a native desire for finery and cannot make their desires and pocketbooks meet on common 252

ground; the so-called "smart girls" who attempt to appear sophisticated, and become easy victims of the glitter of praise. The question might perhaps be rightly asked as to whether the trends in modern literature, modern dress and the lowering of social barriers, have to any extent influenced the apparent waywardness of girls of normal mentality. Some are of the opinion that such is the case, while opponents of this view are likewise emphatic in their belief that, far from being an unwholesome influence, our modernism has emancipated youth and tutored girls and boys in more effective and worthwhile living.

Several items in the undoing of the girls of normal and superior intelligence are illustrated in the following cases. Other semblances appear in many of the histories in preceding chapters, but in reading the pages that follow, the thought should be borne in mind as previously suggested, that no one phase is generally the deciding cause of the catastrophe. Many go to shape the career and experiences of an individual, for good or for evil. 253

Anna, nevertheless, threatened to have him reported to the authorities. She returned to the room of her newly found girl friend and remained there five days. Feeling lonesome and depressed, she finally decided to return home and to face the consequences. 

BELLE, who was 15 years old, was in the second term of high school, and made her home with her parents and two younger sisters. A 17 year old neighborhood boy named Robert had become friendly with her and they saw each other every day for a period of several months. When 

Robert asked Belle to permit him the privilege of sex intimacies she refused, but he pulled her into a dark hallway where he raped her. Being fond of him, she kept the affair secret and continued to see Robert every evening, with intercourse taking place about twice a week. At the end of four months he asked Belle to run away from home and to stay with him in the furnished room he maintained. She agreed and was introduced to the landlady as Robert's wife. No questions were asked despite the apparent youth of both young people. 

They lived together for a week until a policeman identified Belle as a runaway from a picture displayed in the local police station. Her mother was notified and Belle was returned home. The young girl protested her affection for Robert and wanted to marry him. 

JULIA was only 13 and in the second term of high school. Her parents were dead and she lived 276

with an uncle and aunt, a brother of the aunt, and 17 year old Daniel, who was being brought up by the uncle and had been in his home for four years. 

One night while Julia was asleep with her door fastened from the inside, Daniel unlocked the door with the aid of a tool. He proceeded to pull off the covers from Julia's bed, at the same time arousing her.

Then he seized hold of her and said he was going to do something to her. The young girl endeavored to fight him off until she finally became exhausted from the uneven struggle, and 

Daniel raped her. On hearing footsteps in the apartment he went quickly to his own room. 

Julia did not tell her aunt or uncle of the experience as she feared they would consider that she was a bad girl. After a few months their interest was aroused by the marked change in her physical appearance. The facts were then revealed. 

MARIE, at 15, was in the fourth term of high school and lived with her parents, a brother and a sister. She played truant one day and caused considerable trouble for herself and family. When she failed to arrive at school, a classmate telephoned her home and spoke to Marie's mother, who could not understand why her daughter was absent. 

Marie stayed away in order to accompany her friend Margie on a visit to a battleship on which both girls knew several sailors. After being shown around the ship, the girls departed and soon separated. Marie then flirted with a sailor and she agreed to accompany him for a walk along Riverside Drive and the river front. When it became 277

dark they sat in the grass in a deserted part of the adjoining park. He began to fool around with her and to kiss and embrace her. Suddenly he forced her onto the grass and raped her. On coming home, a severe scolding was her lot. Despite what had happened, she went the next day to say goodbye to the sailor friend of a few hours acquaintance, as he was leaving port.

JULIE was 15 years old and had reached the third term of high school. She had lived in New 

York for one year, and made her home with her mother, four sisters 23, 17, 13 and eight, and a woman boarder; the father of the family was in another city. Julie had been introduced to 20 year old Walter a few months after her arrival in New York. She saw him regularly and they went to parties and to theatres together; he also visited at her home. At the end of five months he asked her to run away with him, as he liked her a great deal and feared that Julie's mother would not consent to their marriage. Walter suggested that Julie should live with him in an apartment he maintained with a friend, and promised to marry her within a few days. She agreed and joined him the following day. The first night they were together he asked her to let him have intercourse and she permitted him to do so. Their intimacies were repeated the following night. When they left the apartment for breakfast they planned to be married and then to inform Julie's mother of the event. They were encountered on the street by a member of her family and two police officers who took both young people into 278

custody. The tangled romance was straightened out after the mother heard the full story and consented to the marriage.

PREVENTION THROUGH EDUCATION 

It may be assumed that prevention of many bodily, mental and social ills can be made effective through adequate education. Applying this principle to the girls considered in this chapter, the question may be asked how their sex experiences can be accounted for. Analysis of their family status may throw some light upon the matter. 

Of the 20 girls under discussion, 11 had both parents living, though the father of one girl was in another city; the fathers of three girls were dead, and two mothers had remarried; mothers of three girls had died, while one of the orphaned daughters kept house, another lived with an aunt because the father made his home in a distant state, and the third girl was taken into the family circle of a married sister; both parents of two girls had died, with the result that they were compelled to live with relatives in whose homes they were apparently unhappy; divorce claimed one couple, and their daughter was raped by a 16 year old boy when she was only 12 years of age. These 20 instances were freely chosen from a much larger group and may be said to be representative of the situation as a whole. In the cases cited above, disrupted families again stand out as a major factor leading to sex entanglements of girls, even though these youngsters were obviously of normal intelligence. 279

How can education avail against such a finding?

It should first of all be pointed out that the official policy of the several high schools attended by these girls precluded and prohibited the teaching of any sex hygiene information. On the basis of other experiences, it is fair to presume that some girls at least, despite their disrupted and often deplorable home conditions, would have been saved the mental and physical suffering which was their lot, had they been given proper sex instruction and advised as to the significance of the sexual or mating urge.

THE GUILTY MALES 

It is just as important to look upon this matter of prevention through education from the aspect of the boys and men involved, often as perpetrators of rape without any semblance of willingness or yielding on the part of the girls themselves. Study the ages of the males concerned in these particular cases, indicates that they ranged between 15 years in one instance to 38 years in another, with most in the 19-22 year age group. As this age distribution has been found to be true in many other histories, it leads to the assumption that the community failure to provide proper sex instruction for boys and girls has undoubtedly contributed to the serious situations which developed. It might be of interest to point out that a considerable number of the girls became unmarried mothers following their sex experiences, with attendant burdens to themselves and to the community at large. 280

· OTHER CONDUCING FACTORS 

Reference has previously been made to emotional defectiveness. Girls with a normal mental status may give definite signs of emotional weakness, because of which they may become either sex delinquents, or involved in such situations as result in sexual assault upon them. These girls are indeed difficult to advise and control. The important thing is for parents to realize the need of seeking expert medical advice in dealing with a daughter who manifests any symptoms of emotional distress. 

One girl, whose history is presented in this chapter, aroused the suspicions of her family by her conduct, and she was taken to a physician for examination of her mental and emotional status. The recommendation made for serving such girls applies with particular force to the control and treatment of boys as well, for they are apt to be among those who are guilty of rape and assault. 

The effect of alcohol in reducing or temporarily eliminating normal inhibitions against promiscuous sex relationships is so well known that it needs no further emphasis. Nevertheless, it is important to remind parents and educators that the slighting of the possible effects of alcohol, or the indulgence in alcohol by elders may have definite influences upon growing children. It may be permissible for the adult to take alcohol in some form, either regularly and in small quantities, or at irregular intervals at special festive occasions. This does not hold true to the same degree for the adolescent. 

Even a small quantity taken only once, with the 281

stimulation of feelings not fully understood, and the deadening of natural controls, has resulted in many calamities as preceding histories have indicated. 

Sobriety and temperance may be preached for the adult, but is it safe to apply the same rule to young people of both sexes? How shall parents square their own indulgence with prohibitions applied to their youngsters? These are questions for the elders to ponder.

There are a number of other factors in the breakdown of will and natural inhibitions, among them being sexual experiences during childhood; practices taught by maids and others; premature sexual development; lack of idealism in the home, with no adequate substitute provided for it; the desire to be a good sport and to be popular among boys; and others which can be readily plucked from the stories of many young girls who are involved in sex experiences, who contract a venereal disease, and who become pregnant after having had a "fling" at life.

CONCLUSION 

An adequate summary of the sex experiences of unmarried girls with a normal mental status would take in a panoramic view of what happened in large measure to all the other girls whose stories have been depicted in abbreviated form in preceding chapters. Aside from those who gave definite evidence of mental dullness, all the other situations touched upon were involved in the histories of socalled normal girls, including the runaways, girls 282

in broken homes, in poor environment, girls in crowded homes, and the like. The problems faced by these young people of normal mental status are those with which many have to contend. Whenever society determines to step in and lend a further helping hand to floundering youth, much good may eventuate. It is obvious that many adults are inadequately prepared for the task of worthwhile parenthood. Unless the organized community, through the schools, the police and health departments, and other official and non-official agencies, takes cognizance of the misery and suffering needlessly caused to hosts of young girls, and boys as well, little progress may be expected. 283

CHAPTER X  

TH 

INTRODUCTION 

HE presentation of data relating to the sex experiences of a large group of girls 16 years old and younger is beset with certain difficulties.

Those who are less than 10 or 12 years of age are often unable to give complete details and to remember the sequence of circumstances and facts which it would be desirable to ascertain. When such children are of ages ranging between four and eight years, the problem of obtaining reliable data from them on certain phases of their family histories and sex involvements becomes aggravated. Sufficient factual and statistical material has been obtained, however, to give a fairly correct picture of the situations and of the girls involved, as well as of their prototypes whose stories are not included. 

One would naturally assume that, just as the available information was not always uniform, so accuracy might likewise have been affected, but the nature of the facts sought was such as to preclude any marked degree of error. Some of the items were: age; birthplace; school grade; father and mother living or dead; their respective occupations, if any; boarders in family; at what age first sex experience occurred; who was responsible for first sex act; etc., etc. It is apparent that data secured 284

on this basis of questioning excluded the personal equation and errors to a large degree.

In preceding chapters stories of 160 of these were cited as examples of some of the deleterious influences to which many young people are exposed. Likewise, a picture was drawn of situations and persons in a large measure responsible for providing opportunities for young girls to veer from the straight and narrow path.

The extreme youthfulness of many of the girls concerned might be termed shocking. Certain fundamental factors of a statistical nature are involved in the proper interpretation of these figures. 

It is apparent that the number of cases increases as the age of puberty advances. The exception to this statement lies in the 16 year old group, but this is more apparent than real. The cases studied 285

included primarily those under 16 years of age, and the 47 girls of that age were added to the younger group merely because their records happened to be readily available. If the purpose were to work out rates of incidence for different ages, it would necessarily be important to consider the number noted for each age by including the figures of their part in the population as a whole. Census figures relating to the age distribution of the female sex in New York City could be posted opposite the numbers and percentages in Table I. These findings, on the other hand, would have to be balanced against several factors which cannot be put on a statistical basis; such as, the age at which individual girls were permitted out on the street without proper guardianship and supervision; age at which menstruation began; family overcrowding; status of the families, i.e. father living, mother living, etc.; moral standards of the family groups; etc. With the advance in age comes greater freedom, but there are matters of family custom and education, neighborhood conditions, family constitution, mental and emotional make-up of the girls, which tend to influence the results. 

No separa tion has been made between white and Negro girls; approximately 19 per cent of the total studied belonged to the second group-an undue proportion on the basis of the total Negro population in New York City. Various factors, however, were at work to bring the Negro ratio to this high figure, such as low incomes; marked overcrowding; ratio 286

between the sexes; recent migration from southern states; etc.

Two items of interest stand out in the above table: one is the number of girls recorded in the 

6-A-6-B grades. On the basis of the available records, the number of girls in the sixth year of school outnumber the normal total that should be expected in the light of the age distribution. On the assumption that girls 11 and 12 years of age may properly belong in the 6-A-6-B grades, it appears that there were 152 or 11.8 per cent of such ages, while 158 or 14.7 per cent of those for whom records were available actually were in these grades. The variation would be increased somewhat if the group of 16 year old girls were excluded from the computation. The major point of interest, insofar as this particular group is concerned, lies in the recognized fact that many of the girls were found to be mentally defective and could not advance beyond the sixth year in public school. 

Many of the subnormal girls or those of low intelligence are pushed ahead in their grades until they attain the 6-A-6-B rating, irrespective of their chronological ages. This practice tends to overcrowd, as it were, the sixth grades with girls who are between 13 and 16 years of age.

Another matter of interest is presented by the comparatively few girls in high school, when the age distribution, as noted, would warrant the registration of a much larger number. Accepting the fact that girls of 15 years should be in high school if physically and mentally normal, it would 289

appear from Table I that 516 or 40.2 per cent of these girls should have been enrolled in junior or senior high schools. As will be noted from Table 

III, a total of only 110 girls in the whole series were in such grades, even after including those 16 years of age who attended high school. Perhaps the reason that less than 25 per cent of the expected number were in high school lies in the relatively low economic status of the parents of a large number of these girls, the mental incapacity for high school work of so many of the 14, 15 and 16 year old girls under consideration, the debilitating mental and moral effects of broken homes; disturbing influence of early sex experiences; and other factors already dwelt upon.

A number of the older girls were engaged in several types of remunerative work, many with and others without working papers. 

Such occupations included: various minor business positions 

92; odd jobs 54; housework 23; factory work 12; theatrical work 5; sewing 5; and laundry 5. No information can be presented as to the work records of those employed, though many of the jobs are known to have been part-time or summer jobs only. 

STATUS OF PARENTS 

Records were available for a total of 1212 of the girls as to whether their fathers were living. 

The figures indicate that 364 or 30.0 per cent of the girls had lost their fathers by death-a rather high figure. On the basis of 1257 records of mothers, 290

or 14.7 per cent of the girls had lost their mothers by death. Twenty-five of the girls or about 2 per cent had lost both parents by death. 

These figures indicate twice as many deaths of fathers as of mothers, and it is difficult to estimate how much this circumstance affected the experiences of the girls. A total of 43 widowed fathers remarried, and 151 of the widowed mothers brought a stepfather into the home, frequently to complicate the family situation. Apparently, the rate of remarriage of widows was higher than that of widowers. The possible explanation may be found in the usual circumstance among widows in low income families-the need of a wage-earner to support young children. 

Occupations of the fathers were rather scattered.

There were very few who might be classified among the business or professional groups, while most of them belonged to the low-income category. 

Of the working mothers, many were employed as part-time houseworkers, janitresses, laundresses and factory workers. In a number of cases their absence from home had an undoubted influence upon the delinquency or unwilling experiences of their daughters.

DOMICILE SEPARATE FROM PARENTS 

A number of the girls under consideration, for one reason or another, were making their homes separate from their parents. Usually such girls 291

sought out some relative or had been placed with or were taken in by relatives. Among them were 

27 girls living with an aunt or uncle; 25 with grandparents; 14 with married sisters and their husbands; 4 with cousins; 12 with foster parents; and a number of others scattered among relatives by blood and marriage of varying degrees of closeness. 

Undoubtedly, removal from a normal family environment, resulting in the loss of the usual parental guidance, control and supervision, had much to do with difficulties which befell these girls. Information on this point was incomplete in many of the records studied; otherwise there would probably have been others to be added to those listed as living apart from their parents.

ROOMERS IN HOMES OF GIRLS 

Among families with low incomes, the practice has long prevailed of permitting roomers to share what are often already inadequate family quarters. 

Such a procedure has definite moral hazards, particularly when there are young girls in the family circle. The following Table IV provides informa tion relative to measures taken by the families of some of the girls in order to supplement budgetary needs.

Of the cases cited, there are a goodly number whose problems arose because of the presence in the home of male boarders, usually unmarried and often without local family ties and responsibilities. Exposure of young girls to such an environmental influence is, to say the least, provocative of moral disaster. 292

Psychological effects in after years on children brought up amid poverty in homes that lack wholesome socializing influences, as well as in the presence of immoral or vicious persons, can only be surmised. Runaway propensities, emotional outbursts and the seeking of excitement and various thrills are natural concomitants of such situations.

AGE AT FIRST SEX EXPERIENCE 

The extreme youthfulness of many of the girls at their first sex experience, whether it was voluntary or forced, is a matter of marked concern. It is 293

readily evident from the figures in Table V that even the youngest of children are not spared by those who seek to take advantage of them. The numbers increase with the ages and, as previously pointed out, the closer that the age of puberty is approached, the greater the number of girls found involved. The few who were 16 years old are accounted for by the small number of records available for analysis in this age group. It has already been noted that the age distribution in the population as a whole would naturally have to be taken into consideration if it were intended to work out rates in these cases. But so many factors were involved that it would be virtually impossible to prepare tenable rates for different age groups. The striking increases in the 12, 13, 14 and 15 year groups can be explained on various grounds. biological development of the girls, however, must be given first thought.

Consideration of the persons responsible for the initial sex experience of many of the girls may throw 294

some light upon adequate preventive measures that it may be possible to utilize. In the 160 cases cited, mention is made in each instance of those who perpetrated the assault upon the girls. 

Among those responsible are included friends, acquaintances through neighborhood and other contacts, strangers, pick-ups who might also be included among total strangers, boarders, fathers, brothers, etc. Table VI lists these persons in the numerical order in which they were involved.

Total The above figures are indicative of the group of individuals to whom it may be said young girls are exposed insofar as sex violation is concerned. 

It is apparent that many groups of the population are included. On the basis of the available data, 295

it would be safe to draw the conclusion that few of the better educated, better placed and more responsible individuals were involved. One is immediately faced, however, with the question as to the comparative number of such individuals in any given community. Even granting that they form approximately a small part of a cosmopolitan city population, still these so-called better citizens were rarely cited in these cases. The "whys" and "wherefores" of this phenomenon may be a happy augury for the safety of girls in the future, as well as demonstrative of the fact that the educated and responsible youths and men may have a higher social sense which generally inhibits such antisocial conduct. It is not presumed that persons who have had the advantage of fine parents, good homes, and even above-the-average opportunities in life do not become involved in these cases. The fact is that in a cross-section of 1400 such instances of sex violation which occurred over a period of time in a large city, very few really responsible persons and those of standing were named by these girls as having perpetrated attempts at rape. There are undoubtedly added reasons to account for this phenomenon, but it would serve no adequate purpose to attempt to delve into such matters-at least insofar as the current analysis is concerned. 

Boy and girl friendships without proper social backgrounds and inadequate parental supervision evidently led many girls to disaster. The lack of sex instruction in home and in school, and the attrition of character by other controllable factors 296

likewise added their toll. Workers in settlements, community centers, schools and other meeting places for adolescents of both sexes, are often concerned with ways and means of providing adequate leadership to the young people who are their parttime responsibility. Many youngsters in poor neighborhoods who do not share in the benefits available in well-organized community centersand they far outnumber those who participate in such activities-are largely without guidance along certain social lines. That the schools are doing little in coordinated sex instruction has already been noted. When parents are uninformed and harassed by problems that to them are more pressing than the evolving sex life of their children, then disaster may be in the offing. Young people of both sexes are frequently bereft of the kind of advice and help that rightfully should be theirs. 

Despite the scope and extent of the settlement and community center movement, such agencies have thus far been largely unable to meet needs for sex instruction which parents and schools have failed to provide.

The automobile plays a most important part in the sex involvements of young girls arising out of so-called pick-up acquaintanceships. It is notorious that along certain streets in various cities, automobilists cruise with the intent of picking up girls for an evening of fun, wherever and to whatever it may lead. Further, numbers of young girls apparently learn the locations and evening hours when automobile rides and entertainment may be 297

solicited or freely obtained. Other pick-ups figuring in the picture include sailors on shore leave or congregated about a navy yard. The police are well aware of the tendency of many young girls to "follow the uniform", and are usually on the alert to prevent too close an intimacy between sailors and chance girl friends. Youth somehow finds a way to avoid preventive and protective police action, however, with the consequence that many serious situations developed in the lives of a number of the girls under consideration.

Mention has previously been made of the possibility for evil inherent in the housing of adult male boarders-usually total strangers-in crowded homes in which there are young girls. The involvement of fathers, brothers, stepfathers, uncles, nephews, cousins and other near relatives, has likewise been dealt with and will not be elaborated upon further at this point. Peddlers who enticed young girls were generally those catering to children with their wares of candy and ice cream. The serious situations that arose and involved retail storekeepers usually followed when a young girl would be sent alone to a local store to make a purchase. In several instances, such girls were the daughters of women who went out to work all day and left their children without adequate supervision.

The miscellaneous group noted in Table VI and numbering 30 individuals, included a like number of boys and men who were frequently acquaintances of the young girls with whom they became 298

involved. In some cases they were strangers such as insurance agents, an imposter claiming to be a physician, a theatre usher, and others. Children who, for one reason or another, spend much of their spare time on the streets face many hazards and temptations. The preceding data should be indicative of the care with which parents must guard their young daughters against association with unknown persons who may make suggestive or improper proposals to them; and of the necessity for proper sex instruction by adequately trained teachers and, whenever possible, by parents themselves. A frank and wholesome understanding between parents and their young children, with a sense of freedom on the part of children to approach their elders on personal matters, would go a long way in averting many of the situations discussed. 

Acceptance of this suggestion presumes receptive and understanding fathers and mothers. For children who are not so fortunately placed as to have parents who invite their confidences and are ever ready to listen to their youthful troubles and complaints, the community must come to the assistance of both children and their guardians. For the young people, guidance and instruction by sympathetic and well-versed teachers are indicated; for the parents, leadership and advice through parents' associations, mothers' clubs, special courses, and the like, may ordinarily help to meet some of the needs of enlightenment. The manifold problem is well-recognized, though adequate com- 299

munal machinery to meet it is yet to be made readily available.

FORCED AND VOLUNTARY VIOLATION 

The attempt has been made to differentiate between those who were forcibly assaulted and those who gave at least their tacit consent to the act of violation. It was extremely difficult to draw a valid conclusion in many of the cases, though there was undoubted evidence that considerably more than half of the girls were violated despite every effort on their part to escape. Still others were threatened with death and physical harm by revolver or knife when they tried to resist, while some, at the time of being raped, were soundly asleep, unconscious, drunk or drugged. Of those who were classified as giving their consent, many were either too young to comprehend what was the import of the act, or the nature of the request or demand made of them; while others belonging to the older age groups stated that they likewise did not know what it was all about. When requested by friends or acquaintances, relatives or pick-ups, to participate in the sex act, some consented because of the desire to retain a friend, or because of the offer of a gift or money. Had they been the recipients of adequate sex instruction and under proper guidance and parental control, many of these girls would undoubtedly have avoided the pitfalls that awaited them. 300

PLACES WHERE SEX VIOLATION OCCURRED 

Many young fellows who might properly be called loafers were involved as perpetrators of sex crimes against young girls. They evidently did not encounter much difficulty in finding places to take the girls whom they intended to rape, as access to furnished rooms, cheap hotels, automobiles, etc., for illegitimate purposes is all too easy in a large city. That this should not be so is quite apparent, but thus far efforts at control have been ineffective to a large degree and the evil situation continues to prevail. A listing of the places where the offenses took place may add something to the weight of the words of advice and warning which parents, teachers and community workers might well pass on to young girls.
