From the preceding table it is apparent that furnished rooms, hallways, public parks, automobiles, roofs and hotels were the statistically significant places to which girls were taken. While the figure of 256 is given for furnished rooms, there should be added to this almost all of the 213 recorded for the homes of men, as these were often a two-room or larger apartment in which boys or detached men lived. If this addition be made, then it is apparent that 469 or 44.3 per cent of the cases recorded were inveigled to the rooms of the men, where the act of rape took place. Whether or not some better type of control of rooming and furnished apartment houses of the cheaper class can be developed, is a question that might bear further investigation. The landlords of such places, where unmarried couples may live for a night or longer, might be more vigorously approached in the effort to remedy this situation. Perhaps some type of effective control and registration should be insisted upon in various communities. This matter possibly represents a fertile field of study and service for protective agencies and women's organizations. Realization of the problem should lead to definite and concerted action. An active educational campaign would probably bring about concrete results. 

Dark and narrow hallways in poor tenement houses with the rear parts obstructed by entrances to cellars, were significant in the list of places involved. As noted in some of the cases cited, young girls were dragged or enticed behind cellar doors 302

by vicious and immoral characters and, by threats and force, compelled to submit to the assaults which were inflicted upon them. Tenement house roofs, hall toilets of tenements and vacant apartments, were likewise utilized for these nefarious purposes. In some instances girls yielded to presumably legitimate offers and accompanied boys or men to the roofs of tenements. The promises were generally fraudulent and the girls were almost invariably raped.

The 

The assault took place in the homes of the girls in 194, or 18.3 per cent of the cases in which information was obtainable. Among these should be included those who were violated by their own fathers, stepfathers, brothers or other relatives; boarders; boy or men friends; and others. protection to be offered to young girls against the insidious advances and more direct approaches of such persons involves many difficulties. Often the first inkling of the danger to which a daughter has been exposed is when she tells her mother or other relative after the event, or when parents learn that the daughter is pregnant. The roomer situation has already been discussed, and it remains but to add further emphasis on the need for careful instruction of parents, as well as of their daughters. The acceptance of boarders into an already crowded home would often be obviated if the family had an adequate income, or if decent housing were provided at rentals which wageearners could pay. In some places attempts have already been made to limit the number of persons 303

who may occupy a given number of rooms. This is a step in the right direction, but it does not meet the economic problem which is faced by many families even in so-called normal times. 

The large public parks in cities have provided the environment for many acts of violence and rape, as well as of promiscuous relationships. This, of course, is no argument for the abolition of such public benefits, but rather for the better policing and lighting of those parks which come to the attention of the authorities as places to which evil-minded males are wont to go when seeking to assault young girls. An astonishing fact is that several of the 58 instances of violation occurred in one important park in the center of the city, and took place in the afternoons when many people and vehicles were presumably about. And yet, such is the ingenuity and unconcern of these degenerates, that they are not phased by such obstacles. They were still able to find secluded spots behind rocks or shrubs in which to carry out their immoral purposes.

Those who have been engaged in community activities, and particularly in large cities, have long known that many smaller hotels are operated in which the scrutiny of potential roomers is even less than a matter of form. Men of all ages accompanied by young girls are permitted to register for them as their wives, with the assigned rooms infrequently used for more than one night, if for that length of time. In cities where the services of competent policewomen are available, a watch 304

is often kept upon the more notorious of such hotels, with the result that many a girl has been saved the shame and misfortune that awaited her but for the interference of these newer officers of the law. With the growth of roadhouses, overnight cabins, tourist boarding houses and similar facilities for automobilists, the situation has become aggravated. In larger cities, more effective control might be exercised with adequate police personnel and proper backing by prosecutors; likewise, licensing authorities and the police might cooperate more closely in keeping under surveillance at least those hotels which become notorious in a community for the clientele to which they cater. Supervision of the other types of sleeping accommodations, such as are provided in roadside cabins, has aroused much discussion in certain quarters. The situation is complicated by the fact that the police and licensing authorities in rural areas are either incompetent or inadequate for the job, or both. Whether the several states will have to interfere in order that licensing and policing shall be organized on a county or state basis, as has already been done in some instances, is a matter still to be determined. 

Naturally, no slur is intended upon the motion picture theatres as a group, even though girls are listed as having been assaulted in such theatres, and many more exposed to petting, handling, indecent exposure, etc. The theatres in which these several acts took place were of the small neighborhood type, where unaccompanied young children somehow 305

gain admittance without a guardian, though the law specifically and rightly prohibits these admissions. One of the authors was for some time engaged as a license official assigned to the supervision of a number of local theatres; almost invariably young children were found in attendance without apparent guardians after school hours and on days when school was not in session. Attempts at worthwhile control would require a large staff of properly trained and supervised inspectors and thorough cooperation on the part of the police and the courts. Further, the assistance of parents in keeping their children away from public gathering places unless properly chaperoned, is imperatively needed. Otherwise, little can be accomplished.

Among other places to which girls were taken for carnal purposes are included open fields, usually after late automobile rides with pick-ups and acquaintances; automobiles and taxicabs; the homes of relatives; back rooms of stores; offices; backyards and alleys; and four instances occurred where houses of prostitution were utilized, with the evident purpose of initiating the girls as inmates of such houses. A fuller public knowledge of the places to which girls are taken with the intent of raping them should lead to at least some mitigation in sexual assaults, and the apprehension of those responsible for involving young girls in such experiences. 306

RUNAWAYS 

Some material has already been presented relative to girls who ran away. Out of the total number under consideration, complete records as to runaway activities and propensities were obtained in 

1167 instances. Of these 510 or 43.7 per cent gave histories of having run away from home for one or more days. Two girls, incidentally, were ordered out of their homes by their fathers. The runaways gave a wide variety of reasons for leaving their own homes or those of relatives and others with whom they were boarded. Unquestionably, many of the girls fell into sex difficulties just because, by running away for whatever reason or excuse, they cut the bonds which tied them to family protection and supervision. This fact is illustrated in a number of the cases cited. 

The causes given for running away show comparatively marked variations in validity and reasonableness. Among them were the following: fight or disagreement with father; cruel father; incestuous father; intoxication of father, mother or both; cruel mother; cruel stepmother; both parents cruel; severe punishment by brothers, older sisters, aunts and other relatives; dislike of school; fear of beating by father, mother or brother because of reaching home too late after attending a motion picture show, or dance, or visit with boy or girl friends; fear of returning home after being raped, or participating more or less voluntarily in sex act; desire to be free; desire to have fun; desire to make 307

new friends, especially of the male sex; wish for pretty clothes; influenced by boy or girl friends; went away in an automobile with pick-up or chance acquaintances; overwork at home; foster mother mean and cruel; left to marry; went to look for a job and to be independent; and a fairly large number of other reasons.

It is apparent that cruel and abusive parents may maltreat their growing children until the youngsters pass into the adolescent stage, when the surging within them of resentment and new ideas prompts them to desire to be on their own responsibility. A quick and temporary way out for such young people, though, of course, often an unsatisfactory if not hazardous way, is to leave home and seek one's fortune along lines featured in success stories and cheap magazines. How far their essay for freedom and riches succeeded, is portrayed in the cases depicting the doleful stories of runaway girls and what happened to many of them. Mistreatment by other relatives is on the same plane with that inflicted by parents, and, as is evident, may lead to the same kind of catastrophes.

Quite a number of the girls reported that their troubles began when, thoughtlessly, they lost track of the lateness of the hour while away from home. 

Fearing chastisement perhaps because of similar experiences previously, they decided to spend the night in hallways, vacant apartments, riding in the subway, accepting invitations for an automobile ride late at night from strange men, walking the streets all night perplexed as to the next step, 308

and similar efforts to avoid one evil by creating another. Overstrict parents are probably accountable for some of the disasters which befell their daughters. On the other hand, understanding parents are apt to know their children and insist on the keeping of early hours, so as to shield their susceptible daughters against experiences which befell them once they broke the family rule of returning home at a reasonable time. In the light of the fairly large number of girls of inferior mental equipment among those reported upon, such a parental requirement seems reasonable and imperative.

Unsatisfactory school records or adjustment were given by a number of the girls as another important cause for running off, frequently without knowing where the night was to be spent, and where the money was to come from to meet even the minimum requirements for food and lodging. The impetuousness of harassed youth may, in part, be blamed for this type of response to a situation which was really simple for classmates and friends, but which, to particular girls, presented insurmountable difficulties. Therefore, a way out was sought in headlong flight, with a new runaway added to the rolls. Educators and psychologists, as well as those directly concerned with so-called conduct disorders among children of public school age, have for some time been giving consideration to the problem of adjustment of the curriculum to meet the needs of particular types of boys and girls. 

In a properly organized and supervised school 309

system, there should be place for sufficient guidance personnel. They should be available to meet the psychic and emotional, environmental and school problems of young people. With wisely selected and trained staff members of grammar and high schools, charged with the responsibility of helping to meet the personal needs of those boys and girls on the verge of running away or becoming involved in other conduct problems, it might be possible to avoid the types of situations already dwelt upon. 

CONCLUSION 

A wise teacher and physician in the field of mental hygiene and with long experience with delinquents, has written that children who have lived a miserable and unhappy life without any of the things which children love, such as toys, candies, etc.; who have been unwanted perhaps in the first place and are considered merely as a burden; whose environment does not offer them love and affection, or an opportunity to develop along natural lines-may become delinquents solely as a result of attempting to compensate for these natural deprivations. Often the early beginnings in sex and other delinquencies lead to disastrous consequences in later life. This analysis of delinquents in general has very definite application to the lives and histories of many of the young girls whose experiences have been revealed. On the other hand, girls who attempted to lead clean and wholesome lives, were often the victims of the brothers and 310

their prototypes of these same girls who found home life bare and drab, with denials mostly and satisfactions few and far between. 

Further evidence of the uniformity of sex delinquency and violation as attendant upon certain given conditions, whether in New York or Ohio or California, is presented by an experienced official and observer in the latter state. There it was likewise found, as in New York, that thousands of children are permitted to roam the streets; they frequent motion picture houses where much that was seen gave a wrong impression of life; they attend public dance halls, beaches and other resorts unchaperoned and uncontrolled. The similarity of the experiences and end results in widely separated American communities are definite indices of the need for organized and studied attention on a wider scale to the problems dealt with. 

Until there is a greater visualization of the moral and physical hazards to which young girls are exposed, little will be availed. Necessarily, this cannot be limited to the organized community, whether through its usual municipal functions as administered by police, license and institutional departments; nor yet, is this objective a concern and task only for the voluntary agencies cooperating with municipal and state functionaries. Rather, the observation must be made that the sharing of responsibility for existing conditions cannot be segmented, but must be expected alike from parents and public authorities, as well as from voluntary agencies and institutions concerned with the moral, 311

social and educational standards of the youth of the city or state. A new impetus must arise adequately to cope with the interrelated problems which have been touched upon and which press for control and solution. 312

CHAPTER XI COMMUNITY FACTORS AFFECTING 

IN 

GIRLS INTRODUCTION 

N 1775 Blackstone, the distinguished English jurist, published his comments on the possibilities of prevention of criminal acts. His words were: "We are also concerned with the means of preventing the commission of crimes and misdemeanors. And really, it is an honor, almost a singular one, to our English laws, that they furnish a title of this sort: since preventive justice is upon every principle of reason, of humanity, and of sound policy, preferable in all respects to punishing justice, the execution of which, though necessary, and in its consequences a species of mercy to the commonwealth, is always attended with many harsh and disagreeable circumstances." The fundamental thoughts presented by Blackstone are as valid today as they were in his time. Prevention has substance and value which no amount or kind of punishment can approximate.

In the immediate matters of concern regarding the girls under consideration, it has been apparent that preventive measures would have saved many a girl from physical and moral harm and suffering.

The determination of which methods are feasible and applicable involves several questions of prime importance. It must be fully realized that, whether one is dealing with rape and assault or with other 313

types of antisocial conduct, community factors often stand out as of incomparably greater importance than individual conduct. Evils, such as those that corrupt youth, can be effectively fought only through an attack at their roots. Past attempts to remedy conditions by occasional and half-hearted efforts at control, by arresting and imprisoning individual violators of laws or ordinances, or taking other measures which do not get at the fundamentals, have left much to be desired. Many of the girls under scrutiny made reference to the part that motion picture theatres played in their difficulties; the apparent stimulation of sexual passions of the boys and men whom they accompanied to such theatres; the influence of salacious literature; and the frequency with which cheap dance halls contributed to their sex involvements. Various communities have done little to control some of these corrupters of youth. Perhaps a better understanding of the problems involved might be achieved if a portrayal were given of the current situation insofar as it relates to motion pictures with sex implications; theatres which parade sex in most obnoxious forms; sex magazines with pornographic stories, pictures, cartoons and advertisements; and taxi-dance halls with their blatant activities.

It may be desirable to make mention at this point of a matter which has thus far been deliberately kept out of the discussion. This relates to the nature of the material presented in the preceding case histories, and some of the data 314

which follow in succeeding pages. The only apology for detailing the sordid tales of the girls is because in this way alone, by a presentation of the crude facts, may it be possible to arouse the conscience of those who can and should take the lead in remedying a situation which has remained subdued too long. Unless parents and community leaders are willing to face the facts, unpleasant as they may be, little will avail. Squeamishness and prudery on the part of elders cannot protect our youth. The same thought applies to the discussion which follows. It would be far better were it not necessary to call attention to certain failings in motion pictures; to what has been denominated an abomination by some but goes under the name of a burlesque performance; to so-called sex magazines displayed on newsctands and elsewhere and which no self-respecting person wants to be seen buying; to the latest version of cheap dance halls but currently termed taxi-dance halls these are some of the community factors affecting the health and morals of youth. These words are not to be mistaken as being in the guise of an apology-rather, they are a challenge to serious thought and to action. 

There are many demoralizing influences in every community, and in the lives of young girls and boys of low income families the exposure to such influences is particularly severe. If there be added to these items of individual and group disruption, community factors which are beyond the control of parents, and much less of the young people 315

themselves, then their lot becomes a particularly distressing one. Many have taken these things as a matter of course, assuming that no social control was possible, necessary or even desirable, or that somehow all would turn out well in the end. If the preceding stories of the girls are deemed to be insufficient as an indication of the present deplorable state of affairs, there remains little doubt that a presentation of community aspects of the problem, thrown on a wider screen and reaching into the lives of many thousands of young people, may focus more effective public attention on the agents and persons responsible for the demoralization of young people. 

THE INFLUENCE OF MOTION PICTURES 

Several attempts have been made to evaluate the effects of motion pictures on the conduct of children. In the last analysis, it should be a fairly easy matter for thoughtful parents to determine whether they desire their children to view pictures portraying women of loose morals, murders, holdups, and others with salacious titles and plots. 

When motion pictures were in their early stage of development, objection was often taken to their subject matter, their crudities, and the possible effects upon the morals and behavior of adolescents. 

Since then, while the techniques have been improved and talking movies have come to the fore, there has been nevertheless, only a moderate advance in the presentation of so-called clean pictures. 316

In the preparation of this material, a one-day survey was undertaken in a crowded section of New York City, in order to determine the type of pictures being exhibited, and the nature of display and advertising signs and posters used to draw an audience. There were few pictures whose titles alone were not suggestive of sex in some form or another. Following are the captions of pictures which were being shown in a poor residential neighborhood about one mile square, with the price of admission ranging from ten cents to twenty-five cents: The Third Sex; Love is Dangerous; The 

Kiss Before the Mirror; Best of Enemies; Goldie 

Gets Along; His Private Secretary; No Marriage 

Ties; Lady for a Day; Devil's Mate; What Price 

Innocence; Her Splendid Folly; My Weakness; 

Her Forgotten Past; I Have Lived; Moonlight and 

Pretzels; Baby Face; Wives Beware; Laughing at Life.

Now, it might not have been so bad if these titles alone were featured, even though their suggestiveness is most apparent in several instances. 

To emphasize the sex aspects of the pictures, there were displays of posters, including photographic reproductions of scenes in the pictures. It was of considerable interest to stand in front of the theatres besides young boys and girls, and overhear their comments on bedroom and love scenes, the overdisplay of nudity and the like. If these were apparently of interest to the youngsters and the necessary admission fees were available to them, they went directly to box offices and, in violation of 317

the law, were admitted without further ado. This matter of permitting unaccompanied children to attend such performances has been of sufficient import to warrant periodic attempts at control. 

To judge from the number of children under 16 years of age who were freely admitted to the theatres visited, it is apparent that the authorities must consider the law as unenforceable, or the motion picture theatre owners have no fear of the law and continue blithely to violate it. During the time that one of the authors was a city inspector of such theatres, he found a general lack of respect for this provision of the Penal Code, many of the owners of smaller theatres stating that they would be put out of business if the law on admission of minors were properly enforced. Virtually none of the larger theatres will admit an unaccompanied minor. Studies in various cities have indicated that the smaller neighborhood theatres seemingly specialize in the presentation of pictures dealing with sex and crime. 

Some time ago the gangster and gunman pictures were common fare for our youth. It was not until various organizations began to exert pressure that producers and exhibitors decided to call somewhat of a halt. There have been a few important exceptions within the past year or two, with the production of films depicting prominent actors in criminal roles. More recently, the counter-activities of federal police agencies have been drawn upon in plot construction, with the necessary gun-play included. The effect of such 318

productions has been measured time and again. through interviews with young delinquents, while the summary of judgment has been that such portrayals have definite evil influences inherent in them, and should be discouraged as far as possible. 

How far the exhibition of such sex pictures as those noted above can affect the lives and morals of young boys and girls is difficult to evaluate. It is of undoubted significance that delinquent girls have time and again reported that viewing such pictures has made it easier for them to fall into evil ways. 

Turning back for a moment to the stories of the girls whose lives have been delineated in part, it may be of interest to raise the question whether one is to wonder that they came to look with envy and desire upon ways and means of having a "good time," expensive clothes, automobile rides, dining in expensive restaurants, living in hotels, going to dances, and the like. In their drab existence there was often a vision of better things, from the material point of view, through friendships with men, and in turn the dispensing of their sex favors. 

A further aspect which cannot be slighted is the undoubted sex stimulation following a viewing of pictures suffused with sex themes and scenes. The dangers latent in the present situation apply with particular force to those neighborhoods in which the least amount of parental control is apt to be exercised. The small local theatres are the ones usually patronized by children, more especially by those unaccompanied by parents or guardians. 

With the price of admission generally low, there is 319

a definite trend for these youngsters to attend local theatres. A significant observation was the fact that many of the objectionable pictures listed were produced by small concerns, apparently at limited costs, and with the objective of presenting attractions with sex themes and box office drawing power. 

The overstimulation of sex in young people, to whom many of the refinements and opportunities of life are denied, is undoubtedly a cause of harm. 

Motion pictures are generally recognized as being one of the most powerful forces in shaping the thoughts and habits of the public. They influence a great many people and are particularly potent in their effect upon those of immature minds-the impressionable youngsters of today. How to protect and guard them against such deleterious influences as are foisted upon them through quasisalacious pictures, with undue emphasis on sex and crime, is a matter that presents a challenge to producers, exhibitors, parents, teachers, public authorities, and interested citizens at large. All must learn of the needed action to forfend growing, impressionable and volatile youth from forces against which children have no remedy. If the fundamental educational value of motion pictures is to be retained for the benefit of the new generation, then the time for some permanent housecleaning has come rather, it has long been overdue. 

Emphasis is placed upon the continuity of selfcensorship by the motion picture industry because of the latent feeling among a large section of the interested public that official censorship does not 320

always provide the desirable remedy. As a matter of fact, such a check exists through the official motion picture censorship boards already functioning, and through the readily available police powers of constituted authorities. The recent campaign for the stricter censorship of pictures and the threat of boycotting theatres and pictures may possibly have done some good, but the aim for permanence in policy must still be fostered. 

THEATRES AND MORALS 

A recent report in The New York Times stated that the license commissioner had issued an order demanding that burlesque shows in New York eliminate objectionable features from their performances. Among other things, it was charged that the license inspectors' reports "show conclusively that many acts and scenes" in the shows "are immoral and indecent." Further, it was noted that unless "indecent and immoral features are forthwith eliminated and all female members of the cast decently clad," drastic action would be taken. On behalf of the theatres it was reported that there was nothing immoral in the performances and that they were "classical" in nature. The issue thereby became locked between the representative of the public and the operators of the burlesque theatres. 

This is not the first time that a storm has arisen over present-day tendencies in burlesque performances. When a previous license commissioner finally decided to take some action after much pressure from business interests and from others 321

concerned with the preservation of the public morals, considerable interest was focused upon these theatres, both in the daily press and in weekly publications. While some saw little but Rabelaisian portrayals with no decided objection to the performances as a whole or to particular acts, other equally competent critics were outspoken in their strictures. The theatre operators at that time offered to cease offending public taste and partially, at least, purged their performances. 

Cursory observation on a "before and after" basis does not indicate that the cleansing has been sufficiently sweeping. Formerly, there were particularly obnoxious scenes and words dealing with homosexual matters, which somehow met with vociferous approval on the part of the audiences. 

The producers were undoubtedly catering to the depraved tastes of their customers, and continued to do so until ordered to desist. Part and parcel of the burlesque shows were overt and direct references to various abnormal sex practices, which usually brought much applause from the audience of young and middle-aged men, as well as from the few escorted women who attended. 

Simulation of the sex act, reference to homosexuality and heterosexual abnormal practices, vile bedroom scenes, and the like, were only one part of the show. The undoubted drawing power of burlesque shows has been and continues to be the display of young women in markedly abbreviated costumes, or almost completely nude, and the gyrations through which they go and which have a sex 322

significance readily discenible by the audiences. 

After the earlier complaints, some deletion followed in the extremely raw jokes, and a reduction in the references to homosexuality and other abnormalities. Nudity apparently continues with little abatement. The theatre operators led a vigorous attack upon any restrictions being placed on their performances, but some type of compromise was apparently affected. What the next steps will be is not predictable. Unless public spirited citizens and groups come to the fore and insist upon some limitations on indecency in theatrical performances, it is presumed that things will continue as they are at present.

The most notorious phase of the burlesque performances is the so-called "stripper" act. This consists of the slow disrobing of an actress, if such these women can be called, while she struts up and down the stage. The presumable purpose is gradually to arouse the sex feelings of the male audience, until the woman is almost completely in the nude in their full view. In addition, these "strippers" go through various suggestive contortions, aiming apparently still further to arouse the emotions of the onlookers. To judge by the shouts and noisy applause, as well as by repeated curtain calls, these "stripper" acts are the most popular. 

In theatres where these acts have been observed, the women finally appear completely nude to all intents and purposes as they wear only a 3″ x 5″ "triangular" covering and nothing else, if their shoes be excepted. As further part of these per- 323

formances, the chorus or a group of "strippers" may appear in such garb at various stages of the socalled show.

To add to the salaciousness, if all that has already been described is insufficient, photographs and sketches of quasi-nudes are liberally displayed in the foyers and on bulletins advertising the shows. 

There has been some limitation on these posters within recent months. Still further, during the intermission, inducements are offered to have the audience purchase sex magazines, vulgar cartoons, and the like, often with the accompanying and "come on" statement that it is prohibited to sell these articles, so they are given away free to all purchasers of a ten cent bar of chocolate or a bag of nuts.

In viewing this matter of burlesque performances, it should be remembered that many cities throughout the country tolerate such shows, and that the degree of vulgarity and nudity displayed depends largely upon the attitude of the public authorities. It might well be asked at this point whether such exhibitions, usually at admission fees of twenty-five cents and upwards, have any relation to the major question which has been raised, and that is, the protection of the morals and health of young girls, and of boys as well. It has already been noted that many of the boys and men who assaulted the girls under consideration were of the so-called loafer type. Whether or not they received the stimulus and urge to seek a sex outlet by view- 324

ing such burlesque shows as have been described, is of course speculative.

The considerable number of young women who are employed as chorus girls and "strippers" in these shows throughout the country, raises very serious questions as to the nature of the influences to which they are exposed. Recruiting must go on as others drop out, and it is fair to assume that girls of finer sensibilities would hardly think of joining such groups. What happens to the attitudes of girls who enter this field may well be imagined. As to the ultimate effect upon the morals of girls and boys in a community harboring obscene and salacious performances, it may well be said that, while the end results of the exposure to the full force of such influences cannot be measured, yet experience warrants the belief that a debasement of youth undoubtedly occurs. When a burlesque theatre is located within 300 feet of a girls' high school accommodating upwards of 5000 students; when others are on main thoroughfares where girls and boys in large numbers are sure to pass them daily-it is fair to presume that these unwholesome influences in a community should be curbed. Public authorities who already have a full measure of power should be prodded into action by the complaints of parents, teachers and public spirited citizens, if at least one phase of vulgarity and obscenity is to be checked. 

Over a period of years there was a tendency among some theatrical producers to offer performances which were but a step removed in vulgarity, 325

salaciousness and nudity from the current burlesque offerings. Whether there has been a change in taste among those who attended the higher priced and more artistically staged sex shows, or whether those in the theatrical business have found that in the long run such performances did not pay, there has been a definite decline in the number of musical comedies in which indecent jokes and extremely abbreviated costumes are the outstanding features. It would be gratifying to believe that public taste looked askance at these offerings and that their limitation has been the result of good business judgment. Be that as it may, one or more "nude and beauty" shows reach the theatre every season, and the burlesque substitutes continue on their merry course. In addition, the so-called floor shows of large restaurants have filled the gap with displays of nudity rarely attempted by the legitimate theatre.

SEX MAGAZINES 

Observation and perusal of the wares offered for sale by newsstands in the busy sections of cities may present some unusual discoveries. Outstanding among the newspapers in different languages and periodicals with covers in many colors and designs, are the so-called sex magazines. Their sex-alluring names are of particular interest, for the attempt is definitely made to attract the passerby with the promise of more startling information in the hidden pages. The purchaser who seeks pictures of nude young women in many enticing positions; of drawings and cartoons patently vulgar 326

and with double meanings; of jokes and stories of the utmost salaciousness-such a purchaser is not deceived, as he often obtains more than he might bargain for.

To begin with, the names of these magazines are as suggestive as the mind of man can conjure up. 

Following are the titles of some periodicals offered for sale at prices ranging from ten to twenty-five cents and found displayed on one newsstand: 

Gay Paree; Paris Follies; Follies; Wild Cherries;

Pep; Classic Art; Spicy Stories; Scandals; French Night Life; Nudist Beauty; Honeymoon Stories; 

Paris Gaieties; New York Night Life; Broadway 

Night Life; Cupids Capers; Hooey; Snappy; 

Streets of Paris; Tatler; and Hollywood Nights.

These are by no means all that have been freely offered for sale, but rather those whose titles were copied on cursory inspection. Many more in 

English, French and other languages are offered for sale and, judging from the cover illustrations, are undoubtedly in the same category with those whose titles, pictures and contents have been noted. Suggestiveness in names and cover designs are only minor parts. The stories contained in the magazines, plus the pictures, cartoons, drawings and advertisements, constitute the most objectionable aspects of these so-called sex magazines. Credit should be accorded to the present municipal administration in New York City which has, for the time at least, compelled newsstand keepers to remove these magazines from their stands a measure which should be followed 327

in every community. Stores have sprung up in which these salacious periodicals are sold, but thus far no way has been found to deal with these and many other retail stores which continue to display and sell so-called sex magazines. 

The stories in these magazines are most uniformly woven about the sex escapades of lovely young girls, and rape and sexual assaults perpetrated upon "enchanting, alluring young beautiful girls blessed with golden hair that looks like rays of sunshine," by rich and handsome men, with athletic build, broad shoulders, and dressed in the height of fashion. Perhaps such stories, if properly written, might be assumed to have the effect of warning young girls to keep away from the enticements of such men as are described. The apparent purpose is to stimulate the sex interests of the girls and their boy friends. The stories most uniformly deal with the glorification of illicit love affairs, with the rich men won for husbands by girls who permitted themselves to be raped or seduced, and the happy lives they lived ever after. Some of the magazines seem to specialize in tales of young and unmarried girls, while others tend to deal with illicit affairs of married women. The detailing of some of the plots and excerpts of stories may throw further light upon the seductiveness of these magazines, and may provide a measure of their probable influence upon the morals and attitudes of growing girls and boys throughout the country. 

In one tale describing the experiences of a recently married young woman, the story reads: 328

"In the absence of her husband, the newly married bride's one time lover called to pay his respects. 

While he rested his eyes on the beauty evident under the inadequate kimona, he lifted her on his lap and his burning gaze became riveted on her fully exposed charms. Little by little she ceased to struggle as his eyes and tongue thrilled her throbbing breast." The plot goes on to describe the usual climax, and to tell what happened while the bride's husband was at that time visiting the wife of her lover. "They decided to give the bedroom a final inspection and when she removed her coat, she displayed the round fullness of her superb breasts. With one hand he succeeded in extracting one of the cherry-tipped white mounds. The other hand wandered over the soft flesh which her tight skirt revealed. Against her weak resistance he removed her skirt and blouse and in his surging delight he discovered her sans brassiere and sans panties. In an instant his clothes were removed... 

Another story of infidelity reads in part: "She threw off her cheap silk and lace gown and stood before the mirror admiring the slim sensuous lines of her white body." (The illustration accompanying the story depicts a young woman in the nude). 

Her husband told her "if you don't like what I give you, nobody's stopping you from going where you can get better, see?" Periodically she threatened to leave her husband but it was not till one evening when they got "lit up" at a friend's bootlegging establishment that she decided to leave her husband to live with the bootlegger. "Sure, you might as 329

well come. 

We'll have a swell time. You love me some, don't you, baby? I love you, baby; you set me afire." After a few months she became ill (venereal disease) and told him: "What kind of a life is it for me? Lousy bums coming day and night for their booze, and you necking with another woman, and who knows how many more you live with." "Well, if you don't like it, you can get out. I ain't going to have no skirt dictating to me.' The story further relates that the woman finally returned to her husband who accepted her despite the illicit affair. 

"J. A. needed only one look at the girl in his office to make him forget his wife. The girl wanted to find out who was a correspondent in her divorce proceedings. Her legs were so crossed that areas of pink-white could be seen above her stocking tops. She dropped into a sea of cushions, crossed her alluring legs and gently tugged at her dress, thus raising her skirt to reveal more of the rounded whiteness of her thigh. While J. A. was trembling in the throes of anticipation, she raised her silk dress higher and flecked aside the lace edge of her step-in and his eyes fell upon ... 

A. came through the door like a breath of wisteria and honeysuckle wrapped in a strictly modern bouquet. His arms closed passionately about her, pressing her slender vibrant body to his in an ardent embrace. "I can't wait. I want you so much."

She sat down and he could not keep his eyes off her as she crossed her legs. She seemed so capable of passion, yet she was always 330

a little frigid on the surface. They left in a little while and were in their bridal suite four hours later. "You embarrass me," she said. He turned and saw she had removed almost all of her clothes. 

Her sole garment was one brief piece of lingerie. 

As he watched, she slipped down the shoulder straps of this garment to reveal her breasts as she stood stripped to the waist before him. In one bound he was at her side . 

Perhaps these illustrations sufficiently portray the type and tenor of the stories that fill the socalled sex magazines. Some are out and out lewd, others suggestive, with many on the borderline between indecency and frank vulgarity. It does not require much imagination to visualize the possible effects of such tales on the minds of girls who may be feeling the first throbbings of sex, who may be yearning for affection and friendship, and physical and emotional outlets the full consequences of which they do not fully understand. 

The same, of course, applies to young men into whose hands these magazines find their way. They hardly need any sex stimulation, for their awakening sex powers are apt to cause them sufficient psychic and physical disturbance to render any further excitement not only unnecessary, but definitely harmful. It should be noted that all the stories are accompanied by pictures and drawings appropriate to the tales themselves, with particular emphasis on depicting the girls in the nude, with men and women in presumably passionate scenes, 331

and with pretty young women in various indecent or unconventional poses. Still further, many of the magazines carry reproductions of photographs of young women either completely in the nude or with some flimsy covering which does not cover. 

ADVERTISEMENTS IN SEX Magazines 

In addition to many advertisements which offer for sale contraceptives to be used by men and women, these sex magazines carry notices of matrimonial agencies, various quack remedies, and, above all, descriptions of so-called "French Post Cards," photos of nude women in various "alluring" poses, of men and women in similar positions and likewise unclothed, and of various kinds of lewd and salacious stories. Little more need be done to bring a realization of the kind of material young people are being exposed to, than to quote some of these advertisements taken from several of the magazines. The following notices perhaps tell the story quite completely:
