The widespread distribution of many sex magazines, the ease with which they can be obtained at a low price, and the deliberate display of nudity that accompanies the newsstand offerings, are definitely shocking to those of finer sensibilities and tastes. Most of these magazines are written in words of one and two syllables and usually with content that appeals to the baser feelings of the readers. It is probably true that young people who read these periodicals, are apt to develop an “I don't care" attitude towards all matters relating to sex, and become more ready to experiment with sex and love than might otherwise be the case. Censorship has generally been abhorred by the American people, and official attempts at control of reading matter have often met with a determined battle on the part of liberal forces. But the question again arises, as it necessarily must, when is liberty mistaken for license, and vice versa. Unless some public means be taken to control the sale and distribution of such sex "stuff," defenseless young girls will continue to be at the mercy of purveyors of filth and assaults by males whose passions have been inflamed by this salacious dribble. 334

DANCE HALLS 

When the average citizen takes an evening stroll along the main business and entertainment thoroughfares of his city, his attention may be caught by particularly bright signs, loud noises, and the like. Within the past few years there has been a new development, or rather, a change in the offerings to attract lonely men who may desire to spend a few hours of their spare time dancing with presumably pretty girls. Reference is made to what has come to be known as the taxi-dance hall. The noisy music issuing forth from saxophones, clarinets and drums, the bright lights illuminating signs and pictures of girls in various stages of undress, the street barkers, and the occasional female dancer advertising the offerings in a second floor or basement dance hall-all play a part in attracting customers to this present modification of the public dance palace. Among the displays announcing such places are included the following: "Taxi 

Dance Hall-Show Beauties as Dance Partners. 

5c. a Dance." "250 Show Girls as Partners" (with photographs of many girls in abbreviated attire or largely nude.) "100 Beautiful and Charming Girls to Dance with. 5c. a Dance." "50 Beautiful Girls as Dance Partners. Admission 25c." "World's 

Most Beautiful Girls as Dance Partners. 5c. a 

Dance" (with photographs of young women who had apparently been beauty contest prize winners.)

The reader will readily recall that in several of the cited cases, the girls served as hostesses or 335

dancers in such taxi-dance halls, and had come in contact in these establishments with the men who were the cause of their being raped and introduced into a life of prostitution. It is doubtful whether these dance places are an improvement over the older type, which in their time were severely criticized and against which it often became necessary to take police measures. The dance hall of five and more years ago, where girls came unescorted or with a boy friend, the open sale of beer, the use of bouncers to control the more exhuberant and disorderly customers and those who became intoxicated, were nothing of which a community could be proud. Further, such places had come to be known as rallying ground for prostitutes, white slavers, and other dissolute characters of both sexes. 

Is the taxi-dance hall an improvement over former conditions, or is it merely a further aggravation of a previously existing condition over which inadequate control had been evercised in many communities?

A thoroughly competent investigator in this field is of the opinion that the present-day taxidance hall, whose tentacles reach over a large part of the country, is the outstanding factor in recruiting girls as professional prostitutes. Many of the girls seeking positions as dancers and hostesses have no home ties or are runaways and wander from place to place, leaving a dance hall in one city, only to be found a week or a month later in another dance hall in a city far from their homes. A fairly exhaustive study has been made of taxi-dance halls 336

in Chicago, where the conclusion was reached, among other things, that such establishments reveal the passing of traditional forms of prostitution, and the development of new substitutes. 

It readily becomes apparent that the problems of the girls under consideration are closely allied with the prevention and control of prostitution, and the development of more wholesome spare time activities for the foot-loose males in every large community. Also, there is needed better supervision and control of the commercial activities of young girls who are engaged in occupations which in themselves are open to suspicion. The young girl of 15 or 16 years, dissatisfied with home or school life, without a job or with no proper clothing, learns from other girls, or by noting the signs "Girls 

Wanted: Easy Work: Good Pay" at dance halls, that there are presumably simple and glamorous ways of making money. She applies for a job as hostess and, if accepted, is quickly initiated by the other hostesses into the routine of the task she has undertaken. What she does not learn from them is taught to her by the customers, who usually demand dancing of a kind that was hardly permitted in the dance hall a few years ago. If these girls refuse to dance "hot," their trade falls off.

They earn little, and must soon look for another job. 

Some of these places employ between 75 and 100 girls, depending upon the season of the year, and only the more rugged can continue for any length of time. 337

The demoralization of the girls proceeds not only in the dance halls, but follows through the requests of the dance partners for sex favors after dancing is over at 2 A. M. or 3 A. M. Many girls become prostitutes to add to their earnings as hostesses, and thereby do not find it necessary to look for customers on the streets. The control of this situation is bound up with several matters, among them being the large question of provision of decent, and at the same time, attractive spare-time recreational facilities for young and middle-aged men, with and without family ties. Until that phase of the community job is properly and adequately dealt with, control of the problem of prostitution will be left without some solution. How to keep girls from becoming hostesses in taxi-dance halls and their variations, how to determine the minimum ages, hours, etc. and bring about effective enforcement of such rules and regulations, are further matters for community concern. One thing is apparent, and that is, unless there be developed an organized and continuous policy of honest law enforcement for the control of taxi-dance halls, many young girls will continue to be inveigled into sex affairs, much to their hurt and demoralization. 

CONCLUSION 

The presentation of so much material that appears lewd and vulgar in nature, may be distressing to those who are unacquainted with the problems that are ever-present in modern communities. 

Doubtless many may feel that either these things 338

cannot be, or they do not and probably will not ever affect the members of their families-their sons and daughters and those dear to them. That is a hope and an assumption which may be altogether warranted. But, how many parents know the possible effects on their young people of motion picture shows which deal largely with sex themes; how many are aware of the salacious literature which their children may read, and the raw and sexfilled stories and jokes which they hear and pass on; of the theatres and dance halls which they frequent-all probably having a definite effect upon growing and impressionable minds. It cannot be assumed that such matters are only the concern of the parents of boys and girls who are most liable to fall into evil ways, if there be predictable methods of determining who they may be. Rather, it should be the moral obligation of all intelligent parents, teachers and community workers, to concern themselves with the depicted and other factors in the corruption of youth, and to help save the children of others, for thereby they will be building up a bulwark for the protection of their own kith and kin. 339

CHAPTER XII  INTRODUCTION 

T has been recorded in the life of Pasteur that IT when he was but a young man and lived at the 

College of Bensacon, the night watchman would come to his modest room at four in the morning and, shaking him, would call out: "Come, M. Pasteur! you must shake off the demon of idleness."

Idleness and inaction on the part of the community in relation to the problems which have been presented and discussed, cannot be placed altogether at the feet of individuals or groups whose avowed purpose it has been to serve needy and helpless girls. For, after all, some at least have been cognizant of the factors involved in the demoralization of youth, and have attempted to call attention to these situations. Their cries, however, have often gone unheard and unanswered. 

The more interested one is in stemming the tide of carnal abuse, the more does the realization press forward that there is no easy way to achievement. Taking the problems as dealt with in preceding chapters, it is found that, in simplest terms, effective measures should include education of parents; certain additions to the course of study and personnel in the public and high schools; prevention, through various procedures, of runaway escapades; rebuilding and reinforcement of broken 340

homes; elimination of evils attendant upon overcrowded family situations; improvement of family and community environments; control of feebleminded, mentally ailing and vicious individuals; and a host of other matters with which organized society has been battling for a long time, and at considerable expense. 

A further indication of public interest in the main problem rests in the widespread efforts at control through the enactment of rather stringent legislation in every state. 

It has long been recognized that the inclusion of desirable laws in statute books does not necessarily imply elimination or even marked reduction of social evils. Too many personal and group factors are involved to insure success through legislative measures alone. Much more must be done, for the adding of a new law, or the amending and strengthening of an existing statute, must be bolstered up by concurrent and continued community vigilance. Preceding the achievement of any degree of success in the elimination of social ills, there must first of all be an informed public opinion as to the questions involved; and thereafter, the formulation of considered plans for the control of the particular problem against which efforts are to be concentrated.

In gathering the histories of the 1400 girls whose stories from the basis of this attempt to arouse parental and public opinion, one objective was to lay bare the facts, and another to offer such suggestions and recommendations as might be deemed applicable to the situations uncovered. Attention 341

could be focused on the part that parents, teachers, community workers and others should play in the control and elimination of the evils dealt with. 

However, there exists such a close human interrelationship that it is not always possible to divorce the responsibilities and opportunities of parents from the obligations of the school system, of the police and the courts, of other social machinery -all interested in protecting the health and morals of young people. Vice versa, these community agencies are frequently stumped in their sincere efforts to be of service by the lack of cooperation on the part of uneducated, disinterested and degraded parents. It has therefore been deemed more desirable to select several factors, and to concentrate upon them as offering some hope of success, rather than to deal in wider generalities. 

Through the filling of the indicated gaps, and a more vigorous attack on the problems as recommended, it is believed that community debts to growing girls will in some measure be discharged. 

CRIME PREVENTION 

A serious situation which parents and the community must contend with, as has been amply indicated, is the carnal abuse of young girls. The extent of the problem cannot be accurately measured, for cases that come to the attention of the public authorities are believed to be only a part of the total number of instances of sexual assault and debasement perpetrated upon girls under the age of consent. Many who are the victims of such 342

mistreatment are too immature to realize what it is all about, while others are afraid to tell their parents because they dread the consequences or the shame involved. Threats and fear, as noted in many of the cases, play an important part in concealing assaults. In some instances, and for various reasons, girls invite such relationships with men and boys of their acquaintance, or whom they pick up in some way.

The question arises whether the community has not been derelict in waiting till a crime is committed with physical and psychic harm an accomplished fact, before attempting to do anything about the matter. Even then, due to the difficulty of obtaining the conviction and incarceration of the perpetrator, he may be set free and, if he will, repeat his nefarious conduct. The preceding chapters fully indicate that careful analysis will provide data as to the types of individuals who are apt to be involved in rape cases; of situations and environmental factors that are conducive to such conduct; of places and even particular addresses to which those bent on doing harm to young girls are wont to take them. Why, may the question be asked, have public authorities and others so often stood aside until a crime was committed, and only then been driven to action? It is certainly incumbent upon those charged with the responsibility of enforcing the law, to prevent violations whenever adequate information is available. While this may be a more recent concept of practical police administration, yet it is the only way in which 343

vigorous efforts at suppression of rape will yield adequate results. 

Crime prevention, particularly as applied to juveniles, has had a place in most communities for some time, more especially among the private agencies interested in work with young people. 

Of recent years police departments have come to realize the intrinsic value of such a program, and in certain cities have organized special divisions to deal with crimes by and against minors, and with methods of prevention. To be more specific, in one city police are assigned to watch cheap. hotels which have been known to cater to the men who take young girls to such places for evil purposes. Effective action along such lines can quickly put these hotels on the suspect list and either compel them to observe the law, or force them out of business. Elsewhere police are assigned to playgrounds, public parks, amusements resorts and the like, to watch for the suspicious conduct of adults in the company of young girls. 

Certain neighborhoods are known to give rise to a high rate of delinquency, and preventive police and social work would certainly yield worthwhile results if properly and vigorously organized. 

It is apparent that a complete program for crime prevention and, concomitantly, prevention of abuse of young girls, cannot be dealt with in this presentation. Careful analysis of already existing services would be required, including an appraisal of the activities of policewomen in this field, the functions of private agencies, the need for amending 344

existing laws and ordinances, etc., before a thoroughgoing and all-inclusive community program could be worked out. It should be noted, however, that such preventive functions as have already been exercised seem to have been singularly effective in some spheres of work. Concentration of police efforts on the more outstanding aspects of the problem as previously depicted, should yield results of a wholly worthwhile nature.

LAW ENFORCEMENT 

The matter of law enforcement is tied up with so many personal and community problems that it is not possible to make offhand statements that given methods should prevail. Different eras delineate their own changing standards. Before the time of prohibition, the saloon raised many problems of law enforcement insofar as prostitution and rape were concerned. By the enactment of the constitutional provision which doomed the continuance of the old-fashioned saloon and Raines Law hotels, the matter of prostitution and its control took on other aspects. With the ending of prohibition, a new era has been entered. Never theless, certain aspects of the enforcement of laws relative to prostitution, commercial amusements, and the like, remain more or less static. Politically free and effective police and judicial action can go a long way in eliminating many of the excrescences which fester upon the social body. 

It is not assumed, of course, that police or judicial officers in any community wilfully connive 345

at the violation of young girls. Rather, the thought is that, whether because of public inertia, or refusal to recognize the facts, or inadequate police personnel, both in numbers and in training, certain local conditions are permitted to prevail. These factors in turn make it possible for the vicious and degenerates in a community to foist their evil intentions and acts upon the unknowing and unwary youngsters who become the ultimate sufferers. 

Cases and examples have already been cited to drive home this thought. Perhaps it may be well to reiterate that a greater measure of police control over the licensing of taxicab drivers and dance halls; more effective inspection of cheap motion picture theatres; some bar against the sale of sex magazines; a type of control of burlesque and other theatres which would meet with the approval of the better elements in particular communities, and of the theatrical profession itself; more exacting supervision of the operation of cheap hotels and furnished rooming houses-these and other phases of law enforcement are seemingly in need of strengthening.

Integrally related to the matter of law enforcement is the question of the treatment of criminals in general. During recent years there has again come to the fore insistence that the whole field of the handling of crime and criminals be critically surveyed. Serious questions have been raised as to whether part of the vast, complicated web of technical legal defenses now surrounding the accused criminal cannot be done away with. The 346

protection thrown around criminals of all kinds is often extreme. It is not, of course, intended that the accused shall be denied fundamental justice, but rather that the application of the principles of justice shall be expedited and be more certain. 

This is of particular force in cases of rape, in which legal processes, as at present organized, so frequently lead to the discharge of the accused.

Whether this is due to the determination that there was some measure of incitement, or other legal and factual matters, cannot be stated without further delving into particular cases. Another reason might be added relating to the number of cases in which the accused are discharged. Since the acts of rape take place, for the most part, with no witness present, the court is obliged to decide. the vital issue on the testimony of only one person as plaintiff against the conflicting statement of the one who is accused. The presentation of medical testimony relating to the physical condition of the girl is naturally utilized whenever available. 

Taking cognizance of the age, unreliability and frequently the low mentality of the girls whose testimony must be used, it becomes apparent why prosecution often fails. Strengthening of enforcement by possible revamping of the laws and modification of the administration of them should seem to be a justifiable procedure.

The public attitude towards law enforcement against prostitution has indicated varied shades of opinion over a period of years. This has depended in part upon the honesty or dishonesty of public 347

officials charged with the control of the situation; the occasional brutality of the police; the degree of severity of magistrates; the evils associated with law enforcement and uncovered by special investigators; and other factors which have pulled at the heart strings of the public, or nauseated them with the details of the sordidness of commercial prostitution. It is not possible to say how many of the 1400 girls whose cases have been considered, have already become professional or clandestine prostitutes, or will join that unending army in the years to come. If definite bulwarks are to be set up against their entry into the field of the "sale of human flesh," as one motion picture advertisement called it, then the matter of proper law enforcement is of the utmost importance. 

Aside from the activities of determined and sincere prosecutors, of whom there are undoubtedly many throughout the country, it is fair to assume that a community has the type and degree of law enforcement which it insists upon. If municipal officials come to feel that the electors are not overanxious about enforcement of laws against vice and crime, there may be a natural letdown. On the other hand, if parents and their organizations, teachers and community leaders in various fields of human endeavor, make it plain where they stand on law enforcement, worthwhile results can usually be achieved. For the protection of their own children and the children of others, if for no other reason, these groups should insist upon enforcement of laws aimed at the control of vice and crime, 348

but enforcement tempered with justice and not with harshness, with sympathy and not with misunderstanding.

FAMILY WELFARE 

Dependents, defectives and delinquents were long ago characterized as representing major community problems. The three groups of social liabilities are included among the girls and their families to whom some thought has already been given in preceding pages, and whose problems ever press for adjustment and solution. In considering the community situations created by the voluntary or forced rape of young girls, one phase that requires judgment is whether or not the economic status of their parents has anything to do with the involvements of the daughters. Without question, unemployment, sickness, death of the chief wageearner, desertion, divorce or separation of parents, crowded family life, inebriety of the father or mother, poor environment, and other well recognized factors-contributed their definite share to the totality of sexual violation which occurred. 

Discussion is often aroused as to whether or not economic conditions have any definite influence upon the incidence of prostitution. Answers have been made to uphold contentions on either side, or equivocally to support views which closer analysis may set aside. Careful study of the preceding histories, as well as of all the 1400 cases, brings conviction to the conclusion that economic conditions do influence prostitution, and in the ways 349

which have been indicated. In other words, it is believed, as a result of this particular investigation, that the several family situations which have been noted, prepared the way for the assault of young girls with attendant initiation into early sex practices. Further, the lack of proper family life and surroundings, degenerate parents and neighbors, all may conspire, perhaps unwittingly, to bring about the future downfall of young girls and so ease their way into a life of prostitution. It is, of course, true that a variation of this conclusion is applicable in the case of some girls who are mentally or emotionally unstable, and who might have fallen into sex difficulties irrespective of the economic status of their families. 

It has often been pointed out that children who misbehave or become involved in antisocial acts may be found in homes of every economic and social grade. Even though children in homes of the wealthy may occasionally be guilty of serious misconduct, yet it is the children of the poor, of broken and overcrowded homes, who have the heaviest burdens to carry in their social adjustments. When one notes the wide background of deprivation in the lives of the girls under consideration, the longing for a little spending money, the natural desire for the simple delights of some new clothes or occasional attendance at a motion picture show, it is not difficult to understand how the bare fact of being poor is many times a sufficient explanation for voluntary or forced sex escapades or involvements. 350

Suggested solutions for our various social ills are legion. The application of proposed panaceas to the elimination of poverty, sickness, prostitution and the like, would involve the working out of plans of such wide local and national magnitude, that the trend of thought inherent in the present discussion would be completely lost sight of. In dealing with family situations, it has long been apparent that families must be dealt with as units, and that the attempt to solve the problem of a delinquent or emotionally unstable daughter, for instance, requires much more than mere concentration upon the erring or ailing girl. Rather, her rehabilitation may be tied up with decent housing, steady employment of the father at an adequate wage, trade training, removal of sick relatives from the family circle, proper recreational outlets, reorganization of the school curriculum, and other matters which it is needless to emphasize at this point. It remains to be said that, unless many of these social ills and community failings be given early and comprehensive attention, it will continue to be a difficult matter to prevent the repetition of the sad and devastating experiences to which so many girls are being exposed.

PARENTAL EDUCATION 

Training in the preparation for marriage and parenthood is urgently needed in all communities. 

It will be readily conceded that it would be desirable to have such instruction provided by schools and colleges, in order to prepare for courtship, 351

marriage and parenthood and to further the satisfactions of intelligent participation in family life. If this task were adequately carried out for a period of years, members of the coming generation would be the eventual beneficiaries. Noting that the development of preparental education has been rather slow, attention must in this day be especially focussed upon parental education. It is perhaps an accepted axiom that every community should insure opportunities for parental education which will provide instruction in the principles of home making, family relations, and the rearing and education of children.

Realization of the need for parental education is but the first step. It may be well to formulate the principles for the instruction of a homogeneous group of parents, all understanding the English language, so as to be able to participate in a discussion; all sufficiently literate to be able to read pamphlets and books on child care and similar topics; and with adequate income, leisure, home surroundings and the like, to enable the mothers particularly to benefit by whatever parental education program may be set up. It is, of course, of importance that parents who belong in these categories be given the stimulus and benefit of such instruction. Serious problems arise, however, when the attempt is made to apply the plan on behalf of those who, as it were, "do not belong."

As an example of situations which develop in this field of endeavor, it may be of interest to note that one of the authors was recently asked to give a 352

series of talks to parents who were members of parents' associations of public school children. 

Having had previous experiences with such groups, there was no dismay to the lecturer when the discussion soon disclosed that many of these parents were undoubtedly illiterate; they could not understand English very well, if at all; no effective approach on parental education had been made to them previously; and, on the whole, little could be accomplished on a one or two lecture basis. 

In a study made of the home life of a group of girls who became involved in sex difficulties, it was found that many of the parents of these girls, though living in this country for a period of years, still clung, and perhaps quite naturally, to the foreign ways and customs. They were inclined to be overstrict, and to limit the liberties of their children. Athletics were never encouraged, and as far as the home life was concerned, the child was often brought up in an atmosphere foreign to this country. As a result conflicts were inevitable between parents and children. The latter lived an 

American life in the schools and outside of their homes, and came into frequent friction with the older and foreign-born and foreign-minded generation.

The purpose in mentioning these matters is in no way to decry the halting influences of parents of foreign birth, for often such may have much to commend them. Rather, the illustrations are indicative of the fact that a wide plan of parental education is needed, if the children and young people 353

in this country are to be adequately served in their formative years. Recognition must be taken of the fact that a one-type plan of parental education is unsuited to present-day conditions, more particularly in our larger cities. If plans for the education of parents are to be developed, they should take into consideration the several categories of parents to be encountered, their probable prejudices and superstitions regarding child care, and the bringing up and training of adolescent boys and girls; their own family, economic and health problems; the possible need for elementary education in 

English as the beginning to a wider approach; and kindred matters which are well known to informed community workers and teachers. 

Certain aspects of parental education programs need to be emphasized, whether they be intended for the average American woman of public or high school education, or for those who have attained a higher academic status; or on the other hand for those of foreign birth and inadequate English and other attainments. These special matters lie in the realm of mental and physical health. An integral part of parental education programs should include instruction relative to the importance of noting early manifestations of mental and nervous disorders in children, and the taking of necessary steps to put such children under competent medical care. Many social and personal problems arise relating to children and adolescents because their parents are unaware of the disabilities of their own children or, if so informed, they take no 354

effective measures for their treatment or correction. 

Calamities in the lives of girls of defective mental and emotional status could often be obviated, if their parents were only better informed. Problems of rape, venereal disease, unmarried motherhood, and the like, might be avoided through proper protection of girls who are mentally and emotionally defective, and through adequate control exercised over boys and men who belong to the same categories. A sense of helplessness overcomes many parents of such children. Perhaps their despair is understandable, but society should at least take definite steps to inform parents of their inadequacies, how these can be overcome, and the available community facilities which can be called to their aid.

SEX EDUCATION 

Adolescence has been defined as an "indefinite period of transition from the simple organization of childhood to the complex mechanism of maturity." Noting again that the majority of the girls under consideration belonged to the age group which is commonly recognized as being part of the adolescent period of youth, it becomes of singular importance to inquire into some of the phases of their development. In this way, light may be brought to bear upon their problems, as well as on inadequacies in communal provisions along educational lines. Unless parents, teachers and others with definite responsibilities to youth, take cognizance of their failings in these matters, 355

they may rightly be charged with derelictions of duty to growing children. While a major cause of difficulty for the 1400 girls revolved about their sex experiences, it has been amply indicated that the belief is not held that the transient or continued sex affairs were the only matters of concern. On the other hand, efforts have been made to note that, time and again, young and adolescent girls were so uninformed about sex matters, that their problems arose, in part at least, from their state of ignorance. 

It may be of particular significance that these girls lived and were being educated in a community which has consistently and officially frowned upon attempts to organize instruction along lines generally denoted as sex education. The term sex education or instruction may not be the best choice to cover the imparting of information which includes data relative to human anatomy; sex differences between boys and girls, men and women; the parts played by fathers and mothers in reproduction; fertilization; inheritance; hygiene of pubescence; wholesome conduct in boy-girl relationships; and a number of other matters with which the growing child is concerned. At various times it has been appreciated that other terminology might enable educators to get by hurdles which the word "sex," either alone or in combination with others, seems to create in the minds of some. 

This may be due to a variety of reasons, but the situation will have to be faced until there is a wider acceptance of the program for which the term "social hygiene" stands. 356

Sex education has been variously regarded as the function of parents, the church, and the school. 

There is no denying that this type of education is best imparted by the mother to the daughter, the father to the son, at such times and in such ways as the intelligence of the growing children can comprehend. But is it not presuming too much in the present state of affairs, to expect all or even most parents to be equally competent to discuss questions about which they have no adequate information, no decided points of view, and frequent inhibitions whose origin goes back to their own childhood? Still further, it becomes necessary, as previously pointed out, to take cognizance of the general state of adult education in this country before attempting to burden many parents with an educational function which is perhaps rightly theirs, but which they cannot be expected to discharge at the present time. For instance, if we are, systematically and consciously, to direct the education of the child's impulses, it is first of all necessary to know and understand the child's impulse life. Yet how many parents are there who are so prepared in the field of psychology that they can undertake such education? Seemingly the purpose must be to continue all efforts at preparental and parental education of those competent to comprehend fundamentals of the child's inner life and emotions. In addition, there should be set up ways and means of imparting the simpler aspects of the problem, in order that the so-called average mother and father, may participate in the instruc- 357

tion and be enabled to handle the usual questions that their boys and girls direct to them. .

There are some who hold that no sex instruction can be wholesome or adequate unless it is imparted by parents to children in infancy and childhood; also, that long before the child begins to ask questions, even far earlier than school entrance, he should know the father's part in reproduction, as well as the mother's, and should be told that sexual intercourse is an act of affection. There can be little, if any, quarrel with the thesis that parental responsibility stands first, and that the child should learn from his parents of his origin, the part his mother and father played in his conception and growth. Perhaps the nub of the whole situation lies in the willingness of parents to assume this responsibility, and their own feeling of ability to deal with this educational aspect in the lives of their children. Given the parents whose education and background have been informative and ennobling, and who have a marked sense of parental duty, there can be no question of their choice for the task of instructing their own children in matters relating to sex. But, the number of men and women to whom life has been unduly kind, who have had the benefits of education in its broadest sense, are altogether too few in number to expect a community-wide parental rising to the needs of childhood. The requirements of children of such parents will, in the ordinary course of events, be properly provided for, while many will 358

continue to go unserved unless reliance is placed elsewhere than upon parents alone. 

THE FUNCTION OF THE CHURCH 

The function of the church in sex and character education of the young has long been accepted, and rightly so, to be an important one. 

The question that naturally arises, however, is whether the modern church, or the church in modern times, is prepared to accept the charge inherent in such recognition. It may be of moment to note, without disparagement of course, that many of the girls whose tales of woe have been considered, went to church regularly and, despite such attendance, came into evil ways. Far be it from the purpose of these pages to throw the responsibility for the involvement of these girls upon the church collectively, or upon the churches of particular denominations. Too much material has been presented to indicate that many of the causes leading directly or indirectly to the situations in which these girls came to find themselves, had no overt relationship to the fields of endeavor in which the church ordinarily functions. It may, of course, be held that every part of human activity is a concern of the church, and that the fundamentals of religion recognize no limitations. This is undoubtedly a correct view, though its application requires careful thought.

Can the church in modern urban society assume and carry out the plan of teaching the young what they avowedly should know about sex matters? 359

Are the leaders of churches throughout the land philosophically and scientifically prepared to carry out a program of sex education? It appears that these are basic questions which many have, either by inaction or otherwise, answered in the negative.

They have thereby thrown the responsibility back upon the parents and on the community as a whole. 

It remains to note that the leaders in one great church have consistently opposed the teaching of sex material in the schools, and their point of view can be readily sympathized with. If their thought be read aright, the fear has been that poorly and inadequately prepared teachers can perhaps do more harm than good; further, that the imparting of sex information is not a matter for group instruction, but rather one for personal and intimate discussion. In large measure, these objections are shared by all thoughtful persons who have been concerned with the entire question as a challenging one in the field of education. Allowance should be made, however, for the growing concept of sex education, for the veering away from purely sex factors to broader fields of human interest and concern.

Undoubtedly, the church has a fine opportunity of possible service in the field of social hygiene and an important phase thereof, namely sex education. Whether or not the church will be able to rise to the opportunities under current conditions is a matter the future alone can determine; likewise, whether the most profitable results will come from work among the young, the adolescents, 360

or adults, is also a subject for appraisal after due consideration of experimental efforts along these lines. In the meantime, all such well-organized and properly motivated projects need to be fostered and encouraged, in order that the sum total of human effort in a worthwhile cause may be augmented in the battle against ignorance.

THE RESPONSIBILITY OF THE SCHOOLS 

In the last analysis, responsibility for sex guidance and sex instruction, devolves upon the public schools of the country, including the high schools in this category of free educational institutions. 

All normal children are compelled to attend a public school or its equivalent for a number of years; the instruction is generally standardized; physical facilities are usually good, with improvements from year to year; and, barring certain rural and impoverished areas of the country, teachers are trained and supervision may be presumed to be adequate. The great American institutionthe public school-continues to be looked to by parents and the public as a whole as an enormous hopper, into which children are placed, to come out, after a period of years, a more informed, a better equipped group of young people, prepared to face life in its multifarious phases. It may be that too much is being demanded of our schools, while parents are shedding responsibilities which were formerly theirs alone. The more recent movement for the extension of adult and parental education may be looked to, in part at least, to 361

check any such mass movement, and to maintain in the minds of the elders their own particular functions in the body politic as the foundation stones upon which their children must rest and upon which to build their own futures. Whichever way transient impulses may cross the country, the public school will continue to be looked upon as the fundamental agency in the education of children in all matters that pertain to their mental and physical health. Based on this assumption, it becomes necessary to look to the schools as the ultimate servants in the field of sex education, and to take cognizance of the service schools can render in the cause of health and morals among the growing youth of the land.

It would take one too far afield to attempt a complete presentation of the methods and content of sex education which have come to be recognized as being valid and as having definite value. The writings of Bigelow, Galloway, Snow and others, have amply covered the subject matter. It remains but to touch the highlights, as it were, rather than to attempt a detailed outline of courses of instruction, or a critique of existing material and methods of presentation. The standard books by the writers already mentioned and others whose works are listed in the references, supply adequate material for individual parents, teachers and community workers who desire to follow this phase of the matter still further. Other cogent publications are available through various sources. 362

The primary consideration in sex education is naturally the child. If proper preparation for life is to be provided, then the teacher must be so taught and motivated that the child will reap the full benefits of the available information that can be imparted to him. Professor Galloway has pointed out that it is apparent that our children, sexed as they themselves are from birth and environed constantly by social situations that are satiated with the influences of sex, do not have anything like an even chance to make the most human use of their sex nature, unless given suitable and consistent help by all who are interested in them. 

To use sex "humanly" means to allow a full human development to the most effectual aspects of the impulses and functions of sex and reproduction, while controlling uses and abuses which are personally or socially disintegrating; to blend the sex impulses with all the other character elements in such ways that one may attain his own richest, most harmonious personality and permanent happiness; and, by way of these developments, to become fitted to be an efficient husband or wife, father or mother, rather than merely to indulge and misuse selfishly a fundamental impulse. To give this sort of instruction to the young is, according to 

Professor Galloway, sex education.

Full realization of the true import of this definition may perhaps resolve the doubts in the minds of some as to whether or not sex education is worthwhile. Further, it makes evident the need of preparation to impart these matters, either to 363

one's own children or, as a teacher or community worker, to the children of others. Perhaps it also sets at rest the fear that sex education is concerned with the grosser aspects of the problem. Dr. 

William F. Snow stated the matter forcefully and effectively when he wrote that: "There should be no 'sex' courses." This is a definite answer from the director of the American Social Hygiene Association as to the type of emphasis that should be placed on this phase of the educational program. 

Fundamental to the carrying out of a plan of sex education, is the acceptance by school authorities of the responsibility for the instruction of children under their care in the fundamentals of the subject.

Concomitant with that, there must of necessity go the training of teachers in all that pertains to sex education or, better still, sex-character education. Without such official approval and preparation of teachers, little advance can be made. 

The substance matter in which teachers need to be grounded has been carefully analyzed and experimentally worked out by several competent leaders in this field. Among them has been Professor T. W. 

Galloway. He pointed out, among other things, that the specific needs of the pupils of various ages, development and sophistication-from the kindergarten onward-fix the minimum of preparation which their teachers should receive. 

According to Galloway, the elements which should be included in this preparation are: 

1. Certain fundamental data and training which are essential to give the teacher background, confid- 364

ence, poise and tact. These needs would perhaps be best satisfied by a general course in biology which would adequately include the human aspects of the subject. It would put most stress on essential life relations, behavior and adjustments. 

2. Simple and specific facts about sex and reproduction in animals and man, as the nature of reproduction, the nature of sex, the relation between the two functions; sex-reproductive organs, gametes, fertilization; individual development and influence of sex on this development; courtship, mating, marriage, motherhood, fatherhood, family life, care of offspring, etc. Likewise, the bearing of all this on social organization in various species. 

3. Some knowledge of the actual differences which social mores, customs, traditions and conventions produce in the education of boys as compared with that desirable for girls. 

4. Recognition of the stages of development in boys and girls, insofar as these are determined by sex, including the pubertal, postpubertal and preparental periods, and the special sex problems that arise. 

5. An intimate understanding of the opportunities possessed by schools for giving the help needed by young people, including the curriculum, extra curricula activities, and parent-teacher associations. gen- In addition, there might be included nature study, general biology or biological aspects of eral science, physiology and hygiene, physical education, domestic science, educational psychology 365

and social studies. It is, of course, recognized that some, if not all, of these courses are already included in the curricula of schools in which teachers obtain their preliminary and professional education, but it may be well to bear in mind that they are fundamental to a rounded out preparation of teachers for the important task that lies before them.

From the preceding it will be noted that the recommendation is made that sex education should be emphasized as being a part of the general curriculum, and that it should be presented through other subjects or courses. Since it is but a phase of health and character education, such instruction should be developed as integral parts of the general educational program. The following out of this plan may help further to allay the suspicions and doubts of those questioning the worthwhileness of including sex-character material in the school curriculum.

There remains the question as to the desirability of mass or group teaching, as opposed to what often becomes an impossibility in large centers of population, namely individual instruction. If the teachers are carefully selected for their sympathy, understanding, normal approach to the problems of sex, and their broad scientific background, coupled with a knowledge of family and community problems as previously depicted, then there should be little question as to their ability to handle this most difficult of subjects. But such teachers are rare, and the assumption must be that attempts will be 366

made to find the most promising, and set them the task of further preparation for the development of the course.

It would undoubtedly be of great service if efforts were made to enable select teachers to prepare themselves as specialists in the field of sexcharacter education. Not until the higher authorities in school systems give evidence of their wholehearted sympathy for such instruction, and grant their open support and approval, will teachers in any number seek to further their own ability in this specialty, for such it is. There quickly comes to mind the question as to how many of the young girls whose early sex experiences have been depicted, might have been spared their deplorable affairs if well-prepared teachers had imparted the suggested type of information to them; if thereby their own habits of thought might have been stimulated and raised to a higher plane; if school leadership in this field might have permeated the community consciousness to an extent sufficient to have led to a clearing up of many of the local situations which contributed to the sex involvements of the girls, and of the boys and young men as well. These questions cannot be readily answered, but it is fair to assume that a community in which children had been properly guided and instructed by inspiring teachers, would more than have been repaid in the ultimate savings effected in various ways. 

Another matter which relates particularly to the schools is the suggestion that every urban school should have at least one teacher whose respon- 367

sibility it should be to act as advisor and confidant to the pupils. Further, such teachers should be empowered to make home visits and take such other steps as might serve the well-being of the children under supervision. It is, of course, recognized that various communities provide the services of visiting teachers, and that they, in part at least, fulfill the functions which are contemplated. There is needed an extension along the lines indicated, with selected teachers free from duties of classroom instruction, and assigned for particular personal service and advice. Their confidential help would save many a girl and boy from practices and conduct whose baneful influences are not fully appreciated, and so deflect certain emotional expressions in directions which would be of ultimate benefit to the individual child.

A keen observer in the field of sex conduct has wisely noted that no one characteristic of cultural progress is so marked or so important as the repression under which the sexual instinct has fallen in the course of development. Sexuality needs to be recognized if for no other reason than because it is an actuality, and it cannot be dealt with by refusing to accept it. The child also needs to be recognized for the same reason, as possessing a sexual instinct and that instinct needs due consideration in the process of its growth and development. Through such acceptance of sex on the same level of importance as other functions of the individual, some of the community debts to growing children will be discharged. 368

CONCLUSION 

The presentation of a considerable amount of factual data almost precludes the necessity of a lengthy summary or conclusion. If the preceding pages are sufficiently descriptive of situations which exist throughout the country, then parents, teachers and community workers will learn to take into their own hands measures for their correction and elimination. After all, the responsibility for the continuance of conditions which permit the rape and assault of innumerable young girls; which result in the failure to instruct them and their brothers betimes, and in the inertia which has restrained the organized community from taking vigorous and considered steps for the elimination of recognized evils, may be laid to inaction of the individuals and groups to whom these pages have been particularly addressed. It is not, of course, intended to attach any measure of blame or criticism to them, but rather to urge them on to more aggressive action in a field which is of peculiar interest and concern. 

The task is a great and worthwhile one, but courage has never been known to be deflected by size alone. 

Rather, added impetus has been aroused. The solution is left to those who have a great stake in the problem, are most interested in it, and who are, at the same time, most competent to deal with it in an understanding and effective way. 369
